Then each organization they belong to or act for: their title and position, whom they report to and who reports to them, what they may do and within what limits, and the delegations they hold. Their memory never writes an order of the plan down again as a plan of their own.

The narrator's People section gives someone here their titles where an organization you belong to, or know of, ties them, after their party tier: "Marcus Delgado (owner of Alder Automotive, where you work) is here." Its **titles** source (Build → Sections → People) turns that off.

### Story

**Story** has four tabs.

- **Quests**: quests, puzzles, secrets and agendas, sorted into **Active**, **Done** and **Failed**, each row with its stage and its objectives (n of m). Tap one for its quest sheet: where it stands (its stage and cue), its objectives, what you've learned, and its hints. A puzzle stage that takes an answer earns one hint for each wrong answer given at that stage; any other stage has all its hints as soon as the quest reaches it. Hints stay closed until you open them: **Show a hint**, then **Show another hint**, one at a time. Opening one is kept in the chat, with **Undo** on its chip ("Opened a hint: …"), and the narrator is told only the hints you have opened. One you open while a reply is being written shows once the reply finishes; tapping the same **Show a hint** again meanwhile opens that same hint, not the next. Only what you know is shown: what someone you're with is hiding shows as how they behave, in the narrator's Stance section while they're there, never as a quest row. To answer a puzzle, type it ("the code is 1887"); while more than one is open, say which in words from its row or its sheet, or the answer is turned down and doesn't count as a try. A step you take that moves a quest on (a job accepted, a clue read) is told to the narrator in that same reply, and a quest's last stage pays out its rewards as it is reached.
- **What you know**: the facts your character holds, newest first, while the chat tracks who knows what (**Track who knows what** in [Settings → Minds](#settings--minds), or the chat's own switch in **GM → This chat**). Each row is the fact's claim, then how you came by it (**Told by** someone, **You saw it**, **From the start**, **You worked it out** or **A guess**) and since when. A **Suspicion** badge marks one you hold less surely than your suspicion threshold, **Open question** one the story hasn't settled, and **You don't believe it** or **Unsure** what you make of it; **You keep it from …** names whom you keep it from. Only your own holdings show, never what anyone else knows. A secret the story reveals to you shows here, and on its quest, once its change is applied. With tracking off for the chat the tab says so, and with nothing learned yet it says that.
- **Journal**: a running list of what changed, newest first, including the full list behind every Montage, plus notes about changes the engine didn't apply and why. It lists what was applied: a change you undid, or one a later switch dropped, isn't shown as made. A group of changes dropped together (a purchase and its payment) is one note, worded like its Keep report row, with "(2 linked changes)" when the group has more than one change. A turn's entry lists the same lines as its change chip, at most 8; when more exist, money, quest and signal lines are kept and routine ones (activities, other people's moves, needs) go first. Filter by **All**, **Notes & montages** or **Not applied**. **Save to lore…** keeps the latest reply, or an excerpt you select or paste, as a lore entry for the world (see [World lore](#world-lore)).
- **Calendar**: the date and the clock, today's holidays and what is coming up, with **Pause clock** / **Resume clock** and **Timelapse…**. This chat's time controls are in GM mode, and the button at the bottom, **This chat's time controls are in GM → This chat**, takes you there (see [Time controls](#time-controls)).

### The status line and the pill

On a phone, a **status line** sits just above the message box: the time (with the pause icon when the clock is paused, and the time scale as n× when it isn't 1×), your place and your cash, what the engine is doing while a turn runs ("Running the engine…", "Asking 3 minds…", "Ready; reopen the chat to continue", "Already started in another window", "Reading the reply…", with the seconds it has taken), and **Paused** while the chat waits on an edit. When the engine's last turn failed, a red **!** beside the line opens [Diagnostics](#diagnostics). **Act** beside it opens the Actions sheet. Tap the line for the **World** sheet.

On a wider screen, and on a phone where Lumiverse has no room above the message box, a small **pill** floats over the chat instead. You can drag it, and it remembers where you left it (the phone and the desktop remember it separately, and when the window crosses between phone and desktop width, the pill moves to that layout's place straight away; until you move it, it stays centred as its content changes). It shows the time (with the pause icon when the clock is paused, and the time scale as n× when it isn't 1×), your place, the weather and your cash. On screens narrower than 760 px it collapses to the time and a **Show place, weather and cash** button. While a turn runs it says what the engine is doing and for how long (collapsed, a pulse and the seconds); **Failed: …** in red means the last turn's work failed, and opens [Diagnostics](#diagnostics). **Paused** means the chat is waiting on an edit. Tap the pill for its details; its **Fix a value** button opens them too.

The World sheet and the pill's details show the same things: the time and the part of the day, your place, the scene (and the last turn), the weather and your cash, then **This turn** (calls, seconds and tokens: the [call meter](#the-call-meter)), the clock button (**Pause clock** or **Resume clock**), and the **Map** and **Actions** buttons.

#### Fixing a value

Tap the edit button (✎) next to a value to correct it: the time, your place, the weather, your cash or the scene type. You'll find it in the World sheet, in the pill's details and on the scene card in **Now**. A correction like this is a **fix**. It is saved with the reply like anything else, so it survives swipes and branches in the same way, and the status line, the pill and the details show the corrected value at once.

- **Time** sets the date and the time of day, in the world's own calendar (a date picker in a real-world calendar, the calendar's own months otherwise) and as the clock reads where you are, starting from now. A date the calendar doesn't have says which days the month has.
  - **A later time** passes as a time jump the story tells does (see [When the story jumps ahead](#when-the-story-jumps-ahead)): the world lives the time in between, so bills fall due, people follow their schedules and you follow your routine, with nothing stopping it and no Montage. When too much happens before then to run in one go, nothing changes and you're told to use a Timelapse, which gets there in steps. During a fight it changes nothing: a fight's rounds take no clock time.
  - **An earlier time** puts the clock back. Nothing that already happened in between is undone, and none of it happens again when the clock passes that time again: a bill already paid isn't paid twice, nobody's schedule moves them again, and no one's mind goes back over it. Past where the clock had been, the world carries on as before. The clock can't go back before the story starts.

  Its **Corrected** chip says how it moved the clock ("Clock moved on 5 h 15 min", "Clock set back 3 h"), and its **Undo** puts the clock and everything the time in between changed back.

- **Weather** has a fix from Weather level 2, where the sky is simulated; you pick one of the climate's states (Clear, Overcast, Rain and so on) for the region you're in. The weather still follows its climate, so your correction holds only until the climate next changes the sky. During a scripted spell of weather, the corrected sky holds until the spell ends.
- **Scene type** lists the four built-in scene types (conversation, action, exploration and downtime) and those of the places you know. Any other, such as one only a place you don't know about has, is never listed, nor named as the place default's, even when the world gives it a tick of its own. When the scene you're in has such a type, the details and the scene card call it **Conversation**, the engine's default scene, though the clock keeps that type's own tick. Your pick holds until an action, a rule or another fix sets the scene type, even when you move. **Place default** goes back to the scene type of wherever you are.
- **Cash** has a fix from Money level 2. Amounts are typed in the world's own money: "5" is 5 bits in Brindlemere, 5d in Harrowmere and $5.00 in Port Alder, with as many decimals as the currency has. When your cash is held in cash accounts (your wallet), a cash fix doesn't overwrite a number: it moves the difference between your wallet and an outside **Correction** account, so **Play → Me → Money** shows it as a correction and the books still balance. In a new chat whose accounts aren't on your books yet, the fix puts them on first, so the difference is measured from the cash you start with. Undo and swipes treat it like any other fix.

Every ✎ fix is checked before it is saved: a value outside the field's range (energy above 100, cash below zero) or not among its choices is refused with the reason, and nothing changes. A saved fix shows a **Corrected** chip on the latest reply, with **Undo**. What you fix wins over the story. A fix you make while a reply is streaming waits for the reply to finish, then lands over whatever the reply changed: a time you set is the time once the reply is in, however much time it narrates, and a place you set is where your character is. One you make while the engine is still working on the turn, before its reply starts, waits the same way (see [Changes while a turn runs](#changes-while-a-turn-runs)). A reply written before your fix never undoes it, even read again later (a **Re-read edit**, a **Retry read**, a swipe), and that holds when the reply already did what you asked: the fix stays, and is made again if a later read or swipe takes that back. Only a fix that can't be made at all once the reply is in (a time you set after Calendar & time was switched off for your character) gets a chip saying it didn't apply.

### Under each reply

Under each reply the engine ran, a quiet line gives the world's date and time once that turn ended, for the swipe you're looking at, as the clock reads where your character is ("Tue 15 Sep 2026, 12:25 PM"). Times you see read on a 12-hour clock, and temperatures in °F. Under it the engine tells you what it did and asks what it needs to ask. Each thing it says is a **chip**, and a chip shows in one of three ways:

- **The receipt**: one quiet line for what the turn did, at most three parts long (anything that went wrong first, then the time, money, the roll and the changes), then "n more". Tap it for the full list, where every chip shows with its own buttons (**Undo**, **Edit amount** and so on).
- **Decision cards**: a card for each thing that needs your answer, with its buttons. A card marked **Answer before you send** lapses when you send your next message. The Keep report is a card of its own.
- **Offers**: small buttons after the cards for a next move you can take or leave: a Timelapse, the rest of your message, a suggested choice, or **Swipe as…**.

A reply's chips belong to the swipe they came with: they show, and act, only while that swipe is shown, and they go when you delete it, along with what they would have told the next reply.

| Chip | Shows as | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **The change** (for example "Moved to the kitchen", or "3 changes") | Receipt | What the engine applied, worded for you: "Danny Okafor → you: warmth +2, trust +1", "Transfer: $200.00 from checking to savings". With several changes it says how many, and the receipt's list holds them. A change you undid (Undo, or **Not an action?**), or one an earlier switch dropped, isn't listed as made. A **+n min** chip beside it shows the elapsed time. |
| **Roll** | Receipt | For an action with a dice check: the action and its outcome, the dice, the modifier and the total, and the exact odds ("Rolled 1d20 + 2 = 14. Odds: …"). When the world's rules for checks rolled nothing, it says so and why ("No roll: a sure thing (98% to succeed)."), and it shows how your approach was rated when that counted (see [How checks are decided](#how-checks-are-decided)). |
| **Roll Persuasion?** | Card | Your message describes an attempt the chat's rules for inferred checks only suggest rolling (see [Typed text](#typed-text)). Nothing is generated until you answer: **Roll** plays your message with the check, **No roll** plays it as written. It lapses at your next message. |
| **Apply** / **No** | Card | The engine read a change in the story (see [Extraction modes](#extraction-modes)) that would apply, and asks "The narration says this happened. Apply it?" A reply that has you somewhere else without telling the move asks instead whether to move you there, saying where the engine still has you (see **Moving somewhere**). If you ignore it, it expires when you send your next message, and nothing is applied. A purchase or payment the story told always gets one, whatever your money covers: its card says which account it would come from and what that account would hold after it, and has **Pay another way** when nothing would cover it (see [Prices and purchases](#prices-and-purchases)). A change that couldn't apply at all never gets an Apply button: one the engine has no place for (a fact told only in words, a thing introduced as a new character, a payment of no amount, someone or somewhere you don't know) gets a **Not tracked** receipt. A reply that tells your own action again gets no second chip: when the turn lent something to someone, the reply handing that thing (or something inside it, like a lent truck's glovebox) to the same person is the loan itself, and when the turn bought something, the reply telling the purchase again (even in slightly different words, like "brass lantern" for "old brass lantern") is that purchase. Keys handed over with a lend do get their own chip, and so does a different thing bought or given in the same reply. |
| **Undo** | Receipt | Reverses an automatic change ("Applied automatically."). Undo covers the latest reply; for anything earlier, use Keep or Discard (see below). An automatic purchase's chip shows what it charged, the catalog item it was priced as and where the money came from ("Bought milk: $3.63 (priced as Milk, whole)", "From Your checking account: $1,667.27 left."); when the narrated price was capped or clamped it says so and has **Edit amount**, which charges what you type instead, in all, sales tax included. When nothing could cover it, it has **Pay another way** too. |
| **Corrected**, **GM edit** | Receipt | A ✎ fix (**Corrected**) or a GM edit (**GM edit**) saved on this reply, worded as the Journal words it ("Corrected · Rosie Alvarez's mood: happy"). A GM edit your character couldn't see says only whom it was about, if your character knows them ("About Sam Reyes"), or that it happened behind the scenes. **Undo** takes it back. |
| **Opened a hint: …** | Receipt | You opened a hint on a quest sheet (see [Story](#story)). From then on the narrator is told it, with the quest's other lines. **Undo** closes it again. |
| **Your correction didn't apply after the reply** (or *A confirmed change*, *Opened detail*, …) | Card | Something you did while a reply was streaming waited for the reply to finish and couldn't be made at all by then: a confirmation or detail opened that no longer fit, or a ✎ fix or GM edit the reply made impossible (a box put into a crate the reply put inside that box, a time set after Calendar & time was switched off). A ✎ fix or GM edit otherwise always lands, over whatever the reply changed. Nothing changed. **Dismiss** clears it; **GM → This chat → Edits** has the exact reason. |
| **Also bought (item)?** | Card | The reply told a purchase like the one you just made, and the engine can't tell whether it's the same one told again or another one (another colour, a price far from yours, "another" in the prose, or something from the same part of the price list, like "coffee" after the latte you typed). **Apply** charges it as a second purchase, at a price of its own; **No** drops it. A reply that only tells your purchase again gets no chip at all, even in other words: "two larges, black" after you bought two large coffees, because it holds your own words for them or is priced as the same catalog item there. |
| **Bought (item): no price** | Card or receipt | The reply told a purchase that nothing prices: the catalog doesn't list it and the reply gave no price. The thing is its buyer's with nothing charged (under Confirm, once you tap **Apply**): "It's yours" when you bought it, and when someone else did, the chip names them ("Danny Okafor has it"). **Set price** charges the amount you type and **Pick item** prices it as a catalog item you choose (when the catalog has any close to it); either keeps the one thing. **Undo** takes it back, and **No** on the card leaves it out. |
| **The engine didn't finish, so nothing was generated** | Card | Something the engine needed before the reply failed, and the card says what and why ("Reading your action: No connection profile found", "Priya Nair's mind: connection reset (after 2 retries)"; someone you haven't met is "a mind"). Nothing was written. **Retry** runs the engine again, asking only what failed while nothing else changed, then writes the reply (for a **Continue**, it continues the same reply); **Diagnostics** opens the details. Fix the cause first when it's a setting (a connection, Max tokens). A new reply you asked for after a reply (**Send** with nothing typed) has no **Retry**: press **Send** with nothing typed again, since a Regenerate there would roll the reply before it again. A reply for an earlier message, from **Edit and Send** in place or a swipe of an older reply, gets this card with no Retry, because Lumiverse can start only the last reply again. Instead, use **Edit and Send** as a branch, or regenerate the last reply (see [Editing an earlier message of yours](#editing-an-earlier-message-of-yours)). |
| **The reply didn't start, so nothing was generated** | Card | The engine finished, but the chat itself changed before its reply started, and the card says what changed ("A message of the chat was sent, edited, swiped or deleted…", "A note for the next reply was reworded or dismissed…"). Nothing else you do in the RPEngine tab stops a reply: a turn runs on the settings and this chat's choices (its knowledge, the narrator's knowledge, its engine) it started with, so a change to them while it runs applies from the next turn; and a change to the world you make meanwhile (a payment in Money, a ✎ fix, a GM edit, a chip's Apply or Undo, a Realism level, detail you open) waits for the turn and lands once its reply is in. What it prepared is never sent stale, so nothing was written. **Retry** runs the engine again on the chat as it is now, then writes the reply (continuing it, for a **Continue**; for a new reply after a reply, the card says to press **Send** with nothing typed instead); **Diagnostics** opens the details. |
| **The reply failed** / **The reply came back empty** | Card | The engine had prepared the turn, but the narrator's own call failed (a provider error, a timeout, a dropped connection) or came back empty. The engine keeps its work for the turn: **Retry the reply** writes the reply again from that same turn, with the same reading, plan, minds and sections and no engine calls first; **Re-run the engine** runs the turn again (a fresh roll, as a Re-run swipe does), then writes the reply; **Diagnostics** shows why the reply failed. Lumiverse's own Regenerate or a swipe of the failed reply does what **Retry the reply** does. A **Continue** that failed is retried as a Continue: **Retry the reply** continues the same reply. A new reply you asked for after a reply (Send with nothing typed) that failed before writing anything has no **Retry the reply** button, because only your Send can start it again: press **Send** with nothing typed, and it goes out from the same turn; Lumiverse's Regenerate there would roll the reply before it again. When nothing came back, none of the turn applies until a reply goes through. If the empty reply's text turns up afterwards on the swipe that came back empty, while you're looking at it (Lumiverse saves it late, or you write it in yourself), that text is the turn's reply: the engine reads it and plays the turn once, with no engine calls, and the card goes. Editing another swipe of the message, such as the reply you swiped away from, is an ordinary edit (**Re-read edit**), and the card stays. Text that streamed before an error stays, unread, with **Retry read**, as a reply you stopped does; a retry writes its own swipe, so the turn counts once, with the swipe you keep. The card goes, and a retry runs the engine again, once anything the turn was made from changes: a message sent or edited, a ✎ fix, an Undo, a confirmation, or opening detail. |
| **An earlier turn's sections couldn't be cleared, so nothing was generated** | Card | You pressed Impersonate (or something asked for a preview of the prompt), and the world's book still held the engine's sections from an earlier turn, which Lumiverse refused to empty (the card says why). Those sections never go into a prompt that isn't theirs, so nothing was generated. Press the same button again; once Lumiverse saves World Info again it goes through with no engine section. **Diagnostics** shows each attempt. A reply you send instead runs the engine, which writes over them. |
| **Couldn't read your action** | Card | With the reading on your Decision connection, no call was left before the reply to read the details of the action it picked. Your text was played as plain roleplay. **Retry** re-runs the turn and reads it again. (A reading that fails stops the engine instead: see the card above.) |
| **Not an action?** | Receipt ("Read as an action") | The engine read your typed text as an action (or several) and you didn't mean it. **Not an action** undoes what it did, every action of the message, and a Re-run treats the text as plain roleplay. It lapses at your next message. |
| **Action: couldn't** / **Action: declined** | Receipt, or a card when it has buttons | An action you picked could not run (a requirement wasn't met, for example **Pay** from the Actions sheet without enough money), something you typed ran into a hard lock the world's author set (a safe before its code is found), or an action ran but part of it was refused. The chip says why, and nothing changes. |
| **Action: played as roleplay** | Receipt | Something you typed that the engine couldn't play as things stand: a requirement wasn't met (something you'd need isn't with you, or you're already there), the money can't cover what it costs ("I buy a yacht") or nothing gives it a price, or it names someone or something the world doesn't have ("I chat with Rena", when Rena is someone the story made up). Your words still stand: the reply plays them as plain roleplay, the narrator isn't told anything failed, and the engine keeps nothing of that action (if the reply then tells you buying it, the story pays: see [Prices and purchases](#prices-and-purchases)). The receipt says why ("Rena isn't anyone you know"; it says only the words you typed, and reads the same for anyone your character doesn't know, so it never tells you someone is hidden). The rest of your message still plays. Each such action gets its own receipt, with no buttons; it lapses at your next message, and continuing or re-reading the reply keeps it. Money you pay someone the story hasn't established isn't one of these: it goes to **World** (see [Typed text](#typed-text)). |
| **Added: a coffee** / **Added: $15.00 in cash** | Receipt | Under **Player claims: Make it real**, typed text that uses a thing you say you have and the world doesn't ("I pour Danny a coffee", with no coffee anywhere) adds it, in your hands (or where you are, for **Take**), and plays the action. Food and drink are used up once given or used. Money you say you have and don't, once you answer **Add**, goes into your cash, only as much as your cash lacks, and the whole amount is paid over in cash. **Undo** takes the thing or the money back with what you did with it. It lapses at your next message. |
| **Coffee isn't in this world yet. Add it and give it?** / **Only $85.00 of the $100.00 is with you as far as the world knows. Add the other $15.00 and give it?** | Card, on your message | The engine asks before it adds a thing you say you have: always under **Ask first**, under **Make it real** for a key, a weapon or a document, or with a cap for something costing more than the world's claim cap, and for money you say you have and don't carry under any setting but **Hold me to the world** (cash you carry is just handed over). Nothing is written until you answer. **Add** adds it and plays your message; **Not an action** plays the rest of it without that action. Either way one reply follows. |
| **Would you like to add this to your routine?** / **Added to your routine** | Card, on your message | Your message said someone's week has changed for good ("I start nights at the marina Monday", "Marcus says he's started swimming on Tuesdays"). The card names the change: who, what, when and where. Nothing changes until you answer, and the reply doesn't wait for it. **Yes** adds it to their routine from the next day, and the card becomes **Added to your routine** (or Marcus's) with **Undo**; **No** drops it. It lapses at your next message. |
| **Buy: declined** | Receipt | A **Buy** or **Pay** from the Actions sheet ran but the payment was declined; the chip gives the reason. See [Prices and purchases](#prices-and-purchases). |
| **Not tracked: (change)** | Receipt | The reply told something the engine has no place for, or can't keep as told: a thing introduced as if it were a new character, a "new" person with the name of someone you know, a payment of no amount or to or from a thing, a change to a thing that isn't where the change needs it, or a change naming someone, somewhere or something you don't know. Words the reply wrote stay as written ("Not tracked: You learn: The ferry stops running at nine.", "Not tracked: Marcus Delgado went to Old Mill"); where the engine would have turned an id into a name, someone, somewhere or something you don't know reads only as "someone you don't know", "a place you don't know" or "something you don't know" ("Not tracked: You went to a place you don't know"), whether the reply made it up or the world keeps it from you, so it reads the same in every world and never names a stranger. People, places and things you know keep their names. The story stands as told: nothing is asked, nothing changes, and the next reply isn't told otherwise. It lapses at your next message. Something already so (a fact you already know) gets no chip at all. |
| **Suggested choices** | Offer | Up to three next moves for you, each its own chip; **Send** sends one as your message. A suggestion that names someone or something your character doesn't know of, or carries a secret, is kept as a spoiler, unless your message or the reply named them: its chip stays folded (*A suggestion with a spoiler: tap to show*) until you tap it open. They lapse at your next message. Needs the *Suggested choices* step (Custom). |
| **Your other action** (for example "I buy a latte") | Offer | Your message held more than this turn played: the rest of its actions when the turn stopped early (at a check, an action that failed, or something that came up; see [Typed text](#typed-text)), or words no action covers. The chip holds them as you'd type them, and **Send** sends them as your next message, where they play as their own turn, with their own prices, time and checks. Time you let pass after your actions ("…and sleep until morning") isn't on this chip: once all of them have played, the reply tells it, and a Timelapse chip offers whatever the reply didn't. No chip is offered for a placeholder ("none"), for your first action said again (that would play it twice), or for an action that would give away a secret or someone you can't see that your message didn't name (the rest still plays), and nothing after an action that names something the world doesn't have (**Action: couldn't**). Your own words are always offered as you typed them, whoever they name. It lapses at your next message. |
| **Retry** | Card | The reply came back empty on a turn the engine didn't prepare (on a Lumiverse that can't stop a generation). The turn is marked *degraded*, and nothing is regenerated until you tap Retry. Otherwise an empty reply gets **The reply came back empty** (above). |
| **Retry read** | Card | The reply was stopped part-way ("The reply stopped early; read what was saved."), or reading it was: you cancelled the read, or on an agent chat a role set to *Stop the turn* failed after the reply. Nothing of that read stays. **Retry read** has the engine read everything saved by then, a Continue made afterwards included, and replaces what was read in the meantime, so no change or charge lands twice. On an agent chat it reads with the chat's roles again; if a role set to stop fails again, nothing is read, the chip stays and the tap says why. Once the reply is read, the chip goes. A Re-run that stopped after a message or swipe it depended on changed gets no chip: it can't be read where it was made, so play it again instead. |
| **Swipe as…** | Offer | **Re-narrate** keeps this turn's outcome and writes new prose; **Re-run** plays the turn again with a fresh roll. See [Swipes, edits and branches](#swipes-edits-and-branches). |
| **Re-read edit** | Card | You edited a reply, or a Continue was accepted without being read ("The engine kept the world as simulated. Re-read this text to update it."). This re-reads the text so the world matches it, replacing the earlier read: a change you had applied from its chips is undone and offered again, so nothing is charged twice. |
| **Not fully simulated** | Receipt | Part of the turn ran on the engine alone. There is no button; the turn is marked *degraded*. |
| **Keep report (n)** | Its own card, and in the Paused banner on **Now** | You changed something earlier in the chat and later turns were re-checked. The card lists what was dropped, with a **Convert** button for each row. A dropped purchase is one row, its payment first with "(2 linked changes)", and **Convert** converts the whole group. It also lists entries "made on another branch". |
| **Edited message: keep or discard later turns?** | Its own card, and in the Paused banner on **Now** | You edited an earlier message of yours. **Keep** re-checks the later turns; **Discard** removes them. |
| **Overdue: (thing)** | Card | A loan has run out ("Danny Okafor still has it; the loan ran out."). If you lent it, **Take it back**; if you borrowed it, **Give it back**. A loan also ends, and is no longer overdue, as soon as the thing is back with its owner (handed back, taken back) or is given to a new owner; passed on to someone else, it stays lent. |
| **Timelapse to 07:00 (8 h 55 min)?** | Offer | After an activity that takes a long time (a shift, a night's sleep), a chip offers a Timelapse to its end, with the weekday when it is another day: tap **Timelapse**. When the reply already told part of that time, the chip counts from where the reply left the clock; when it told all of it, there is no chip. See [Speed through](#speed-through-and-timelapse-offers). |
| **Timelapse 3 d?** | Offer | You asked in your own words to let more time pass than [Skips without asking](#skips-without-asking) runs on its own (a day unless you change it), or to let time pass after your actions and the reply didn't tell all of it. When the reply told part of a length, the chip offers only the rest ("The reply already told 45 min of the 1 h you asked for. Run the other 15 min as a timelapse?"); when it told all of it, or the clock is already at the time you asked for, there is no chip, so the same time never runs twice. Tap **Timelapse** to run it. |
| **Timelapse stopped: an invitation** | Offer | An interrupt ended a Timelapse early; the chip says why (an invitation, a bill, a declined payment, an incident, a quest event, a world rule, someone arriving, an overdue loan, a need, a weather alert) and **Continue** carries on to the original target. The narrator is told the span that actually ran. |
| **Continue timelapse (n left)** | Offer | A long Timelapse was cut at the safety cap; **Continue** carries on from there, with the time left. |
| **The narration skipped ahead — run it as a timelapse?** | Offer | The reply jumped ahead further than a turn can cover. **Run timelapse** runs the interval so the world catches up. |
| **Mode change** | Receipt | A type of change is now applied automatically (or went back to asking) because of how you've been answering. It has an **Undo** button. See [Learning on your own model](#learning-on-your-own-model). |
| **pending** | A mark | Not a chip but a mark: detail you opened, or changes you made, while a turn was under way: while the engine worked on it, or while its reply was being written. They land right after the reply finishes (or as soon as the turn stops without one); a ✎ fix or GM edit lands over whatever the reply changed. |
| **A change asks first** / **n changes ask first** (the review card) | Card | A chat that plays on agents (GM → This chat → **Engine**) puts every change of a turn that asks first (its type set to Confirm in **Settings → Changes from the reply**) on one card for the turn, in plain words as your character may read them: what an agent of the turn proposed, by its role (**Director**, **Minds**…), and what reading the reply proposed, each with its type of change (and its id, with **Show ids** on). **Apply all** applies them all; with several, tick the ones you want and **Apply n picked** applies those (after the reply the others still wait for you; before it they are turned down); **No** turns them all down. Applied changes get their **Undo** chip as GM edits do. With **Settings → Agents → Review card** on *after the reply* (the default), the turn goes on and the narrator isn't told of a change until you apply it; on *before the reply*, the card comes before the reply ("The turn waits for your answer") and your answer starts it. Once answered, the card says how each change stood: applied, turned down, or refused because it no longer applies. If the engine didn't answer with the card, it says **Couldn't read the changes that ask first**, with **Retry**. It lapses at your next message. |

Chips that start a turn (the Timelapse ones) work like actions from the Actions sheet, and they lapse when you send your next message.

### The Actions sheet

The **Actions** sheet opens from the input bar's **World actions…** button, from **Act** on the status line and in **Now**, and from **Actions** in the World sheet and the pill's details. It lists the actions that are possible right now, each with its targets, its minutes and price, and, for actions with a dice check, the **exact odds** of every outcome as one bar, with each outcome's chance in words beside it (success in bold). You see the odds before you commit, and they are the real ones: the roll that follows uses exactly those faces. An option that isn't possible stays in the list with a lock and says why in plain words ("Not possible here and now" when a requirement isn't met). A step of a quest or a secret that the story hasn't come to, or one already taken, isn't listed at all, so the sheet never tells you what lies ahead: it appears once the story reaches it, locked only by where you are or what you have. When the world's rules for checks decide without dice, the option says so instead of showing a bar: **No roll needed**, **Too hard to try** or **Retry needs a change**; **The story decides (odds 35%)** keeps the bar as the guidance (see [How checks are decided](#how-checks-are-decided)). Before your first turn in a chat, options are judged at your own realism settings, so an option offered isn't declined when the turn runs.

- **Actions**: whatever the world offers here, such as **Go to**, **Move on the map** (with the grid system), **Take**, **Drop**, **Give**, **Hand over**, **Give back**, **Fetch**, **Put away**, **Wear or equip**, **Take off**, **Use**, **Eat**, **Rest**, **Go to bed**, **Get up** (open while you sleep or rest), **Chat with**, **Lose something** (the cash in a lost wallet goes with it), **Transfer between accounts** and the world's own. **Go to** lists the places you can see, nearest first (a route away, or a place inside or around where you are). Once you pick a place, **Who comes along** lists the people with you that you can see, each with a switch: the ones you switch on come too. Typed, it brings along the people with you it names: "Danny and I head to the market" takes Danny, "we" takes everyone here; and your party (people the GM marks as travelling with you) always comes along. **Attempt** lists one option per stat on your sheet, each with its odds. **Take** from a kit asks for the kit, then a piece still in it, with how many are left ("Socket, 3/8 in drive (8 left)"). **Accept invitation** lists only the people who invited you.
  - **Things** among them: **Take** offers what is here that nobody has, up to what a person can carry (never a fridge, never what's in someone's pocket); taking something that isn't yours to use is recorded as taken, and it stays its owner's. **Give** gives a thing you carry for keeps: it becomes theirs. **Hand over** passes a thing you carry to someone here to hold, and it stays whose it was. **Give back** returns a thing to whose it is, here. **Fetch** goes and gets a thing of yours, or one you may use, from close by (the same building or site, a few minutes on foot), and takes those minutes. **Put away** puts a thing back where it's kept. **Drive**, **Use** and **Wear** offer only what you may use, and **Lend** what you may lend.
- **Buy**: what is sold where you are, one priced option for each offer, at the world's price for that place.
- **Pay** and **Lend**: money and belongings between you and others (moving money between your own accounts is **Transfer between accounts**, under Actions; a world's own action whose id ends in a part starting with `transfer`, the part after its last dot or the whole id when it has no dot, gets a **Transfer** tab). A money option asks for an **Amount** in the world's currency (the box shows its symbol or unit, and its limits), with only as many decimals as the currency has. **Lend** asks for the item, then the person, and has a number box for the days (in typed text you can say an end instead; see [Typed text](#typed-text)). An amount or a number outside its limits says what it has to be.
- **Drive**: pick a vehicle you can use, then the destination.
- **Timelapse**: duration buttons, *until* targets and interrupt overrides. See [Timelapse](#timelapse).
- **Combat**: in a world with combat, the actions that start a fight (**Fight**, and any ambush the world has) against whoever they may target; during a fight, your combat actions against each standing fighter you can see, with their exact odds, and **End turn**, under the round, whose turn it is and what you have left. While it's your turn in a fight, the sheet opens on this tab. See [Combat and encounters](#combat-and-encounters).

Actions that need a second pick show a second list, indented under the pick it follows; choosing again at any step replaces that pick and the ones after it. Only the tabs that have entries appear. The header holds **Pause clock** / **Resume clock** and a close button; the button at the foot of the sheet names your choice (for example "Drive: *vehicle* → *destination*"). Picking something, here, in a panel such as Money or on the map, doesn't play it yet: it waits for your next message, and the status line (on a desktop, the pill) says **With your next message:** and the action. Send your message and it plays that action, with your words as how you go about it, and the engine settles it first, then the narrator describes a result that is already decided. Picking the same thing again, or ✕, clears it; picking another replaces it; **Send now** plays it at once with no words. Bookkeeping you do alone, such as moving money between your own accounts, still happens at once (see [Your money](#your-money)).

The input bar has four buttons for the world: **World actions…**, **Timelapse…**, **Pause clock** / **Resume clock** and **World map**. They work only in a world chat.

### Typed text

You don't have to use the Actions sheet. Every message you type is read by a small helper call, which works out whether your character does something, which of the world's actions it is, and the details. No list of words decides whether a message is an action, so you can put it however you like. What you type is your character's will, and the story follows it: when an action's requirement isn't met (you're already there, or you'd need something you don't have with you), or the money can't cover what it costs or nothing gives it a price, the engine plays none of it, and your words play as plain roleplay, with a **…: played as roleplay** receipt saying why; the narrator isn't told anything failed. Only a hard lock the world's author set refuses it (a safe that opens only once its code is found): the reply shows **…: couldn't** and is told the attempt failed. Dice still decide what an action that plays does. The helper is offered the actions the Actions sheet would list, so a step of a quest or a secret the story hasn't come to is never one: "I ask Marcus about the shop's money" before anything has told you of it plays as roleplay, not as an action the reply has to tell you failed. A thing that matches nothing in the world, or nothing your character knows of, is up to the **Player claims** setting (below); under **Hold me to the world**, and always for people and places, the action is left out and your words play as plain roleplay: the reply shows **Pay: played as roleplay** (with the action's own name) and why, for example "Rena isn't anyone you know", and nothing is said to the narrator about it. The rest of the message still plays, so "I pull on my work jacket and walk down to Alder Automotive" walks you there even when the world has no jacket. One message can hold several actions ("I walk to Bluebird Coffee, buy a latte and sit by the window"): up to five play in order in the same turn, each from where the one before left you, and their times add up. A quest stage one of them reaches is reached before the next plays, and the reply is told of it. An action that plays as roleplay doesn't stop the rest. The turn stops early at a check (it rolls, and you see how it fell before going on), at an action that fails (a hard lock), or when something comes up: you go down (dying, or worse), a fight, a long activity such as sleep that offers or runs a Timelapse, being moved somewhere you didn't go, or anything you'd stop a Timelapse for (an invitation, someone arriving, a quest moving on), as long as you could see it. Then the reply offers the rest as a chip whose **Send** sends it as your next message, so it plays as its own turn. If it got it wrong, tap **Not an action?**: that undoes it, and a Re-run treats the text as plain roleplay. **Not an action?** takes back the actions themselves; a quest stage one of them reached stays reached. Reading a message costs one call on every preset, whether or not it holds an action; an action you pick from the Actions sheet or a chip needs none, and it still has to meet its requirements and be paid for.

**Money to someone the story hasn't established.** Typed money never just vanishes. **Pay** takes anyone the world shows your character who keeps money: a person, a business, a household or a trust, wherever they are, so "I pay Alder Automotive $50" pays the shop even from home, and the Actions sheet offers the ones around you too. Paying a thing or a place your character knows pays whoever it belongs to, when your character knows of them: the shop's tip jar pays the shop. Money you put in something of your own ("I put $5 in my piggy bank") stays yours: nothing moves, and it plays as roleplay with a receipt. Anyone or anything else, as far as your character knows, pays **World**, the world's catch-all for money that leaves you for somewhere the story doesn't keep track of: "I drop $5 in the tip jar" when the world has no tip jar, or "I tip the busker $2" when no busker is in the world. Someone your character can't know is paid the same way, to World, so nothing tells you they exist. How it's paid follows your words ("in cash", "by card", "from savings"); when they don't say, it follows the purchase: cash into a jar, to a busker or at a counter, a card online or by phone, and otherwise cash for something or someone in front of you when the cash you carry covers it, else the way you usually pay, as for a business or someone who isn't with you. The reply's Outcome says how it was paid and from which account. The intent gate's card names the receiver in your own words ("Pay: the tip jar, $5.00"), and its **Edit** keeps whom it pays and how. Your Money history shows the payment as "Payment", to whoever got it (World for a receiver the story hasn't established). A payment with no amount still plays as roleplay with its receipt ("it needs an amount"), and a reply that tells the same payment again doesn't charge you twice. Handing over cash works the same way: "I put $5 in the jar" pays the jar's owner, or World, from your cash.

**Send with nothing typed** after a reply asks for the next reply, and nothing is read: the message you sent before it has already played, so it isn't read, gated or asked about again. The people with you still choose what they do, and their minds think from the reply before it.

**Things you say you have.** When an action uses a thing the world doesn't have, as far as your character knows ("I pour Danny a coffee" with no coffee anywhere), the [**Player claims**](#player-claims) setting decides. **Make it real** (the default) adds it, in your hands, plays the action and shows **Added: a coffee** with **Undo**; food and drink are used up once given or used. **Ask first** asks before the reply: **Coffee isn't in this world yet. Add it and give it?**, with **Add** and **Not an action**. **Hold me to the world** leaves the action out, and your words play as plain roleplay with **Give: played as roleplay**, as above. Money is handed over as cash, in a world that keeps track of money: "I hand Danny a twenty" with $85 on you just pays him the twenty from your cash, asking nothing, under any setting. Money you say you have and don't asks first, unless you're held to the world, and only for what you lack: "I hand Danny $100" asks **Only $85.00 of the $100.00 is with you as far as the world knows. Add the other $15.00 and give it?**, and **Add** puts the $15 in your cash and pays Danny the $100 in cash (**Added: $15.00 in cash**, with **Undo**). The money goes only as your words would hand it over: to someone who isn't there, it plays as roleplay, and nothing is added. Money with no amount or no one to give it to plays as roleplay too. Keys, weapons and documents always ask first, and with a cap anything costing more than the world's claim cap asks. A thing you add never stands in for one the world has: an added key doesn't open a door that needs a particular key (what you do with it plays as roleplay, or, at a door its author locked hard, fails), and adding one never tells you whether such a thing exists.

**Answers to puzzles.** An answer counts only in your own words. The helper that reads your message may know more than your character (a code in the lore, the name of someone you've never met), so when what it reads as your answer isn't in your message, case and spacing aside ("the FUEL pump relay" or "1 8 8 7" count as typed), nothing is tried: the reply shows **Answer: played as roleplay**, "The message doesn't say the answer itself", without repeating what the helper read, and your words play as plain roleplay. It isn't a wrong answer, so it earns no hint. A name counts by any word of it that only one person, place or thing your character knows has: "It was Danny" names Danny Okafor while you know one Danny, and when you know two, say which ("Okafor", or the whole name). Someone your character can't know counts only by what you type, and a description ("whoever was in the shop last night") names no one. Accents, letters like ø or ß and digits of another script don't matter. A name with 's ("Okafor's truck") is about something of theirs, so it isn't an answer: say the name itself. A one-letter answer counts typed alone, in quotes or as a capital ("door B"), since "a" and "I" are just words. A number counts by its digits, whatever words the helper adds around them, but not written out in words ("eighteen eighty-seven"). The same holds for an answer later in your message ("I nap for an hour, then punch in the code"): it isn't tried, and the rest of your message is never offered with an answer you didn't type in it. Say the answer itself ("The answer is …"), or pick **Answer** on the Actions sheet and type it there.

**Routines you state.** When your message says that someone's week has changed for good, your character's ("I start nights at the marina Monday") or someone else's that they told you of ("Marcus says he's started swimming on Tuesdays"), the engine never changes it on its own: your message gets **Would you like to add this to your routine?** (or **to Marcus's routine?**), naming who, what, when and where. **Yes** adds it to their routine in this chat from the next day, and **Undo** takes it back; **No** leaves their routine as it was. When the place you name fits several places you know ("the lake"), the card offers each (**At Lakefront Park**, …) instead of **Yes**, and the one you pick is where it happens; when it fits none you know of ("the community pool"), the card says so, and **Yes** adds it with no set place. GM → Routines shows it at once among the changes the story made, by its own name, marked **From your message** with your message as its turn (a change the reply made reads **The story**). A question ("do you still swim on Tuesdays?"), a passing mention, a one-off or a plan asks nothing, and neither does a change already in their routine or someone your message doesn't name.

**Attempts.** When your message tries something whose outcome is uncertain and no action covers it ("I try to talk the bouncer into letting me in", "we sneak past the guard"), the reading can treat it as a check of your character's own sheet (**Try something**): a skill your sheet has, how hard it looks, the companions who join in ("we") and the people it goes up against. A question about someone counts too: "I watch Marcus as he answers: is he lying?" rolls your read of people against his deceit. The people it goes up against set how hard it is, rolling against you when everyone involved rolls, and each companion rolls their own; their numbers stay hidden, so the receipt shows only your own roll and how each companion did, and the narrator is told how it went and to narrate it that way. Whether and when this happens is the chat's rules for inferred checks (see [Inferred checks](#inferred-checks)): **Suggest** asks first with a **Roll Persuasion?** card, **Roll automatically** just rolls, and **Off** reads your message as it was read before inferred checks: a message that says it uses a skill ("I use my Mechanics skill on the engine") is read as the world's own **Use a skill**, **Attempt** or the like and rolls that action's check, and an attempt no action covers plays as roleplay. An action of the world's own that rolls its own check keeps it: the same attempt is never rolled twice.

A message can also take a move for granted. "I sit down next to Marcus" while your character is at the market has you somewhere you aren't, so it is read as going first to where you last saw Marcus, as if you had said so; the same goes for something that belongs to another place ("at the shop's counter"). And when the last reply's chip held the rest of your message, a message that takes those actions as done ("I set my chips and soda on the counter") plays them first, in order. If the reading misses a move anyway and the reply has you somewhere the engine doesn't, the reply gets an **Apply** chip for that move: see **Moving somewhere** in [Extraction modes](#extraction-modes).

What the helper reads besides your message: what your character can do now (the world's own actions and the built-in ones, each with a line on what it does, what it takes, such as the people and things it can name, and what it needs, in words), where and when you are, who is there, the people your message names who aren't with you (with where and how long ago you last saw them), what you carry, your vehicles, the invitations waiting on your answer, what your last message left unplayed and the places you know, all as your character knows them, and the messages before yours, so a line that hangs on the last reply ("I take what she offered", "I follow him out") is read from it. A stage direction ("[Alex heads to the roof]") or the story told about your character counts as what you do; a line that is only speech, or out of character ("((OOC: …))"), plays as roleplay, unless it tells time to pass (below). When it can't tell what you mean, it reads no action rather than guess. How far back it reads is the **Recent chat** setting on the *Interpret typed input* card in **Settings → Steps and connections**: the last 8 messages and about 3,000 tokens unless you change it (either can be **no limit**, and switching it off reads none); a long reply that doesn't fit whole is read from its end.

Typed text can also let time pass ("wait until noon", "sleep until the next morning", "skip ahead to Friday at 6 PM"). An out-of-character time skip is always read as one, whatever else your message says: "OOC: over the next hour, I sort the stock", "(OOC: skip ahead two hours)", "[OOC] later that evening". So is time your character spends on something no action covers ("I spend the next hour sorting the stock"). What you say happens in that time is what fills it, and the reply tells it: the turn is a Timelapse of that span, run just as one from the **Timelapse** tab is (with character minds on, a [time skip](#character-minds)), never the activity you describe at its usual length. The one exception is a message that, before its out-of-character part, gets your character out of bed or up from a rest: "I get out of bed and make coffee. (OOC: skip an hour.)" plays **Get up** first, so the night ends there, and the reply tells the hour after it. What the out-of-character part itself says still fills the skip, so "(OOC: skip to morning, I get up)" sleeps through to the morning. To be sure a night ends, send **Get up** on its own first. The span is read from your own words, the same way every time: a length ("over the next hour", "for the next two hours", "half an hour", "an hour and a half"), a vague one ("a moment", "a few moments" and "a few minutes" are 5 minutes, "a little while" 15, "a while" 30, "a few hours" 3 hours, so "a few minutes later" skips 5), a part of the day ("skip ahead to evening", "later that night", "tomorrow morning"), a moment of your own day ("until dinner", "until lunch", "until breakfast", "until bedtime", "until morning": to when your character's day has that meal, goes to bed or wakes up, or, when it doesn't say, to the evening, midday, morning or night), your next shift or commitment ("until my next shift"), or a day and time you name. A message that doesn't ask for time to pass is never made a Timelapse by the time words in it: "“I'll be back in an hour,” I tell Danny" is only speech. Time you let pass after your actions ("…then wait two hours") is the reply's to tell once they have all played: the narrator is told to tell it after them, and the clock moves by what the reply tells. A **Timelapse** chip after the reply offers only what it didn't tell, and none when it told it all; more than [Skips without asking](#skips-without-asking) is never told, only offered.

What you say happens in a skip is an instruction, and nothing the engine tells the narrator overrules it. The narrator gets your words as what your character does in that time, in the Outcome, the Outline and the Montage, and is never told to "add no events" against them. A skip you don't fill ("OOC: skip ahead an hour") gets a plain line that time passes, and what else happened in it is in the Montage. While minds read your message (**Settings → Minds**, **The player's message**), your words also reach what plans the skip: the skip plan for the people the model plans, the step that looks for moments to stop at, and the minds of the people with your character at those moments and at its end. A Timelapse you set on the sheet and send with a message is filled by that message. A skip too long to run without asking keeps your words on its **Timelapse** chip, and so does a **Continue timelapse** chip, after the safety cap or an interruption ("Timelapse stopped: …"): tapped, it runs just as the skip would have run in the turn, your words and all.

A length of time up to [Skips without asking](#skips-without-asking) (a day unless you change it) runs as the turn's own Timelapse, with your interrupt policy and a Montage; a longer one is never run unasked: a **Timelapse 3 d?** chip offers it, and **Timelapse** runs it. An *until* runs as the turn's own Timelapse however far off it is, so "sleep until my next shift" runs to the shift even when it is days away (a long run still stops at the safety cap, with a **Continue timelapse** chip). A day and time you name runs to exactly then, in the time where your character is (the time the status line shows), however far off it is: one more than 366 days away runs there at most 366 days at a time, each step ending with a **Continue timelapse** chip. A time that has already passed doesn't run, and the Journal says why ("Timelapse not run: that time has passed"). The Actions sheet's **Timelapse** tab has no day-and-time entry; type it instead. The narration can still skip ahead on its own, as described in [When the story jumps ahead](#when-the-story-jumps-ahead).

Some details can be said in your own words. A loan ends when you say it does: "I lend Danny my truck until 7 tonight", "for 3 hours", "until Friday", "tomorrow morning". A said end wins over the days. Words that name no time fall back to the days you gave, and a lend with neither is declined ("… names no time; say how many days or until when"). A piece of a kit can be named by its words in any order ("the metric combination wrench"): filler words and plurals don't count, at least half the words must match, and a lone number doesn't.

### Prices and purchases

When the story has you buy something, the engine prices it from the world's catalog, or from the item's category band with a little seeded variance. A purchase described in your own words ("a round of beers", "a large pepperoni pizza for delivery") is priced from a catalog row only when the row names it: whole words (plurals count), the item's main noun among them, preferring the row that holds most of its words and then what the place sells. A "desk lamp" is not a desk, a "coffee machine" is not a coffee, and "lantern oil" is not vegetable oil (a row that is another kind of the thing doesn't name it); with no row that fits, the stated price is used, then the category. A word that tells none of the catalog's kinds apart leaves the place to choose: "two large coffees" are two cafe drip coffees at a coffee shop, taxed as prepared food, and a bag of ground coffee at the grocery store. Brindlemere and Harrowmere price their categories in their own money (bits and pence). For something the catalog doesn't list you can state the price yourself, and the engine uses it. In worlds set to *lenient* pricing, a price the narrator states is accepted if it falls inside the band, and clamped (with a chip) if it falls outside. A price the narration gives for something the catalog doesn't list is capped at the world's **narrated-price cap** (by default the top price of its dearest catalog item or category, or 500 of its currency without either), and the chip says so ("narrated $12,000.00, capped at $6,000.00"). To set it, open **Build → Packs → economy**: the **Narrated-price cap** field takes it as the world's currency writes it ("$6,000.00"), shows the default it uses when left empty, and Problems warns about a cap that isn't a whole number above zero. Two things bought are two purchases: when the reply bundles your own purchase with something else ("a coffee and a bran muffin"), your part is dropped and the rest is priced on its own.

**Prices in the story.** The narrator states the prices the engine charges. Wherever your character is, the narrator's Money section lists what the place sells at the engine's price there (before sales tax, where it runs), and the narrator is told to give that exact price whenever a purchase or a price comes up, or what the Outcome says a purchase this turn cost. A catalog item's price where it is sold is one price for the day: the Buy options, the narrator's list and what a purchase there charges all match, and another day may bring another price. To keep prices out of the narration, switch off **Settings → Narrator → Prices**: the narrator then gets no list and no rule, and purchases are priced and charged as ever. The rule's words are yours to change in **Settings → Agents → Prompts → Final output**, under Narrator rules (**Prices**).

**How you pay.** A purchase can say how it is paid, whether you pick Buy or write it: "with my credit card" pays on a card; "my debit card", "checking" or "from the bank" pays from checking; "from savings" pays from savings; "in cash" or "from my wallet" pays from your wallet. When you have several cards or accounts, the one you or the reply names pays: by its name ("with my Visa", "from the rainy day fund"), its bank ("my Lakeshore card", "the American Express") or its last four digits ("the card ending in 4417"), and a card in your gear the reply names as the payer pays as that card. Words that only say a kind ("my credit card") or fit several alike name none of them, so the purchase goes the way you'd usually pay among them, and its chip says which account took it ("From Your checking account: $1,667.27 left."). A card you don't have falls back to checking, and any other missing account falls back to the way the engine would pay anyway. The same holds for a purchase the narration describes: "paid with my debit card" charges checking, and if the reply names something someone owns as the payer (a debit card in the wallet), its owner pays the way its name says. A named source pays only when it is the payer's own cash, checking, savings or card account; a loan or brokerage account named as the source is ignored and the usual way is used. When you type a Buy and the reading of your line leaves the way of paying out, your own words fill it, but only when you say it as a payment: after "with", "on", "using", "by", "from" or "in" ("pay with my credit card", "in cash", "from savings", "on my Chase Sapphire card", "with the card ending in 4417"). A mention you rule out ("not with cash") is skipped and the last mention counts, so "not with cash, with my debit card" pays by debit card, and "I buy a credit card holder for $12" is something you buy, not how you pay.

**Money handed over.** When the reply has someone hand over money ("Danny hands me $20 for gas", "a twenty", "a $20 bill"), the engine moves that amount from their money to yours and never turns cash into an item. Cash handed over goes into the wallet of the person who gets it (or their checking, if they carry no cash), and comes out of the giver's wallet when it holds enough, otherwise the way they'd usually pay. Only an item that is money as a whole counts: "my $300 jacket" or "a $20 gift card" is a thing with a price, handed over as a thing. A payment in the story is between people, or between someone's own cash, checking, savings or card accounts ("I move $100 from checking to savings" moves it; nothing is lost or made up). A payment to or from a thing, a loan or an investment account, or one with no amount, can't be kept: a **Not tracked** receipt says so instead of letting it vanish, the story stands as told, and the next reply isn't told otherwise.

**When you can't pay.** From Money level 2 up, a payment you choose in the Actions sheet that the account can't cover is declined: nothing changes hands and no item is created. The action still runs, the narrator is told "it didn't go through (… doesn't have $X available)" and to narrate it failing, and the reply shows a **Buy: declined** chip with the reason (no buttons; it lapses at your next message). If an action's own requirement fails (**Pay** requires that you can afford it), a **Pay** you picked in the Actions sheet gets **Pay: couldn't** instead. A **Buy** or **Pay** you typed that the money can't cover, or a **Buy** of something nothing gives a price, plays as plain roleplay instead, with **…: played as roleplay** saying why, and the rest of your message still plays; if the reply then tells the purchase, the story pays for it (below).

**When the story pays anyway.** The story always wins: if the reply tells a purchase or payment your money can't cover, it still happens. What you bought is yours, and the money goes the way the reply says you paid when that way can take it: "paid with my debit card" charges your checking account, even past what it holds. Otherwise it goes the way you'd pay: the first way that covers it, and if none does, the last of your ways that can run a debt takes it all, so your checking account goes overdrawn or your credit card runs over its limit. Your wallet and your savings never go below zero for it: if nothing of yours can run a debt, a savings account that covers it pays. They don't after a switch either: switch an earlier reply to a swipe where the wallet or savings that paid holds less, and a purchase it can't cover there is dropped with what it bought (see [Switching to an earlier swipe](#switching-to-an-earlier-swipe)). The chip says so ("Bought brass ship's compass: $174.90 (over Your credit card's limit)", "On Your credit card: $4,624.90 owed, $124.90 over its limit."), the Money hub shows the account as overdrawn or over its limit, and nothing tells the next reply it didn't happen. An overdraft fee isn't counted against you there: when your overdraft covered the purchase and only its fee went past the limit, the chip just says what your account holds now ("From Your checking account: overdrawn by $116.90."). The money your overdraft moved in from a linked account stands only with the purchase: after a switch that leaves the linked account unable to spare it, the purchase still stands and takes your checking account further overdrawn, and where the switch drops the purchase itself, that money stays where it was. On the chip:

- **Pay another way** moves the payment to the way the reply named, once that way covers it, else to the first of your savings, cards, checking or cash that covers it now. Whatever the payment brought with it goes back too: the money your overdraft moved in from a linked account returns to it, and the overdraft fee is refunded. If no way covers it, a notice says "No other way to pay covers it." and the chip stays. After a switch it puts right only what happened on that version of the story: money your overdraft didn't move in there isn't sent back, and a purchase the switch dropped gets the notice "This payment was dropped from this version of the story, so there's nothing to pay another way." with nothing moved (and **Edit amount** on it, "This purchase was dropped from this version of the story, so there's nothing to reprice.").
- What you put right with **Pay another way** or **Edit amount** goes with the purchase. Switch to an earlier swipe where the purchase didn't happen and the correction doesn't either: no money comes back for a purchase never made, and the account you moved it to pays nothing. Where only your overdraft's transfer didn't happen, the payment still moves, and the linked account gets nothing back that it never sent.
- **Undo** (or **No**, under Confirm) takes the purchase and its charge back.

When nothing of yours covers it and nothing can run a debt (your checking account and cards are frozen or closed, say), no money moves. A purchase is still yours with nothing charged, and its chip says so ("Bought brass ship's compass: nothing charged", "Nothing you pay with covers $174.90 or can run a debt: it's yours with nothing charged."). A payment is a **Not tracked** receipt that says why ("… has nothing that covers $200.00 or can run a debt, so nothing was paid."); the story stands as told.

When someone else's purchase has no price, its chip says whose it is ("Danny Okafor has it, with nothing charged", or "Apply gives it to Danny Okafor"), never that it's yours.

At Money level 1 nothing is declined either: payments overdraw, and the books track it in bands.

### The map

The map lives in **Play → Places**. A bar along its top holds the **breadcrumbs**, the **List of places** button and the **Full screen** button. Drag to pan, scroll or pinch to zoom, and use the **Zoom in**, **Zoom out** and **Fit map** buttons. **List of places** lists the places you know at the level on screen, with where you stand with each and the quickest way there.

Tap a place, on the map or in the list, for its place sheet: whether you're here, inside it, have been there or have only heard of it, and **Getting there**, where each travel option shows its mode (walk, road, off-road or water), time, vehicle and cost, or why it isn't available ("No route from here."). A drive tells how its time breaks down: "13 min: 4 min to reach it at home, 1 min to get going, 6 min driving, 1 min to park, 1 min on foot from Harbor Street". Your vehicles are offered wherever you know they are, within a walk (you walk to it first); one that can't get all the way there is left where the walk from it is shortest, and is offered only when that beats walking. Choose one and the move or drive is sent. A place with places inside it offers **Open** *place*, and **Show on the map** takes you back to it on the map. A place you can't make out reads **Somewhere** and offers nothing. Where you are, the place sheet adds **Things here**: **In the room** (the furnishings), **Lying here**, and what is in each holder there that you may use. Your own things there have Manage's controls (**Take**, **Put in…**, **Put away**), at once, with Undo.

**Full screen** opens the map over the chat, as do the **World map** button in the input bar and **Map** in the World sheet and the pill's details. On a desktop it is a floating widget (480 px wide, resizable, with **Fullscreen** and **Close map** buttons); the phone and the desktop remember its size and position separately. On a phone (a screen up to 600 px wide, or any touch screen), opening it closes the drawer first and goes fullscreen; if the drawer can't close, Play opens on **Places** instead. Over the chat, a place's details dock over the map rather than opening a sheet; Escape closes the dock first, then the map. Choosing a way to travel closes the map. Place names never overlap: a name sits beside its dot, or further out on a short line, on two lines, or shortened (the full name shows in the place's sheet or dock when you tap it); on a crowded map a name may be left off, and its dot stays tappable.

The map shows one place at a time, with the places directly inside it (a town's streets and shops, a manor's rooms) and the routes between them. It opens where you are: on your own place when there are places inside it, otherwise on the nearest place around you that has a map or a grid, or else on the place just around yours. The **breadcrumbs** lead back out, a level at a time, up to the **World** (the places at the top of the world). When the place on screen is the one you stand in, its breadcrumb says **you are here**. The map draws the places you know and the places you're inside. A stranger's home you were let into keeps its name while you're anywhere in it, its kitchen as much as its hall; a secret place you're inside, or a room of one, shows only as **Somewhere**, never by its name, and so it does on the status line and the pill, on the scene card, on your sheet and in People. A room inside a place you don't know about is as unknown as that place, and nothing offers a way there. Travel isn't limited to the place on screen: the Actions sheet and a typed move reach any place you know, and so does the map from any of its levels. From Space and travel level 2, a place with a grid shows its walls, doors and terrain behind its rooms, and from level 3 the square you're standing on.

**Known** and **Fog**, on the map, choose how it shows what you know, for this chat only (until you pick, the world's choice). **Known** draws a place you've heard of but never been to as a dashed ring, and its sheet says so. **Fog** dims the places you haven't been to and lays fog over what you haven't seen: the squares you haven't had in sight when the world uses the grid system, or else the rooms you haven't been in. Under fog there is nothing to find: the map is sent without what lies there, and a room you don't know about is just more fog. With **Known**, a room you don't know about is solid nothing on the grid. Things lying on a grid's squares show as small amber diamonds only where you see them now: within your sight when the world uses the grid system, or else in the place you're in. Something you saw earlier isn't shown, so the map never tells you where it went. Tap the square to see what lies there.

When the world uses the **grid system**, you stand on a square of the grid you're on, and walls and doors are walked: tap a square of that grid and its card offers **Walk here** with the time it takes, going around the walls and through the doors, those under fog included, or says "No way there from here.". Walking into a room puts you in it, but never into a room you don't know about, nor through one. You see a few squares around you (the world sets how far), and walls block the view.

### Detail on demand

The world doesn't store every coin in every wallet. When you look at something that has no detail yet (open a trunk, check what a character is carrying, audit an account, open **Play → Me → Money**), the engine generates it then, consistent with what is already known: a character known to be "rich" gets a balance inside the rich band. Once shown, it is recorded and never changes. Previews in the inspector are generated from the same seed but are not recorded, and GM mode shows what isn't generated yet as it stands (an account not opened yet shows the balance it will open with). If a recorded detail is removed from the story, for example by a branch taken before it was shown, it is generated again from the same seed the next time you look, and it can come out differently if what's known about it has changed since.

## GM mode

**GM** mode shows everything the engine knows, including secrets, truths you haven't discovered and people who were removed, and lets you change any of it. It wears a gold frame, so you can tell at a glance that you're behind the curtain. Looking never changes your chat; only the edits you save do. The [GM guide](GM-GUIDE.md) covers running a chat from here.

### The gate

The truth sits behind a spoiler gate, **Spoilers ahead**: tap **Show the truth** to open it. While it is open, a bar at the top of every page behind it says **The truth is showing**, with **Hide** to put it away. The gate closes again when you tap **Hide**, when you leave GM mode, and when you open another chat. What it guards is fetched only while it is open, so none of it travels with what Play shows you. This chat's rules and the Inspector don't need the gate.

### The GM bar

A gold bar along the bottom of the drawer holds the GM's places: **World**, **Places**, **Things** and **Story**, then a place for each pack that adds one (**Minds**, **Routines**, **Orgs** and **Money**), then the agents' **Assistant** and **Workspace**, then **This chat**. All but **This chat** are behind the gate.

- **World**: every person, item, vehicle, group and account in the world, including the ones spawned during this chat and the removed ones, with **Search by name** and a kind filter. Badges mark **You**, **Removed**, a life state other than alive, anything that isn't public (**Known by**, **Secret**) and **Claimed**, a thing you said you had that the engine made real (see [Player claims](#player-claims)), and a row says when someone lives their own day or was spawned here. Tap one to open its page (see below). **Spawn** opens a page that adds someone or something new to this chat: a name, a kind, a place and a tier. While a fight is under way, a **Fight under way** card leads the list; tap it for the **Fight** page, with everyone in the fight, seen or not, in initiative order, with HP exact and the round's acts. While there are claimed things, **Claimed by the player** comes next: each one still in the world, by name, with who holds it or where it lies ("held by Sam Rivera", "at Rosie's Diner"). Tap one to open its page, where **Remove from the world** takes it out of the story; one removed, or a drink or a meal used up, leaves that list. Kinds and the world's places are the world's own definitions: the button at the bottom of the list, **Kinds and places are the world's: edit them in Build**, takes you to Build.
- **Places**: every place, under the place it's in, with its visibility (public, discoverable, secret and so on).
- **Things**: the chat's things as they stand, by where you are, by place and by owner, with **Tidy up** and **Keep in the world** (see [Things in GM mode](#things-in-gm-mode)).
- **Story**: every quest, secret and agenda, with its stage and the stages that carry a truth (the one it's at is marked **Now**). Pick a stage under **Stage** to move the track there at once. A stage that follows from where the track stands is entered as the story would enter it: the stage it leaves grants its rewards (unless the new one is how it fails), and the new one applies its effects, grants its own rewards when it is an end, and reveals its truth. A stage it has passed only sets the stage, so nothing is granted or revealed twice. Either way the **GM edit** chip has its **Undo**. Your pick wins over the story: if the story moves the track by itself, in a Re-run or a re-read of that reply or in the reply your pick waited for, the move is made again from where the story left the track. A track the story already took to your stage stays as it is, so nothing is paid twice; one anywhere else moves on to your stage from there.
