- **Minds**: every character's place on the fidelity ladder and why, their latest mind, plans, commitments, notes and summary, with pins and edits (see [Minds in GM mode](#minds-in-gm-mode)).
- **Routines**: every character's routine and schedules as this chat has them, how much their routine binds them now, today as laid, their invitations and what the story changed, with per-chat changes (see [Routines in GM mode](#routines-in-gm-mode)).
- **Orgs**: every organization, the party first, each page as the GM sees it with the GM's controls (see [GM → Orgs](#gm--orgs)).
- **Money**: anyone's money, as their own money hub with the GM's controls (see [Everyone else's money](#everyone-elses-money)).
- **Assistant**: a chat with the GM assistant about this chat's world, whose changes wait for you (see [Assistant](#assistant)).
- **Workspace**: the notes the agents keep for this chat (see [Workspace](#workspace)).
- **This chat**: this chat's rules, its edits and the inspector (see [This chat](#this-chat)).

**Changing many at once.** **Select** (beside **Spawn**) turns the **World** list into a list you pick from: tap rows to pick them (a character's row shows their fidelity level), and the bar under the list, which stays above GM mode's tabs, shows how many you've picked, with **Select all** (everyone the list shows, its search and kind filter as they stand), a button for each fidelity level under **Select a level** (exactly the characters at that level) and **Clear**. Under its title, **Change…** says who is picked ("7 people, you among them and 8 things") and opens one change for everyone picked: **Tier**, **Detail in one area** (a level, or **From the tier**), **Their routine** (its role in this chat, or **As the world has it**), **Pin their fidelity** (or **By score**), **Your intent for them**, **Move to a place** and *your character*'s **party** (a tier, or **Not in the party**). Every control starts on **Choose…**, and **Apply** stays off until you pick a value; each card says how those it reaches stand now ("Now: 3 Leads, 2 Supporting cast"). A tier or a level goes to the people picked, unless you turn on **Also the … picked that aren't people**; a routine, pin or intent change goes to the characters among them, never your own character; a move to anyone not removed; and a party tier to the people picked but your own character, only where it changes something (a tier change keeps since when they joined). **Apply** says how many it reaches, and is off when none of them fit. A change is sent as edits of at most 64 each; if one is refused, the ones before it stay and the rest aren't sent. **Done** stops picking. What you pick stays in GM mode: nothing in Play shows it.

### Assistant

**GM → Assistant** is a chat with the GM assistant about this chat's world, behind the gate. Type a message and **Send** it (or press Ctrl+Enter). Ask it anything ("Who knows about the shop's debt?", "Where is Danny?", "What does Jordan carry?"): it looks things up with the engine's own tools and answers only from what they show it. It works outside the story, for you: it is no one in it, and nothing in the world is kept from it, so it reads everything GM mode shows you, each secret with who knows it. Its system prompt is followed by your **AGENTS.md** and **roles/assistant.md**, if you wrote them in GM → Workspace (see [Workspace](#workspace)). Each message it is sent goes with the conversation so far, newest first, as much as **History tokens** holds; when older messages are left out, the first message it is sent says how many. While it answers, a running row shows its label, the tokens it has spent so far and **Cancel**, which works at once, even while an Apply is still landing, and **Send** waits for it; the drawer's activity line says only "Asking the GM assistant…". Each answer shows its paragraphs, headings, lists, code blocks, quotes, bold, italics and code as they read, a link as its words and address (never one to follow) and an image as its description (never loaded), and nothing in it runs in the page; your own messages show as you wrote them. Each answer says what it spent and folds away what it looked up (each tool it called, in short, with what a failed call gave back); one that stopped short says how (at a limit, repeating a call, cancelled, or failed) and why. Ask it to change something and it proposes the change instead of making it: a card under its answer lists each change in plain words, with **Apply all**, **Apply picked** for the ones you tick, or **No**; a change of a kind you've set to ask first says so. Applied, the changes are one GM edit with its **Undo** chip, as an edit you make yourself, and the card shows what landed and what was refused, and why, with **Undo** until the next reply; changes applied while a turn runs wait for the turn and land once its reply is in, so the turn goes on as planned, and their card shows **Undo** then, as the chat does; if the turn's close refuses one, its card says why and waits for Apply or No again, unless a learned note it wrote beside it already landed: that note stays applied, and so does the card, which offers **Apply again** for the refused changes alone, so the note isn't written twice. An Undo pressed in the chat or in GM → Edits marks the card undone too; a learned note it wrote beside the edit stays, as no Undo reaches the learned notes, and the card says so. With **Apply** set to *At once*, its changes apply as soon as it has answered, each with Undo, except a kind of change you've set to ask first. What you apply, turn down and undo teaches it, per kind of change, on its connection, as your confirms and undos teach the reply's changes: a kind you nearly always apply comes to apply at once, and one you often undo goes back to asking. **Settings → Changes from the reply** shows each kind's mode under *The GM assistant's changes*, with where it came from, and pins it. The conversation is this chat's: a Branch keeps it as it stood, and **Clear** forgets it, after asking; the changes you applied stay. If its connection turns out to take no tools, the assistant asks again with them written out, and Diagnostics shows the refused call.

### Live

**GM → Live** shows an agent turn as its agents work it, behind the gate, like a terminal session: open it while a turn runs and it catches up on what has happened so far, then follows along. Each role run and each main-agent stage is a group you can fold, in the order it started, with its role, its stage or phase, its model, how it stands (working, done, failed, stopped, cancelled), its tokens and its time. Inside: what each agent was told (its instructions, folded), each message it was sent, its reasoning when the provider returns any, its answers (long ones fold under their first line), each tool call as a card, a stop with why, and every change its run staged with what came of it: landed, held for the review card, refused (with why) or dropped (with why: its run failed, a revision replaced its run, the turn went on without its run, or the turn's run ended). A tool call's card shows the tool's name and its arguments on one line, with how it stands in words and in its arrow's colour: **running…**, **done** with its time, **failed**, or **no result** when its agent stopped first; open it for its arguments laid out and its result, as the agent was sent it. The turn's phases, a wait at a gate and anything that stopped the turn sit between the groups. The feed follows the newest unless you scroll up (**Follow the newest** takes you back); **Show the work of** shows one role's groups; **Cancel the turn** stops it while it can be stopped. After the turn, Live keeps showing that turn until the next one starts; nothing it shows reaches your character's screens, where the activity line stays as short as ever. **Settings → Agents → Budgets → Chats in the live view** (16) says how many chats' latest turns it keeps in memory.

The **final review** shows there too: the main agent's review of the narrator's prompt is a group of its own (*Stage 6: the final review*, or *The final review* on the fixed pipeline), with each edit tool it called, and a line after it says how the review ended: it *revised the narrator's prompt*, with each edit (the section, the line it changed and what it says now), it *left the prompt as the engine made it*, or it *failed*, with why and whether the prompt went unrevised or the turn stopped.

### Workspace

**GM → Workspace** is a file browser for everything the agents keep for this chat, and for your own files every chat shares: the list on one side, what you open on the other (in the narrow drawer, what you open takes the list's place, and **Back**, or Escape, returns to it). The list groups the files: this chat's **Notes**, **Learned** (learned/), **Transcripts** (transcripts/, folded until you open it) and the **Pins file** (.pins.json), then **Global (~/)**, your own workspace, each with its folders first, which fold too, and names in natural order (scene-9 before scene-10). Each file shows its size and chips for who wrote it (**You (GM)**, the agent by its name, or **The turn**), at which reply (**Before reply 1** for a note of yours from before the first reply, **This turn** for one the turn under way wrote; a global file is no reply's), and **System**, **Global**, **Pinned**, **Learned** or **Read-only**; hover a chip for what it means in full (a **System** chip says whose instructions the file holds).

**New note** asks where it goes, **This chat** or **Global (~/), every chat** (which puts ~/ before its path), and its path (folders and its name joined by /, such as notes/marcus.md, that no other file has, outside transcripts/; it starts in the folder of the note you have open), then writes the note empty and opens it. An open note is there to edit: the bar over it says whether it is **Saved** or **Not saved**, with **Revert** and **Save** (Ctrl+S or ⌘S saves too). Opening something else, New note, Back or Escape with changes not saved asks before discarding them. Under the text: **Pin** or **Unpin** (a pinned note goes with every agent turn; the agents read the rest when they need them; a system file has no Pin, since it is in every prompt already), **Rename or move** (a new name, folder or both, in the same path dialog) and **Delete**. Each change is one GM edit: the notice over the bar says what it did, with its **Undo** (a deleted note comes back with it), and one made while a turn runs waits for the turn. A note you create while a reply is being written opens as the empty note it will be: you can write and save its text meanwhile, and its other actions wait until the reply is in and the list holds it. The notes follow the story: a swipe from before a note was written lacks it. A note an agent of a turn writes (on a chat whose engine is **Agents**) is part of that turn: it lands when the turn commits, listed with the role that wrote it and its reply, and a Re-run or a swipe of that reply takes it back; while the turn runs, its agents (the main agent too) already see it in the workspace. With **Workspace notes** set to *Confirm* in **Settings → Changes from the reply**, it waits on the turn's review card for your **Apply** instead, and the agent is told so. The list, the session and the note you have open are read again when the chat moves on (a turn, an edit, an Undo), not each time you open the GM view.

**The main agent's session**, at the top of the list, opens in the same pane, all there: every turn it holds whole, each message a bubble (the engine's message to the main agent, or the player's words in a fixed pipeline's digest, on one side; the main agent's answer, or the reply, on the other), with the roles each turn ran and what landed, and the compaction summary; the pane says how much of it the main agent reads (its own messages and the summary, whole). **Transcripts** holds each turn's transcript (reply-0004.md is the transcript of the fourth reply on the chat): everything that turn's agents did, as Live showed it. A transcript opens read-only, with nothing to edit, pin, move or delete; it follows the story as notes do (a swipe has its own), is never pinned, and every agent can read it. Every turn keeps its transcript unless you say otherwise in **Settings → Agents → Retention** (see [Retention](#retention)): there you can keep only each chat's latest turns' transcripts, or only those younger than an age, and after each turn the older ones leave the list. The **Pins file** says which files each role is shown first, and how much of them; you edit it like a note, but it is never pinned itself.

Every agent reads every file, the transcripts and your global ones included, whatever perspective it works from (**Settings → Agents → Context**): no file is kept from an agent, as nothing in the world is (their tools read the world with every secret marked).

**System instructions** are files of their own: **AGENTS.md**, which every agent is told, and **roles/<role>.md**, which one agent alone is told (roles/main.md the main agent, roles/assistant.md the GM assistant, and a turn role by its id, such as roles/director.md or roles/minds.md). Each goes right after that agent's system prompt, your global ones first and then this chat's, AGENTS.md before the role's own, so this chat's have the last word. None ships: write the ones you want, at either level, as any note. **Settings → Agents → System instructions** says how much of a prompt they may take together.

Notes under **learned/** are the learned notes: what the agents keep across the story, which a swipe, a Re-run or an Undo never changes and a Branch takes along as they stand. They are listed, read and pinned like the others, under **Learned**, but a change to one lands at once, with no Undo (so **Delete** asks first). Notes under **Global (~/)** are yours across every chat: each chat of yours reads the same ones, and no swipe, Branch or Undo changes them; a change to one lands at once too, with no Undo, and **Delete** asks first. A file stays in its part: none moves between the story's notes, learned/ and ~/, so write it at its new path and delete the old one.

Opened from its row at the top of the list, **The main agent's session** shows, for a chat whose turns a main agent decides (**Settings → Agents → Pipeline**), the session it keeps from turn to turn at the reply you're on: how many turns it holds whole and about how many tokens, and whether a compaction summary stands in for its older turns (**The summary** opens it). **Roll back the latest compaction** has its next turn read the turns that summary replaced again; the page then says the rollback waits for the next agent turn, whose record carries it (its row says **Rollback waits**), so no turn already played changes. With no compaction to roll back, it says so, as it does once a rollback has brought the turns back: that is no compaction to undo.

### A character's sheet

A person in **World** opens their **character sheet**, a page made for running them; anything else opens its entity page (below). It has a back button to **World**, and Escape goes back too. At the top, badges say whether they are your character, their level on the minds ladder (**Live**, **Near** and so on), their tier, a life state other than alive, their tier in your character's party, whether they live their own day and whether they're secret or known to some. Then, in order:

- **Now**: where they are, as the whole path ("Port Alder › Harbor Street › 114 Harbor Street"); what they're doing and until when; what holds them (a car, say), with **Take out**; and what the story holds them to, with until when. **Move them…** opens the place picker (see [Picking a place, a bed or a list of words](#picking-a-place-a-bed-or-a-list-of-words)), with **Their home** and **Where** *your character* **is** offered first. **Bring to** *your character*'s **place** does it in one tap. **In** *your character*'s **party** picks their tier there: **Core** and **Companion** go wherever your character goes (Go to, driving, a move the reply tells, your character's own routine in a skip), a companion unless their own shift, appointment or plan is due then; **Hired** and **Ally** come only when taken along; **Not in the party** takes them out. A badge at the top says their tier ("Companion in Jordan Ellis's party").
- **Where they live**: **Home**, through the place picker, and **Sleeps**, through the bed picker; each shows as its path.
- **Mind**: their level and what the GM means them to do, with **Open their mind**, their page in **Minds**.
- **Routine**: what their routine has them doing now and next in today's laid day, with **Open their routine**, their page in **Routines**. When that isn't what they're doing, a line under it says why: their routine is context only (informative) or switched off, so it never moves them; the story holds them (named); or they simply aren't where it has them yet.
- **Things**: **Outfit**, **Carried** and **Wardrobe** first, then **At home**, **Elsewhere**, **Lent out** and **Holding for others**. Each row opens its thing page, and its menu has **Take off**, **Wear** or **Put it away**, each one GM edit with its Undo.
- **Money**: their cash or wealth and how many accounts they hold, with **Open their money**, their hub in **Money**.
- **Relations**: each in the world's own words ("Regard → Priya Nair: warm", "Member of → Rosie's crew: Role: owner, Rank: 1"), never the numbers behind them; the party and what holds them aren't repeated, since **Now** shows them. A relation's edit button loads it into **Add or change a relation**, which shows a regard's value now with its word ("Now 32, warm") above the fields it rests on, as on an entity's page.
- **Simulation**, **Life**, **Classes and tags** and **Remove from the world**, as on an entity's page.
- **All fields**, folded: every other field under a plain name, each with its edit button. Place, home and bed aren't repeated there.

Their pages in **Minds** and **Routines** have **Character sheet** at the top, which brings you back here. Every change on the sheet is one edit with its own **Undo**, like any GM edit.

### An entity's page

A group, an account or any other record that isn't a person or a thing opens its entity page (a thing, a vehicle included, opens its thing page in [Things in GM mode](#things-in-gm-mode)). It has a back button to **World**, and Escape goes back too. Its **Fields** show what its kind defines, with each number's range: a lock marks a field never offered to a model, and a value worked out from others ("Worked out from other values") is shown but not edited. Tap a field's edit button to change it, with **Save** or **Cancel**: a choice or a reference to someone or somewhere gives a picker, and money is typed in the world's currency. **Add to a value** adds to a number, an amount or a duration (a minus takes away, and the result stays inside the field's range: adding 50 to an energy of 80 gives 100). Below come:

- its designed sheet (as **Play → Me → You** shows yours);
- **Where it is**: where it is now, with **Bring to** *your character*'s **place** in one tap; **Move to a place**, whose search box narrows the places as you type; for a person, **In** *your character*'s **party**, their tier there or **Not in the party**, as on a character's sheet (a core member or a companion comes along on every move your character makes: Go to, driving, a move the reply tells, and your character's own routine, schedule and trips in a skip, unless the same reply sends them elsewhere); an account can also **Correct the balance**, with **Add** and **Take**. Each of these is an edit with its own **Undo**;
- **Relations**: each relation's edit button loads it into **Add or change a relation**, where **Add or update** saves it with its fields, each checked against its range, and its remove button removes it;
- **Simulation**: its tier, whether it lives its own day, and each domain's level, or *From the tier*;
- **Life**: its life state and who learns of a change, where **Also tell** picks people and each **Don't tell** *name* button takes one off again;
- **Classes and tags**: its value of each of the world's classifications, the ones players can't see included, and its tags; **Save** sets them for this chat.

**Remove from the world**, at the bottom, takes an entity out of the story without deleting it: it disappears from Now, People, the Actions sheet, what the narrator is told, schedules and the money processes, and its relations, and everyone's relations to it, go. Whatever it holds goes with it. No rule, schedule or zone picks it out any more, it no longer counts in its group's regard, and a removed group's schedules stop moving its members. **Restore** brings it back where it was, without the relations it lost (undo the removal instead to get those back too); someone who lives off-screen picks up from then, without catching up on the time they were away. Your own character can't be removed.

Every change you save is checked first: a value outside its range, a choice that isn't one of its values or a relation that doesn't fit is refused with the reason, and nothing changes (only **Add** keeps its result inside the range instead). Once a change is saved, a notice above GM mode's tabs says what it did, in a sentence: who, what the setting means and the value in words, a place with the places around it and a thing with where it is ("Marcus Delgado now sleeps at Alder Automotive (Lakeshore County › Port Alder)", "3 minds are now pinned at Near fidelity"), with **Undo**, which takes all of it back; it stays until you dismiss it or make another change, or until the chat moves on. A save is one edit, however many things it changes: a **GM edit** chip appears on the latest reply with the same **Undo**, and swipes and Re-runs treat it like a ✎ fix (see [Re-run and Re-narrate](#re-run-and-re-narrate)). The chip says only what your character could know: the change itself when your character sees it, else who it was about if your character knows them ("About Sam Reyes"), and never where they went or what was set; with no one your character knows, it says the change happened behind the scenes. Your edits win over the story. Saved while a reply is streaming, an edit lands when the reply finishes, over whatever the reply changed: someone you moved is where you put them, wherever the reply took them, and a part of the edit the reply already did (a relation it ended too) is simply passed over. A reply written before your edit never undoes it, even when it is read again later (a **Re-read edit**, a **Retry read**, a swipe): the edit is made again after that read. Replies written after your edit carry the story on from it.

A classification or tag you set here holds in this chat only, over what the world says: rules, schedules and **Known by** selectors match it from then on, and an Upgrade keeps it unless you pick the revision's. A classification players can't see stays out of Play, and tags never show there.

### Things in GM mode

**GM → Things** shows the chat's things as they stand now, secrets included, behind the gate. **Search things** finds a thing by the words the story uses for it. Four views:

- **Here**: where your character is. **In the room** lists the furniture, built-in things and vehicles (with what's in them), **Lying here** what lies loose, and **On the people here** what each person has, with whose it is and why they have it.
- **By place**: every place as a tree, what lies there and what holds what, each row with its size and whose it is.
- **By owner**: each owner's things on them, at home and elsewhere; **Nobody's** holds what no one owns.
- **Tidy up**: things that need a look, each as a sentence ("Refrigerator is on Jordan Ellis: too big to carry (Furniture; a person carries up to Armful). It was bought: put it at 114 Harbor Street, where Jordan Ellis lives?"). It finds a thing **too big to carry** on someone; one **in two places at once** (lying somewhere and inside something); **an owner a handover changed** (a reply read before this build made a thing handed over the receiver's); things that may be **the same thing** twice; a thing made in play whose **size** nothing says; a thing **worn but lying elsewhere** (its fix takes it off them); and a Gear or Place line **dropped by the final review** (the review took it out or rewrote it), with the things it names and no fix. **Fix** makes the one edit that fixes it, or pick among the fixes offered; **Leave it** puts it aside for this chat (**Show N left** brings those back, **Bring it back** one); **Ask the Curator** sends the sentence to the GM assistant and opens it. **Findings per run** says how many a run shows (50 by default). The first kind and a thing worn elsewhere are fixed on their own every turn (see [Things: who has what, and whose](#things-who-has-what-and-whose)).

Under Tidy up, **Keep in the world** lists what this chat made in play that the world doesn't have, things and places (**Made in play**): pick the ones to keep, and **Keep** copies them into the world's draft, each under its own id, so an Upgrade later treats them as the world's. A thing whose holder or place the draft lacks goes into the nearest place it has; a place inside one the draft lacks is skipped unless you keep that one too. What was kept, skipped and why shows after.

**A thing's page** (from any view, or from **World**) says where it is and why whoever has it has it, with **Move to…** and **Put in…**; **Hand to…**, as **Holding** (it stays whose it is), **Gift** or **Loan** until a time, or **Return it to** its owner; **Kept at…** and **Put it away**, which takes it there; **Whose**, with **Make it theirs…**; **Who may use it**, each with why ("The Kowalczyks · members"), each grant with ✕ and **Let someone use it…**; **What it is** (its name, the names the story may call it by, its description and size, and **Sleep in or on** and **Consumable**); **Holds**, with **+ Add inside**; **Secrecy**, with **Reveal to** *your character*; its **History** in this chat (each change, at which turn and by whom: the story, you, a fix or an agent); every field and relation under **Advanced**; and **Remove from the world**. Each control is one GM edit with its **Undo**, and its notice says what it did. A thing's page never shows a tier, a life or simulation levels.

### Everyone else's money

**Money** shows anyone's money: pick a person from **Whose money**, a list that gives each one's Money level under their name (with a search box when there are more than 8), and their money opens under it at their own Money level, in the segments yours has in Play, with the GM's controls in place of the player's actions. An organization's money is on its page in [GM → Orgs](#gm--orgs), under **Money**, with the same controls. Its pages open over the hub, each with a back button, and Escape goes back too. On an account's page, **Add money** and **Take money** make a correction against an outside account, so the books still balance; **Status** sets it to open, frozen, collections, charged off, closed, paid off or matured; the **Autopay** row sets how it pays itself, from its debtor's own accounts whichever party's page it was opened from; **Account fields** holds every field the account declares, each with its edit button; and **Close** closes an account that holds and owes nothing (its cards are cancelled), and otherwise says why it can't. A hold's row opens its **Release** page, to release it early, and a card's page has its **Card state** (active, frozen, lost or cancelled) and **Card fields**. A standing payment's page can edit it (its name too), set its status (paused, ended and so on) or delete it, and **New standing payment or one-off** adds one. A job can be ended (**End the job**) or edited, and **Add a job**, under **Income**, gives the person a new one, from one of the world's businesses picked by name or **An outside employer (type the name)**. An invoice's state can be set, and a holding's price too (**Set its price**, for securities, funds, crypto and other assets: an option follows its underlying). Where a holding's payouts, a property's rent or an owner's share of a business's profit go can be set for the person at any level, and **Add a property** gives them one of their own at a value. **Summary** ends with **Their organizations**, the businesses they hold a role on and their household, each opening it in GM → Orgs; under **In & out**, their **Household** row opens their household there, or reads **Not in a household** and opens GM → Orgs, where you add them to one. A person whose accounts haven't been opened yet shows the balances the world gives them to start with. None of this reaches your chat: other people's paydays and bills never appear in your chips, Journal or Montage, and their declines never stop your timelapse.

### GM → Orgs

**Orgs** lists every organization in the world, the party first, under **Search organizations**, with a chip for each type it has (companies, households, groups, trusts, the party) and a person to narrow it to those they're in. Each opens the same page Play's Orgs draws, as the GM sees it: every member named, every tab built, secret ones included, with the GM's controls on it. Each save is one edit, with its **GM edit** chip and **Undo**:

- **People**: its members by **Member of**, each opening their sheet (their title, position, rank and since; **Remove** them), and **Add a member**; a company's or a trust's owners (their role, share, units and class), with **Add an owner**; its jobs, each with its fields, title and position, **End** it, and **Add a job**. A person's sheet from Play's People shows every way they belong there, each edited in place: their member link, their job, their owner row and a delegation of theirs.
- **Rules**: **Add position**, **Edit** and **Remove** a position; **Delegate…**, a delegation's **Edit** and **Revoke**, with its powers, limits, until when and who granted it (the GM's delegation asks for no power); **Set dues**.
- **Money**, **Property**, **Ownership** and **Taxes**: as GM → Money draws a person's money, with the GM's edits; **Property** also sets a place's keys (item ids or item types, between commas). **Taxes** shows for a group or a trust from Money 3 too, and adds **GM: tax-exempt status**: one of the world's statuses ("Charity or nonprofit (501(c)(3))", "Church or religious organization (501(c)(3))", "Social club (501(c)(7))") or **Not exempt**, and **Set the status** (never on a household or the party); its Taxes then reads **Exempt as** the status. Where a corporation may file a consolidated return, **GM: file a consolidated return** (or **GM: stop filing one**) changes the election. A C corporation's **Ownership** has the GM's own **GM: list on the market** (once listed, **GM: offer more units**) and **GM: take it off the market**, each one edit with its Undo, no one's powers or votes asked.
- **Decisions**: as Play's, with the GM's controls: a vote for any voter, **Close it now** on the votes cast or **Withdraw** a motion, **Approve** or **Deny** any request, and how its decisions are made.
- **Who may…**: pick a person, a power, or both: the answer is yes with the chain it rests on ("Helen Kowalczyk holds Head at The Kowalczyks", "Head holds every power"), or no with why ("No owner role, position, delegation or member's default at Alder Automotive gives them that."); with a power alone, everyone who holds it.
- **History**: its own History, every line and everyone in it named, then this chat's edits in force that name it, newest first.
- **Fields**: every field it declares, each with its **✎**.

Play's editors open the GM's own here: a member's sheet opens their every way of belonging, and a position, a delegation or the dues opens its GM sheet. What you change applies as the world's, not as anyone's act: it needs no power and writes no one's name on its History line.

### This chat

**This chat** is a list of pages, each with a back button to the list:

- **Rules**: **Engine** (below), **Realism** (see [Promote and demote](#promote-and-demote)), **Checks** (see [New chats and this chat](#new-chats-and-this-chat) and [Inferred checks](#inferred-checks)), **Time** (see [Time controls](#time-controls)), **Sections** (see [What the narrator is fed](#what-the-narrator-is-fed)), **Knowledge** (below), **Things** (below), **Money** (below) and **Pins and upgrade** (see [Revisions and Upgrade](#revisions-and-upgrade)). These change the chat you have open; Settings holds the defaults for new chats.
- **Engine**, under **Rules**, picks which engine plays this chat's turns: **Classic steps** (every chat's until you switch it) or **Agents**, whose turns are worked out by agents as **Settings → Agents**, **Roles** and **Main agent** set them, with the same gates, cards and chips; on **Agents** every reading of a reply is the agents', a Re-narrate's, a Continue's and a Re-read edit's included. A switch applies from the next turn: earlier turns stay as they were played, and switching back to classic plays on from the same story. Switching drops a turn the engine has already prepared for the next reply, which the engine you switch to works out again. While a turn waits on you at a gate (the action or the outline shown before playing, a question asked first, or the review card until the reply is written), the switch waits for it and says what waits: answer or cancel it first, since a turn always finishes on the engine it started on, so nothing it kept for you, the changes you approved among it, is lost. Like **Knowledge**, it is kept with the chat: no Undo, and a Branch keeps it.
- **Knowledge**, under **Rules**, has two groups. **Knowledge tracking**: switch on **This chat has its own setting**, then **Track who knows what in this chat**; off, the chat follows your **Settings → Minds → Knowledge**. **What the narrator knows**: switch on **This chat has its own choice**, then pick the narrator's knowledge and secrets (see [What the narrator is fed](#what-the-narrator-is-fed)); off, the chat follows the world's default (**Build → World**), else your **Settings → Narrator**, and the page says which. A save applies from the next turn. It is kept with the chat, not its story: no turn already played changes, there is no Undo, and a Branch keeps it.
- **Things**, under **Rules**, is this chat's **Tidy up**: whether impossible holds are tidied every turn (see [Things: who has what, and whose](#things-who-has-what-and-whose)). Switch on **This chat has its own setting**, then **Tidy impossible holds every turn in this chat**; off, the chat follows the world's **Tidy impossible holds every turn** (**Build → Packs → gear → Tidy up**), and the page says whether that is on. A save applies from the next turn. It is kept with the chat, not its story: no turn already played changes, there is no Undo, and a Branch keeps it.
- **Money**, under **Rules**, is **Cash in this chat**: **As the world** (the world's rule, **Build → Economy → Cash**), **Needs a machine** (a cash machine or a branch for cash in and out) or **Anywhere**. It is one GM edit, with its Undo, and applies from now on; a chat that never sets it plays by the world's rule, as before.
- **Behind the curtain**: **Edits**, the corrections in force on this version of the story, worded in full, newest first, and any change that didn't apply after a reply, with the exact reason. Each edit on the latest reply has **Undo**, as its chip in the chat does. It is behind the gate.
- **Inspector**: **Context**, what each reply was given, and the call meter. See [The Context inspector](#the-context-inspector).

### What the narrator is fed

Each section the narrator reads (Outcome, Scene, People and the rest) has settings. The world sets them in **Build → Sections**, and **GM → This chat → Sections** sets them for the chat you have open. Each section's row says, in the form's own words, whether it's in the prompt, whose knowledge it tells, its caps and whether it takes sources it picked, and whether those settings are this chat's or the world's. Tap a section and its settings open as a page of their own: change what you like and tap **Save for this chat**, or **Use the world's** to go back to the world's. The chat's settings stand in place of the world's for that section, whole: the form opens on the settings in force, and what you save is all of them, so you can lift a cap or a list of sources the world set. Each save is a GM edit with its own **Undo**: a notice says what it set ("In this chat, the Outline section tells what your character knows, cut to at most 400 tokens"), with **Undo**, as any GM edit's does.

**What it holds now.** Each section's page opens with what that section would give the narrator if the next turn played now: its text exactly as the narrator reads it, its opening line about what it is included, with its size in tokens against the section budget, and any fact your character doesn't know shown with its mark. A section too big for what is left of the budget keeps its first lines and says how many it left out from its end; one that gives nothing says why: switched off, nothing to tell right now, or not even its first line fits what is left of the budget (the Minds section has its own). **Preview all**, at the top of the list, shows every section that way, in the order the prompt holds them. The preview is built the way a turn builds the sections, from the chat as it stands, with no model call, so it changes as soon as you save a setting. What the turn writes itself comes only when it plays: the Outcome of your action, a time skip's Montage, the Outline, and how long the turn took in Scene; the preview says so in those sections.

- **In the prompt**: off leaves the section out.
- **Cap its lines**, then **At most lines** (off is no cap): only its first lines go in, the background hints included.
- **Cap its tokens**, then **At most tokens** (off is no cap): lines past it are cut from the end.
- **What it tells** (**Sources** in **Build → Sections**, by name): a box for each kind of fact the packs tell, in plain words ("What the people here wear and carry"), its own marked **Its own** and ticked unless the section picked others. Ticking exactly its own is the plain setting, so it also takes what a pack adds to it later; tick others, or untick some, and it takes just those. A section with sources of its own needs at least one ticked, or switch it out of the prompt. Outline and Montage, which the turn writes, have none, so they save their other settings with nothing ticked. By name, `location`, `calendar` and `weather` fill Scene, `people`, `routines` and `present_gear` People, `stance` and `cues` Stance, `conditions` Outcome, and `money`, `gear`, `vehicles`, `quest`, `place`, `place_lore`, `property` and `minds` their own sections. A custom section with sources, say a **Weather** section with `weather`, gets those lines too.
- **Whose knowledge**:
  - **The narrator's knowledge** (the default): what the narrator's knowledge, below, says: everything, with what your character doesn't know marked, under **Everyone in the scene**; what your character knows under **Your character's only**.
  - **What your character knows**: only what your character knows.
  - **Also what the people with your character know**: facts your character doesn't know but someone with them does, such as a person in the room only Maya knows is there. Never a secret (something only the GM knows), nor a quest's hidden truth: only **Everything** tells those.
  - **Everything, with what your character doesn't know marked**: every fact, secrets included.

  A fact your character doesn't know is marked for the narrator ("[unknown to you; known to Marcus]", "[unknown to you; GM only]"), and the narrator's rules, the same on every turn of every world chat, tell it, under your own instructions and the story, what a mark means: a fact your character doesn't know, and who does. The narrator keeps every character consistent with what they know, so no one says, acts on or is narrated as knowing a marked fact they don't until the story reveals it to them or you make it known. Unless your own instructions say otherwise, the reply doesn't tell you a marked fact: it comes out only through what the story has already established or what your character does or finds, and until then marked facts are for steering the story and the people who know them. The engine's own screens never show them: Play, chips, the Journal and the Context panel's sizes show only what your character knows. But marked facts are in the prompt, so anything in Lumiverse that shows the prompt or the engine's book shows them too; choosing a wider scope is choosing to put them there.

  What each section adds: People, someone here your character can't see (and, from what people carry, a thing on them he can't see); Scene, the place around his and nearby exits he doesn't know; Stance, people's private agendas; Money, the money of the people with him; Gear, a thing on him he doesn't know of; Vehicles, cars at his site he can't see; Quest, quests and secrets he doesn't know; Place, hidden things here, hidden ways out and, under **Everything**, who is next door; Property, other people's property.

**The narrator's knowledge.** How much the narrator knows has two parts. **Whose knowledge the narrator gets**: **Everyone in the scene** (the default), every fact, with what your character doesn't know marked for the narrator; or **Your character's only**, what your character knows. **Secrets**: **Marked** (the default), a line that would give away a secret you haven't found goes in marked as a secret kept from your character; or **Left out**, such a line is dropped. A section whose **Whose knowledge** is set keeps its own; one left on **The narrator's knowledge** takes it. The default is yours, in **Settings → Narrator**, which holds these two choices. How a secret is marked is yours to word too, in **Settings → Agents → Prompts → Final output** under **Secret marks** (see [Your own prompts](#your-own-prompts)): **Known to some**, **Kept from some**, **Known to some, kept from others**, **The word for everyone**, and **What a mark means**, the sentence in the narrator's rules that explains them; Settings → Narrator points there. A world sets its own knowledge in **Build → World** (**The narrator's knowledge**, with **This world sets what the narrator knows** on), which beats yours in its chats, and **GM → This chat → Knowledge** sets one chat's, which beats both. A change applies from the chat's next turn; a reply already prepared is prepared again.

**Out-of-play actions.** A money, organization or Gear → Manage action you tap with no words is done at once, off screen, and the next turn's Outcome opens with one line for each one you chose to tell ("Off screen before this turn, you saw to this yourself: …"), so the narrator speaks of it as done rather than playing it out. **Settings → Narrator → Out-of-play actions** sets which are told by default: a **Tell** switch per group (payments to others, credit, property, business, staff, members and structure on; your own accounts, investing, governance and things off), **Tell payments to others from** (whole units of the chat's money; 0, the default, tells every one) and **Reset to defaults**. Each action's chip has **Tell** / **Don't tell** until the next turn, and what it says is fixed when you do it, so a later change here leaves it alone. Only the last thing you did can change: once you do something after it (resume a payment you paused, say), the earlier chip keeps its **Undo** alone and says why, and its **Tell** / **Don't tell** comes back if you undo the later one. Nor does it change while a turn is under way, which tells it as it stood. A Re-narrate tells it as the turn did, and an Undo before the turn leaves no line. The line's words are **Done off screen** on **Settings → Agents → Prompts → Final output**.

Two sections are new, both on unless the world or the GM switches them off, and ranked last, so a small budget leaves them out first. A world that lists its own sections gets them only when it adds them.

- **Place**: the place's description (only when your character knows the place), whose the place is when your character knows them both ("12 Birch Lane is Marcus Delgado's."), every room inside it and every way out (rooms and spots the story made count too), and the things there your character can see (only things, never a business, a bill, a job or a group that the world gives a place, and not vehicles, which have their own section): **In the room**, the furniture and fittings, grouped by whose they are ("In the room: Refrigerator, Sofa (the household's)."); **Lying here**, every loose thing, one line each; and **In** each container here your character may use, what's in it ("In Refrigerator: Milk, Beer ×6."). Each comes with whose it is when he knows (one nobody owns reads as whoever's it plausibly is), how many, its condition (from Gear 3), any other field its kind has that he may see, and its description; then the lore about it, every entry whole: each lore entry, your World Info included, that links the place or a place it's inside, nearest first, as your character may read it (a secret one only once its gate holds). So a room whose lore lists its whole inventory reaches the narrator item by item. An entry of your World Info told here is left as it is: it still reaches the narrator by its own keywords and rules, so the narrator may read it twice, and its secret lines are told here only as any secret is. Where your character is somewhere without leave (see [Entry and Property](#entry-and-property)), its **trespass** line says so ("You're in Marcus Delgado's house without leave.").
- **Property**: the property your character holds a part in and knows of (his own, or his family's), where it is, and who holds it, each with their role and share. It is told as his ("yours") only when he owns it outright or jointly; when he only uses, keeps or benefits from it (an authorized user, a custodian, a trustee, a beneficiary), its holders come first and then his role, so the narrator never takes it for his. A holder he doesn't know of reads "someone".

People also tells what each person with your character visibly wears and carries (never what is in a bag or a pocket), every thing of it, with whose it is when it isn't theirs ("Danny Okafor carries Toolbox (yours)."). The **Gear** section sorts your character's things by who has them, never by whose they are, one line each with its details (a count, its condition from Gear 3, its description): **On you** (worn, in a pocket, in hand, or what it's in: "On you: Debit card (in Wallet): …"), **Holding for** someone (theirs, with why you have it), **Borrowed from** someone (until when, or overdue), **Lent out**, and **Not with you**: your own everyday things that aren't with you now, with where ("Not with you: Smartphone (at 114 Harbor Street)."), up to the world's *Not with you* lines (3; **Build → Sections → Gear**). At Gear 1 it lists only things in play: the world's notable ones and anything that changed hands or the story brought in. The guidance at the head of Gear, Place and People tells the narrator that having a thing isn't owning it, that a thing not with you is elsewhere and used only once fetched, and that furniture stays put. Each is a text you can reword, in **Settings → Agents → Prompts → Final output**.

## Swipes, edits and branches

A world chat survives all the ways people mess with a chat. Here is exactly what each one does.

### Re-run and Re-narrate

There are two ways to retry a reply:

- **Re-run** plays the turn again with a **fresh roll**. Your typed text keeps the reading it was given, but the engine draws new dice, so a failed check can succeed. This is the default.
- **Re-narrate** keeps everything the engine decided (the same dice, the same outcome, the same events) and only asks for **new prose**. Use it when you like what happened but not how it was told.

Which one a plain swipe uses is a setting (**Settings → Swipes**, under *Replies*: *Re-run (new roll)* or *Re-narrate (same outcome)*). Either way, the **Swipe as…** chip on a reply has **Re-run** and **Re-narrate** buttons for a single swipe. Anything you fixed by hand (✎) or changed in GM mode, a workspace note included, carries over to the new swipe either way, made again after everything the new reply tells, so it still says what you said: Re-narrate after the same outcome, and Re-run after the new turn and everything the new roll did. It carries what the swipe showing when you asked had: Lumiverse puts a regenerated reply after all its swipes, but one made while an earlier swipe shows is made from that swipe, keeps its fixes, and Undo takes each back on the new swipe as on the old. Cash you set is that amount whatever the new roll spent, and a quest you moved on with **Stage** moves on from wherever the new reply leaves it, or stays as it is when the new reply takes it there too. One that can't be made at all (the thing it changed was only in the old reply) is dropped with a **Not carried** note in the Journal, and a correction you undid stays undone. With Re-narrate, any detail you opened on the original reply carries over too, and anything that depended on results of the original narration is dropped with a note in the Journal.

A Re-narrate is sent the texts its turn was made with: your narrator rules and section guidance as they were then (**Settings → Agents → Prompts**), so only the prose changes. A Re-run is sent the texts of the reply it plays again, so it differs by its roll and not by an edit you made since. To see an edit of yours on an older turn, set **Settings → Swipes → Swipe with** to *My current texts*, which both then use. A turn from before LumiRPEngine kept its texts was made with the shipped ones.

### Your own prompts

**Settings → Agents → Prompts** holds every text LumiRPEngine sends to a model. It opens on its pages, grouped (**The narrator**, **Agents**, **Turn roles**), each with how many texts it has and how many are your own; a page with a text that needs a fix says so. **Find a text** searches all of them at once, by name, by any words in them (yours or the shipped ones) or by id, and lists what it finds by page; the chips under it show **All**, only **Yours**, or only those that **Need a fix** (a text of yours longer than **Longest text of your own** since you lowered it, or with a placeholder the text no longer has), each with its count. On a page, a heading with more than 12 texts is folded: **Show all** opens it, **Fold** closes it. Open a text to edit it: one is open at a time, and a text you've changed without saving keeps your words, marked **Not saved**, while you open another. **Compare with shipped**, under the text, shows what your text takes out of the shipped one and what it adds, line by line. Escape clears the search, or takes you from a page back to all of them.

**Final output** is what goes into the narrator's prompt: the narrator rules (where they come from, the order of authority, what a marked fact means, and what leads your preset's own rules), how a secret is marked (**Secret marks**: a secret known to some, kept from some, or both, the word for everyone, and **What a mark means**, the narrator rules' sentence that explains every form your marks take; the sections, the narrator rules and your World Info entries in the prompt all mark a secret in these words), the guidance that leads each section's lines, the line that tells the narrator what you did off screen before a turn (**Out-of-play actions** → **Done off screen**, for each action you chose to tell; it must keep `{{what}}`), and the line that ends the Scene with how long the turn took (**Elapsed time**: no time passed, how long it took, and how long from when to when where the Scene tells the time of day; an agent turn's director is told the same line). A wording of a secret's mark you set on Settings → Narrator before it moved here is among your texts here, as you wrote it; a Re-narrate or a Re-run of a turn from before then marks secrets in it too, as that turn did, and an earlier LumiRPEngine you go back to still finds your wording. **GM agent** holds the game master's assistant's own texts: its **Instructions**, the assistant's system prompt and role instructions, and **The conversation**, the note it is sent when older messages of a long conversation are left out (**History tokens**). Each text starts as the shipped one: open it, write your own and **Save**; it is sent from the next turn, or the assistant's next message, and **Reset to the default** brings the shipped text back. A text may use only its own placeholders, listed beside it (a section's guidance must keep `{{lines}}`, where its lines go; each form of a secret's mark keeps `{{text}}` and the names it gives, `{{known}}`, `{{kept}}` or both; **What a mark means** keeps `{{forms}}`, where every form of your mark goes; and the elapsed-time lines that tell how long keep `{{span}}`); anything else in double braces is flagged and can't be saved, and so is a text longer than **Longest text of your own**, or an empty secret's mark. A world that words a section its own way (**Build → Sections**) keeps its wording in its chats.

After **Main agent**, **Every agent** holds what any agent is told besides its own texts: its built-in **scratch_read** tool, the notes on tool results, tools offered as text, and how your system instructions (AGENTS.md and roles/<role>.md, see [Workspace](#workspace)) are framed, with what an agent is told when some of them didn't fit. It also holds what every agent with tools is told of them (the GM assistant, the main agent and each role alike): under **Tools**, what each tool does and what each of its arguments means, one text for every tool and every argument, so the page lists exactly the tools there are; under **Tool answers**, what a tool answers when a name finds no one ("No such person here: …"), when a name fits several, or when an argument is missing or not one the tool takes; and under **Lore index**, how the index of your lore entries is worded. These were on **GM agent** before; a text you wrote there is still yours. The pages after it are the agent turn's roles: **Every role** holds what every role is told besides its own texts (the perspective it works from, a hint in its system prompt, since its tools read everything, secrets marked, whatever its perspective; how your preset's guidance leads in; how its pinned files come; and how the sections it starts with are framed), and each role has a page of its own with its **System prompt**, its **Role instructions** and, for each of its tasks, the task's text and the description of its answer. While a chat is open, each role's text has **Test**: it runs the role once on that chat's latest exchange (its last reply and your message before it, as that turn left the story) with the text as you've written it, saved or not, one model call with no tools, and says **Passed** when the role's answer kept the form the engine reads (the role's contract), or, in words, how it didn't; a text with a problem can't be tested until you fix it. The role gets the task a turn would give it there: the intent reads your message, the read reads the reply, and a memory is the first one that reply would make. A task only a turn under way has (judging your approach, a puzzle's answer, a person's decision, a time skip) runs on the role's texts alone, and Test says so under **Left out of its task**, as it says what else your chat couldn't give (no reply yet, the step turned off, no one there to remember). Test changes nothing in the chat, and shows no part of the answer, which may hold what this page shouldn't; Diagnostics keeps the call, behind the GM curtain.

### Switching to an earlier swipe

Swipe back to an earlier version of a reply, or delete or add a swipe on an earlier message, and the turns after it are **automatically rebased**: the engine re-checks each of their results against the new situation. Something that no longer makes sense (an item you never bought, a payment under a different balance) is dropped, with a note. A purchase or payment is one unit, so the money and the item are kept or dropped together. So is a quest's step, with what it pays: a later turn's step is kept only where the quest still stands at the stage that step began from, so one the new version has already taken is dropped whole, and its rewards are never paid twice. Nothing pauses and nothing is erased.

A **Keep report (n)** chip lists what was dropped. For each entry you can **Convert** it (keep the effect as a fixed correction; it is your own adjustment, so it is kept even if it leaves an account short). It also lists anything that was done on a different branch of the story as "made on another branch", and shows converted entries as "kept".

Everything is reversible: switch back to the original swipe and the world is exactly as it was. Anything you do while you are on a different swipe, such as a fix, a chip confirmation or a new turn, counts only while you stay on that version of the story. A new turn on a rebased path builds on the rebased version.

### Editing the AI's reply

Fixing a typo, a name or a trimmed line is routine, and the world doesn't care. **When you edit an AI reply, the world stays as it was**: nothing pauses and nothing is re-read. If the edit changes what *happened*, tap **Re-read edit** on that reply and the engine re-reads the new text, replacing what it had read from the old one. A change you had already applied from the old read's chips is undone with it and offered again from the new text, if the new text still tells it, so nothing is charged twice. While the chat is paused, or when the message is a switched-away view, the read is refused without calling a model and the chips stay as they were; Keep or Discard, or switch back, first. Keeping is the default.

Re-read edit, Retry read and **Undo** run only on a reply that is on its own timeline. If you have swiped away from the timeline the reply was made on, they are refused and ask you to switch back first. One case can't be fixed by switching back: if an earlier message or swipe the reply depended on changed while the reply was written (deleted, or its text replaced), the reply was read without it, so Re-read edit, Retry read and Undo are always refused on it. If it is the latest reply, **Re-run** it to play the turn again; an older reply can't be changed this way.

### Editing an earlier message of yours

If you change what *you* wrote earlier in the chat, every turn after it was built on something that no longer exists, so the chat **pauses**: a **Paused** banner appears at the top of **Play → Now**, and a card under your edited message asks "Edited message: keep or discard later turns?" (the same card sits inside the banner):

- **Keep**: the later turns are re-checked against the edited message, as in the swipe case above, with a Keep report.
- **Discard**: the later turns' records are removed for good. Their messages stay in the chat as plain text, and the world forgets what happened in them. This cannot be restored, not even after a restart.

While the chat is paused, a new turn is written but gets no simulation (it is marked *degraded*), and fixes and confirmations are refused until you choose. If the edit lands while a reply is streaming, that reply is left unread. A swipe that finishes while the chat is paused carries none of the fixes and GM edits of the swipe it replaced: each gets a **Not carried** note in the Journal.

**Edit and Send** on an earlier message works in a world chat as a branch: Lumiverse makes a new chat from the edited message, and its reply is that chat's latest. In place, the reply would be written onto the older message after yours, which the engine can't prepare, because Lumiverse can start only the last reply again. So an in-place Edit and Send on an earlier message stops with **The engine didn't finish, so nothing was generated**, and nothing is written: your edit stays and pauses the chat as above. Instead, use Edit and Send as a branch (Lumiverse's setting to branch on Edit and Send), or regenerate the last reply.

### Continue

When you use Lumiverse's Continue on the latest reply, the engine reads only the new text and applies what it says, as one more step of that turn. Continue always continues the latest reply: when the engine has run first, it starts the Continue again with Lumiverse's own, which knows only the latest. Some Continues can't be read that way: a Continue on an older message (only possible through Lumiverse's API, and only where Lumiverse can't stop a reply), on a message the engine never simulated (a greeting), one you stopped half-way, or one where Lumiverse re-rendered the earlier text. These are accepted as they are, like an edit. The world stays as it was until you tap **Re-read edit** (or **Retry read** for a stopped Continue). Before the reply starts, a Continue shows the world as it was, because the engine learns which message you're continuing only when the reply ends.

### Undo, retry and what lapses

- **Undo** covers the latest reply. Earlier changes go through Keep or Discard. Each ✎ fix and GM edit on the latest reply has its own **Undo**, on its **Corrected** or **GM edit** chip (and, for a GM edit, in GM mode too).
- **Apply / No chips** from a reply expire when you send your next message, and nothing is applied.
- **Timelapse chips** (offers and Continue), **suggested choices** and **your other action** lapse at the next send as well.
- A reply that fails or comes back empty keeps the engine's work for its turn: **Retry the reply** (or Lumiverse's own Regenerate of that reply, or its Continue for a Continue) writes it again from that turn, and **Re-run the engine** runs the turn again first; a new reply after a reply that failed before writing anything goes out again when you press **Send** with nothing typed (see **The reply failed** in [Under each reply](#under-each-reply)). An empty reply the engine didn't prepare is *degraded* and shows **Retry**. The engine never regenerates on its own.

### Changes while a turn runs

A change you make while a reply is being written (a ✎ fix, a chip you answer, detail you open, a settings change for this chat, Keep or Discard) waits for that reply and lands once it is in, over whatever it changed; until then it shows as **pending**.

Before the reply starts, the engine works on the turn: it reads your message, plans what happens and writes the turn's sections. A change you make then (a ✎ fix, a GM edit, a chip, paying with a card, opening detail, an Undo; the GM assistant's changes and workspace saves too) waits for the turn, as it does during the reply: the reply starts as planned, and your change lands once the reply is in, or as soon as the turn stops without a reply (it failed, you cancelled it, or it waits on a question). A setting you save or a GM → This chat choice you change (the engine among them) applies from the next turn; the turn under way finishes as it started. Editing or deleting a message still stops the turn's reply: the chat says so (**The reply didn't start, so nothing was generated**), and **Retry** runs the engine again.

### Branches

A Lumiverse chat branch carries the world with it. Records that belong to the branch you're on apply; changes made on another branch stay stored but do nothing until you go back. When you delete a reply, the settings changes recorded on it (realism, time controls, Upgrade) move to the nearest earlier reply, and its other-branch changes are dropped with it.

## Time controls

Time is deterministic: nothing here needs the AI.

Every turn has an **elapsed time**. It is one of: the length of a Timelapse, the length of the action you took (a drive, a rest), or the **default tick** of the current scene type multiplied by the **time scale**. The **+n min** chip in the reply's receipt shows it, the scene card in **Play → Now** says how long the last turn took, and the narrator is told it in the Scene section: "No time passes this turn." while the clock is paused, "This turn took 20 min." where the world tells only the part of the day (Calendar 1), and from when to when where it tells the clock ("This turn took 30 min, from 6:15 AM to 6:45 AM."), so the Scene's time reads as when your turn's actions are done. These lines are yours to word, in **Settings → Agents → Prompts → Final output → Elapsed time**. The narrator is told to give no time of day or date that contradicts it, and to let any time the reply tells pass on from it.

Every text that tells a model the time of your turn tells the same clock as the Scene: your character's local time where the world shows time zones (Calendar 3), else the world's own. That holds for the narrator's Scene, a Timelapse's "Time passed" line in the Outcome, the facts the helper steps read, a step's `{{clock}}`, and an agent turn's director and read.

### Ticks, scale and pause

- **Tick per scene type.** The time a turn takes when nothing else sets it, before the time scale. The defaults are **conversation 2 minutes**, **action 1 minute**, **exploration 10 minutes** and **downtime 30 minutes**. Worlds can add scene types. A tick is a whole number of minutes: a fractional one, such as a world's half minute, plays as the nearest whole minute, and one under half a minute as no time at all (with **Use my settings** on, a world's own scene type that your settings don't list keeps the world's tick as written). The time scale can still make a turn's time a fraction of a minute ("This turn took 0.5 min."). The scene type is set by actions and by places, or by a ✎ fix.
- **Time scale.** A multiplier on default ticks only, from 0.25× to 10×, default 1×; a tick after scaling is never longer than a day. Action durations, travel times and Timelapses stay exact. When it isn't 1×, the status line, the pill and the scene card show it.
- **Clock pause.** While paused for the scene, no time elapses (neither ticks nor action durations) until you resume it or the scene changes. If a scene change started the clock again, **Pause clock** pauses it for the new scene. The status line and the pill show the pause icon, and the scene card says **Clock paused**. A Timelapse asks you to resume first. A time jump the narration describes still applies (see [When the story jumps ahead](#when-the-story-jumps-ahead)). Pause and resume with **Pause clock** / **Resume clock** in the World sheet or the pill's details, in the Actions sheet's header, on the input bar, or in **Play → Story → Calendar**.

Where to change them:

- **Settings → Time** fills in, for **new chats**, what a world's own time defaults leave out (always the time scale); with **Use my settings instead of a world's defaults for new chats** on, it replaces them.
- **GM → This chat → Time** changes **this chat**. **Play → Story → Calendar** has a button that takes you there.

Both have the same sections: **Default tick per scene type** (with **Time scale**), **Timelapse interrupts**, **Speed through** and **Montage and limits**; **Settings → Time** also has [Skips without asking](#skips-without-asking). A change saves as you make it; there is nothing to apply.

A change in a running chat applies from the next turn forward, is recorded like any other change, and respects swipes and branches. Earlier turns keep their recorded times. Changing Settings later leaves existing chats alone, except for **Skips without asking**.

### Skips without asking

**Settings → Time → Skips without asking** is how long a skip may run inside one turn before the engine asks you first. It covers a length of time you type ("for the next three hours", "OOC: skip ahead two hours") and a jump the reply tells ("three hours later"). It is a day by default; set it in minutes, from 0 (every such skip asks) up to 366 days, and the line under it reads the minutes back as a span ("That's 1 d"). A longer skip doesn't run: it becomes a chip that runs it as a Timelapse when you tap it (**Timelapse** for a skip you typed, **Run timelapse** for one the reply tells). An *until* you type ("until dinner", "until my next shift"), a day and time you name, a Timelapse you start yourself and a ✎ fix of the clock never ask. It is a setting of yours, not a chat's: every chat follows it from its next turn, and turns already played keep the time they took.

### Timelapse

A Timelapse skips ahead while the world keeps living: bills fall due, you commute to work, NPCs go about their days. Open it from the **Timelapse…** button in the input bar (it opens the Actions sheet on its **Timelapse** tab), from **Timelapse** in **Play → Now** and **Timelapse…** in **Play → Story → Calendar**, or from a chip. The sheet has a **For** row of duration buttons (with a **Custom** length in minutes, hours, days or weeks), an **Until** list, and **Interrupts for this run**, folded away until you open it.

**Follow my routine**, below the targets, says what your own character does while the time passes: on, they go about their usual day (work, meals, sleep, as their routine and schedules have it); off, they stay where the story has them while the world goes on. It starts at the world's default, which is off unless the world's **Routines and the story** says otherwise, and holds for that Timelapse only.

**Targets** are either a duration (minutes, hours, days, weeks) or *until* something: next morning or another part of the day, your next shift or commitment, arrival (when the current trip ends), a date and time, a quest reaching a stage, or "until something happens".

**Interrupts.** While the time passes, some things should stop it. Each kind has a policy: **stop**, **note** or **ignore**. The kinds are invitations, bills and declines, incidents, quest and rule triggers, NPC arrivals at your place, overdue loans, needs thresholds and weather alerts. The defaults:

| Policy | Kinds |
|---|---|
| **stop** | invitations, declines, incidents, quest triggers, arrivals |
| **note** | bills and paydays, rule triggers, overdue loans |
| **ignore** | needs thresholds and weather alerts |

The first *stop* you could see ends the Timelapse at the end of that time slot; something hidden from you never stops it, so a stop never gives a secret away. *Notes* go into the Montage. The same world and the same policy always stop in the same place, and you can override the policy for a single Timelapse in the sheet.

**The safety cap.** One Timelapse segment is limited, by default, to **600 agent-days** (the people who are simulated in detail, plus you, times the days) and **5,000 events**; you can lower either but not raise it. A longer request ends at the last whole time slot inside the cap, and a **Continue timelapse** chip carries on from there. Segments add up exactly as if you had done it in one go, as long as nothing else changes the world between them. One segment reaches at most 366 days ahead.

**NPC choices** during a Timelapse use the numbers only; the AI isn't called. A Timelapse that can't run at all (the clock is paused, its time has passed, or there is no upcoming shift or commitment to run to) simulates nothing, and the turn's changes say why ("Timelapse not run: …"). One whose time lies more than 366 days ahead runs at most 366 days and offers **Continue timelapse** for the rest.

### Montage

A Timelapse turn is narrated from a **Montage**: the key events the point-of-view character would know (about people and places you have met; the weather counts when it is your region's), plus totals (money in and out per account at your Money level, distance travelled, anything you can see that crossed a band, such as how someone feels or a need going from calm to frantic, and net changes to your own and your belongings' other numbers; a field shown in bands is told by its band, never its number). A field that changed several times in one batch, such as an hourly temperature, is listed once at its latest value. The narrator is told to narrate the interval from the Montage and not add events; when a short Timelapse has nothing for the Montage, the Montage is left out and the narrator is told only not to add events. The Outline never points the narrator at a section it doesn't have that turn. The Journal keeps the full list, with spans worded in days, hours or minutes. Under **Montage and limits** you can choose the **Montage granularity**: **Key events** (the default), **Per day** or **Per week**; it is shortened to fit the budget.

### Speed through and Timelapse offers

For long activities (sleep, a work shift, travel, waiting), the engine can skip the dull part. When an activity that has a policy has more time left than its threshold (**When longer than (min)**), one of two things happens, per activity type (**Speed through** in **Settings → Time**, or in **GM → This chat → Time** for this chat):

- **Offer a timelapse** (the default): the turn takes only the action's own time, and a chip offers a Timelapse to the end of the activity: the fast way through it. What your character does decides whether the activity goes on. Doing something else ends it, and nothing it would have met is met after: an action that sets what they're doing ("I get up", "I make breakfast"), or going somewhere. Talking doesn't: after "I go to bed", "Goodnight, Rosa" leaves them falling asleep, so a narrated night, any Timelapse or turns that reach its end still see it out.
- **Run it**: the turn runs that Timelapse itself, with your interrupt policy and a Montage.

When you say how long ("I sleep for eight hours", "I rest for three hours"), the activity lasts that long, and the same policy decides: a length under the threshold passes within the turn ("sleep for 20 minutes" is a 20-minute turn, "rest for an hour" an hour's), and a longer one takes the action's own few minutes, then offers (or runs) a Timelapse to its end. That Timelapse keeps you doing what you said until it ends: your routine doesn't get you up for dinner.

New settings give every listed activity a policy (offer, past 30 minutes), and a world can set its own for any activity; settings you saved with an earlier version keep the policies they had and aren't filled in. An activity without one (in a chat started before it had one, a world's own activity that the world leaves unset, or one your saved settings don't list) is never sped through, however long it lasts: when you said how long, the turn runs on to its end; otherwise the turn takes only the action's own time, and the rest of the activity passes in the turns that follow. Its row says *never sped through: its time passes in your turns* and shows no mode or threshold, and picking either gives it a policy.

When the narration implies an activity the engine hadn't seen start, you always get the offer chip, because the engine never starts a generation by itself. An activity with no more time left than the threshold gets no offer either: its time passes in your turns, the same way. The offer reads "Timelapse to 07:00 (8 h 55 min)?" (with the weekday when it is another day); the **Timelapse** button on it runs the Timelapse. A threshold can be anything from a minute to a week (1 to 10,080 on the stepper); a threshold outside that, such as a 0 an earlier version let you save, reads as the nearest end.

### When the story jumps ahead

If the reply says "three days later", the engine can read that as a time jump. The reading counts all the time the reply says passed in the turn, and only the part *beyond* what the turn already used is added: after a 15-minute action, "an hour later" adds 45 minutes, and a reply that tells less time than the action took adds none. It adds up to [Skips without asking](#skips-without-asking) at once (a day unless you change it), and runs the whole interval so the world catches up. A longer jump, or one that can't finish, becomes a chip offering to **Run timelapse** instead; one of more than 366 days runs at most 366 days at a time, each step ending with **Continue timelapse**, so no time the story tells is dropped. The time advance has its own mode (see [Extraction modes](#extraction-modes)): on **auto**, its starting mode, it applies by itself; on **confirm** a chip asks first, and **Apply** runs it. A fight doesn't stop one, nor does a paused clock: the fight and the pause hold the turn's own time, not time the reply says passed.

## Realism

Realism is how much of life the engine keeps track of. It is set **per domain**, from level 0 (off) to level 4 (simulation), and the story never needs it: quests and secrets work at any setting. Use as much or as little as your story enjoys.

| Domain | 1 Narrative | 2 Simple | 3 Detailed | 4 Simulation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Money** | A wealth band | Cash plus one account | Accounts, cards (with autopay), recurring income and bills, interest and APR | Statements, minimum payments, fees, credit, investments, withholding by tax bracket |
| **Gear** | Notable items: those the world marks notable, and anything found, bought, handed over, made or claimed; what people carry every day (a wallet, keys, a phone) | Items, containers, cash in a wallet | Worn and equipped slots, kits, condition; other people's things put away off screen | Wear per use, consumables |
| **Vehicles** | "Runs fine" | Fuel %, location, owner, your family's vehicles | Specs, consumption, odometer, loans and vehicles assigned or lent to you, home base | Maintenance intervals, breakdowns, registration and insurance, marine and off-road specifics |
| **Cast life** | Day-part routines, agendas | Commitments set the day | A day planner | Needs and drives, invitations, NPC-to-NPC encounters, knowledge spreading |
| **Relationships** | One band per pair | One number per pair | Several axes plus appraisal rules | Decay, history, group standing |
| **Space and travel** | Named places | Places, routes and travel times | Grids and positions | Terrain multipliers, mode-specific routes |
| **Calendar and time** | Day parts | Clock and dates | Recurrence and time zones | Holidays, school terms, exceptions |
| **Weather** | Season mood | Climate states | Regional climates | Microclimate effects on travel and rules |
| **Sheets and checks** | Bands as words | Stats plus one check | Skills and derived fields | The full sheet |

The time controls and Timelapse need Calendar at level 1 or higher. At level 1, Timelapse targets round to parts of the day.

**What the narrator is told of vehicles.** The Vehicles section lists what your character may use, each saying whose it is: his own at Vehicles 1, his family's from 2 (with their fuel and range), and those assigned or lent to him from 3 (a loan with when it's due). An owner he doesn't know of reads "someone". Each one comes with everything the world keeps of it that he may know, in its fields' own names: its class, make, model, year, colour and plate when it has them (the vehicle kind has a field for each, in **Build**), its odometer or engine hours and condition from Vehicles 3, when its next service is due from Vehicles 4, and its description; for one where he is, also who is aboard and its cargo by name (what a bag in it holds only when the vehicle or the bag is his, or he knows what is in it). A trailer has no tank, so it shows no fuel. Then other people's vehicles at the same site (a house with its garage and driveway, a marina; not the whole town) that he can see, every one, with whose they are and what anyone can see of them (class, make, model, colour, description).
