The ones he may use that are away from where he is come last, each named with where it is, so on a tight budget they are the first lines left out.

**Money detail.** Below the top Money level the money hub keeps the books simple, and at the top level it shows them all (see [Your money](#your-money)). Under any preset, a world can turn single money features on or off in **Build → Packs → economy** (**Money features** → **Feature switches**). Each runs from its usual level. Switched on, it runs from the lowest level it can (statements from Money 3, cash machines and daily card limits from Money 2), and the hub shows that feature's own detail there and nothing more of the full books. Switched off, it doesn't run at any level, and its detail is gone from the hub at the top level too: with interest off, nothing earns or charges interest and no account shows an Interest block. **Unemployment taxes on payroll (FUTA and SUTA)** is one: on at Money 4, it can run from Money 3, and switched off no paycheck adds to them.

### Presets

| Preset | Money | Gear | Vehicles | Cast life | Relationships | Space | Calendar | Weather | Sheets | Band caps (Leads / Supporting / Background) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Story** (the default) | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 1 | none; every domain level applies to everyone |
| **Grounded** | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 3 | 2 | 2 | no cap / no cap / 1 |
| **Simulation** | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 3 | 3 | 4 / 3 / 2 |
| **Custom** | any | any | any | any | any | any | any | any | any | any |

Moving any dial yourself makes the preset *Custom*.

### Player claims

When you type that your character does something with a thing the world doesn't have, as far as your character knows ("I light a cigarette with my Zippo", "I hand Rosie a coffee"), the **Player claims** setting decides what happens:

- **Make it real**: the thing is added where your action needs it, in your hands or where you are, and the action plays. With **Ask first above the world's claim cap** on, something whose price is over the world's claim cap asks first instead.
- **Ask first**: the turn waits for your answer, **Add** or **Not an action**, before anything is generated.
- **Hold me to the world**: the action doesn't play; your words play as plain roleplay, and a receipt says why.

Money you say you have and don't carry asks first, under any setting but **Hold me to the world**, and only for what you lack: saying you hand Danny $100 with $85 on you asks whether to add the other $15 to your cash and pay him the $100 in cash. Cash you carry is no claim: it's handed over as it is. Each preset carries its own setting: **Story** makes it real at any price, **Grounded** makes it real up to the claim cap, and **Simulation** asks first.

It is set in three places, each with the same control (the three modes, and the cap switch, which only Make it real uses):

- **Settings → Realism → Player claims**: yours, for chats you start from now on. Picking a preset there sets it, and changing it makes the preset *Custom*. Like the rest of Settings → Realism, it fills in for a world that sets none, or replaces the world's with **Use my settings instead of a world's defaults for new chats** on.
- **GM → This chat → Realism → Player claims**: the chat you have open, from its next turn; earlier turns keep what theirs did. A chat started before player claims existed plays by its world's, else by what its realism preset carries (a Simulation chat asks first, a Grounded one keeps the cap), and by Story's when its realism is *Custom*.
- **Build → Realism defaults → Player claims**: the world's own, for new chats. **This world sets what new chats make of them** switches it on, starting from what the world's realism preset plays; off, a new chat takes the player's own setting. Check flags one the engine can't read.

**The claim cap** is about the price of a meal: twice the typical price of a meal out in the world's categories table ($23 in Port Alder), or 25 in the world's currency when it has none. A world can set its own as **Claim cap** in **Build → Packs → economy**, typed in the world's currency; left empty, the field shows the default it uses. A cap that isn't a whole amount above zero gets a warning, and the engine then rounds a fraction or uses the default.

In GM mode, **World** marks each claimed thing **Claimed** and lists them under **Claimed by the player** with who holds each, so you can find one and remove it (see [The GM bar](#the-gm-bar)).

**Your own things kept elsewhere.** When the world has something of yours somewhere else ("I put on my clothes" while it keeps them at home), the action doesn't fail and stop the rest of your message. A thing close by (the same building or site, within the world's fetch minutes on foot) is fetched, whatever the setting, and the minutes are added to the action's time. One farther away goes by the same setting: Make it real brings that very thing to you, never a second one; Ask first asks, in words such as "Your phone is at 114 Harbor Street. Have it with you anyway?"; Hold me to the world leaves the action out with a receipt, your words play as plain roleplay, and the rest still plays. A thing too big to carry (a sofa, a fridge) never comes along. When the reply has you use something of yours that was elsewhere, it is brought along, with a **Brought along** receipt saying where it was.

### Finding things

Some things are hidden until you look: a world can mark a thing **Discoverable** (in its editor's **Visibility**). The **Search** action (typed as "I search the room", "I look through the drawer", "I open the chest") finds the hidden things lying loose where you stand, or, when you name a container, what is directly in it; anything in a container stays hidden until you search or open that container. What you find stays known. A container the world stocks also holds a few things of its kind the first time it's opened, beside what the world put in it. A world can make searching roll a check by giving its own **Search** action one.

**What can't be picked up.** **Take** (and **Wear or equip**) never offers or takes a vehicle, a part built into one or a thing fixed in place, whether you tap it, type it or the reply tells it: a part is a container whose catalog row in the world's Containers table is a fixed vehicle part (a truck bed, a glovebox, an ATV's racks, a boat's lockers), and a thing is fixed when its editor's **Fixed in place** is on (a mill stone, an installed relay). What lies in such a part can still be taken. Nor does anything bigger than a person carries (an **Armful** by default: a fridge, a sofa, a TV) end up on anyone: told into someone's hands, it lands where they stand instead, and bought, it goes to the buyer's home. See [Things: who has what, and whose](#things-who-has-what-and-whose).

**What the reply does with things.** The engine reads the reply for things handed over, picked up or found, put down, put into something or somewhere, shown in someone's hands, used up (eaten, drunk, spent), put on or taken off, lost or stolen, damaged or mended, and a stated change of owner. It finds the thing the reply's words mean (one your character can see first; **Which one?** when several fit), and when the story acts on a thing the world doesn't have yet, it makes it where the story has it, never a second of one that's there. Moves and handovers apply with an **Undo**; a change of owner and a loss ask first (each type's mode is set like the others, see [Extraction modes](#extraction-modes)). Something your character can't see is never named. On an agent chat, the **Curator** does this reading instead (see [Settings → Agents](#settings--agents)).

**Stacks.** Buying something you already carry, or being handed another of it, adds to the one you have (its quantity goes up) instead of making a second.

**Changes you apply from a chip** count at once: a quest that waits on the thing you just took moves on that same turn, and the world's rules about it run.

### Things: who has what, and whose

The engine keeps four facts about every thing apart, so none is guessed from another:

- **Where it is**: one place or one holder (a person, a bag, a car), never both.
- **Who has it**: whoever carries it, up the chain of what holds it.
- **Whose it is**: its owner, a person, a household, a business or a group. A thing nobody owns is taken to be whoever's it plausibly is: on someone, theirs; in a holder, the holder's owner's; at a place, the business trading there, else the household living there, else nobody's. GM mode shows that as "the household's (presumed)".
- **Who may use it**: its owner, anyone they hand it to or lend it to, anyone they let use their things, and, as the world says, their household, housemates, partners, staff at a business's own places and customers where a business is open (a booth, a washroom). This guides the actions and the narrator; it never stops the story: taking what isn't yours is recorded as taken.

**A handover isn't a gift.** When the reply has Danny toss you his truck keys so you can move the truck, you hold them for him: they are with you and still Danny's, and Me → Gear lists them under **Holding for others**. Only giving words ("keep it", "it's yours", a present), food or drink handed over to have, a sale or a trade change whose it is, and those ask first. Handing it back to its owner ends the holding.

**Sizes.** Every thing has a size: **Pocket**, **Hand**, **Armful**, **Heavy**, **Furniture** or **Built-in** (vehicles apart). A person carries up to Armful, nothing goes into something smaller, and a built-in thing moves only when you confirm it. A thing's size comes from the world's catalogs when it isn't set.

**Kept at.** Every thing has a place it's kept (its rest): one the world gives it, else on its owner for everyday things, where it stands for furniture, at the buyer's home for something bought, else where it was made. **Put away** takes it there. Off screen, other people's things lying about drift back to where they're kept: something of an NPC's, or their household's, left loose away from its rest, out of your sight and untouched for a day (the world's setting), goes back. Your own things never move by themselves, and nothing lent, held for someone or lost does.

**Everyday carry.** The first time the story meets someone, from Gear 1, they get what people carry every day if they don't have it yet: by default a wallet, keys and a phone, theirs and on them. The world can change who carries what, in **Build → Packs → gear → Everyday carry**.

**Places made in play.** When the story puts someone in a room, a spot or a new place the world doesn't have ("Jordan slides into the back booth at the Rusty Anchor"), the engine makes it under the place it's in, so the next "the booth" is that booth. A new venue in town asks first. A world sets how many a reply and a chat may make (**Build → World**, 2 and 64 by default); GM → Things → **Keep in the world** copies the ones you like into the world.

**Old chats, and every turn.** Every turn, before the story goes on, anything someone carries that is too big to carry (a fridge bought into Jordan's hands) goes to the buyer's home when it was bought, else to where they stand, as **Things tidied** with **Undo**; something worn but lying elsewhere is taken off its wearer the same way. The chip lists every move, a line each: past its first eight lines the rest fold under **+N more**, which opens them. A thing whose tidy you undid stays while its holder keeps it, and a finding you left in Tidy up is left alone. Everything else waits in GM → Things → **Tidy up**. An older chat's fridges and TVs, bought by an older build onto the buyer, go home on its next turn under one **Things tidied** chip. A world can turn this off (Build → Packs → gear → Tidy up), and a chat can have its own setting over the world's (GM → This chat → **Things**: *Tidy impossible holds every turn in this chat*).

### Tiers and bands

Not everyone needs to be simulated in depth. Every character has a **tier**, one of the world's own (a new world has **Leads**, **Supporting** and **Background**; an author can rename them, add more and reorder them in **Build → Tiers**). Each tier belongs to one of three **bands**, Leads, Supporting or Background, and each band has a cap per domain. A character's **effective level** is the lower of the domain level and its band's cap, unless you set a per-character override (**Level overrides** in the character's editor), which can never go above the domain level. Your own character is never capped. In Simulation, Leads run at level 4 while a Background shopkeeper runs at 2.

A tier can make its people **live their own day (agendas and plans) by default**, and a world names the tier **new people join** (by default its first Background tier): people the story introduces, and anything spawned without a tier. How many people the story may introduce in one chat is the world's **People replies may introduce in a chat**, in **Build → World**: 24 unless you set another, up to 500, and 0 for none.

People you mark as **Lives their own day (agendas and plans)** in their entity editor get goals and agendas that feed their choices, even on Story. They are consulted by the AI only on close calls or invitations. Everyone else follows schedules and seeded incidents. Only they live their plans (from Cast life 3): someone else with a mind still thinks, but their plans are never lived, never ask you along, and are dropped once their time has passed.

**Life and death.** Everyone is **Alive**, **Dying**, **Dead**, or in a state the world adds in **Build → World → Life states** (a label, whether someone in it still acts, and how the narrator reads it, such as "lies unconscious"); an entity's editor sets the state it starts in. Someone who doesn't act keeps no routine or day plan, makes no choices and takes no turn in a fight, and their body stays where it lies, still there to find, unless the story moves it. You learn of it by seeing it happen, by walking in on it, or by being told (the story, a rule or the GM); until then People, Now and the narrator show what you last knew. Rules change it with the `life` effect, and a world's combat can send the fallen into a state of its own (**down** → life state).
A death reaches the Journal on the turn it happened only if you saw it; otherwise it appears when you hear of it. Other people's comings and goings stay out of the Journal (Now and People show who is around), and a body goes wherever a reply carries it, as the story tells. An entity's editor can also say **Who knows how it starts**: everyone else takes it for alive until they find out.

### Promote and demote

A new chat starts from its world's own realism and time defaults, and **Settings → Realism** fills in only what a world leaves out; turn on **Use my settings instead of a world's defaults for new chats** there (it is also in **Settings → Time**) if you always want your own levels, say Story everywhere. A change in Settings saves at once and touches no running chat. To change the chat you have open, use **GM → This chat → Realism**: **Applies to** picks **Everyone** or just the **Leads**, **Supporting** or **Background** band (it names the world's tiers in that band), and each domain has a slider. Before the chat's first turn it shows the levels that turn will use. A single character's level is set on the character in the Build editors (**Level overrides**).

- **Promoting** switches processes on. Anything the character lacks is generated on demand, inside what is already known.
- **Demoting** switches processes off and trims what is shown. The data stays; nothing goes dormant, so promoting again never brings back stale detail. Money is conserved either way.

When you move a slider there, a **migration preview** opens and comes into view, naming the domain the way the rest of the drawer does and saying whose level it is: "*Cast life for supporting characters* drops from level 4 to 2: processes above level 2 stop and their inputs freeze; detail stays stored.", "*Money* rises from level 2 to 4: detail is filled in when first shown." (followed by "People without money detail get it from the pinned tables when you open them."), or "… stays at level 3." Each line is marked with what it does: freezes, rises, gains detail, or a note. The levels it names are the ones the chat plays at: where the world's rules set a minimum level for a domain, a setting below it reads as that minimum. It also lists conditions in your world's rules that depend on something that will stop updating. The buttons are **Apply to this chat**, which has the focus, and **Cancel**, and a toast confirms ("*Money* is now *Simulation*."). The change is recorded like any other and applies from the next turn forward.

### Mixed levels

A character at a low level can still deal with one at a high level: each side records at its own level, and the low-level side's money is represented by an *external economy* account, so the books always balance.

### Other people's money

From Money level 3, the people in the world live on their money as you do. They are paid, they pay their bills, and every Monday they pay a week of living costs that follows their wealth band (a world can set its own amounts; the Modern US data prices them from a weekly grocery basket), so their balances go down as well as up. A bill paid to someone in the world, like your rent to your landlord, lands in that person's own account. The first morning their bills run, the engine opens their accounts with the balances the world gives them. You see all of this only in [GM mode](#everyone-elses-money).

## Routines and schedules

People in a world have days of their own. A **routine** is what someone can be relied on to do on an ordinary day: opening the shop at seven, coffee somewhere three mornings a week, a walk by the lake on a fair weekend. Its blocks can be **fixed** (at a set time), **flexible** (somewhere in a window, or a few times a week) or **optional** (only likely, and the first thing a busy day drops), and someone can have no routine at all. A **schedule** is a commitment with set hours, such as a shift or school. A world's routines can rest on its lore or a character's sheet, and say so.

Routines and schedules are defaults the world relies on, never a script the story has to follow. Where someone's day comes from, most binding first:

1. **What the story established:** where the narration, your actions or the GM put them, a trip, a plan under way, and what the GM says they mean to do.
2. **Their commitments:** schedules, appointments and trips.
3. **Their plans** from the story.
4. **Their routine.**
5. **Their basic needs:** sleep and meals.
6. **Chores, their agenda's steps and things to get back** that the world grounds.

Nothing else fills a day: no outing, party or invitation that nothing in the world or the story grounds. The story always leads the people in it. What someone's routine and their own schedules are to the engine is their **role**, which the world sets for each level of the [fidelity ladder](#the-fidelity-ladder):

- **Informative**, the default for Live, Near and Far characters: their routine and schedules never move or occupy them. The story and context-aware planning (plans the story made, the GM's intent) decide what they do, and their routine is only context their mind and the narrator may weigh.
- **Enforced**, the default for Background characters: the planner lives their routine and their schedules run, the story still first. Off screen, this is where routines matter most.
- **Off**: their routine isn't used at all, not even as context.

**In a time skip** (a Timelapse, a typed span or a skip the reply narrates), someone whose routine is informative spends the time as the world picks for their level, or the GM for them:

- **Stay put**: they stay where the story has them.
- **Follow their routine**: they go about their usual routine for the skip, as an enforced one would be lived, except while a story plan, a trip, the GM's intent or an appointment covers them.
- **Planned by the model**, the default for Live, Near and Far: at the skip's start one helper call plans everyone it covers, from their routine, their plans and the story so far: a few blocks each, for what they do other than simply being at home asleep, and each lives what it planned for them, from the skip's first minute for whatever is under way as it starts. Whenever no block covers them, they keep to their basic needs as their day has them: home to sleep at night, and their meals (one their day has at work they have where they are; it never sends them to work). So does someone it planned nothing for, or everyone when the call fails, runs out of time or is cut off. The call is the **Time-skip beats** step's (**Settings → Steps and connections**), off by default: while it's off no call is made and everyone it would plan keeps to their basic needs, and the GM view's skip note says why. A replay or a swipe reads the recorded plan rather than asking again, and a skip continued inside the planned time reuses it. **Follow their routine** is Background's default.

An enforced routine is always lived through a skip, and an off one never. Either way the story comes first: no one is moved into or out of a scene with you, and someone arriving where you are is only noted.

Whatever the role, a routine or a schedule never moves anyone out of a scene with you, and no routine interrupts play or stops a time skip. The world's own schedules (weather, prices, bills, paydays, opening hours) and money run whatever the roles say: a job still pays on its schedule when the worker's routine only informs. Your own character follows its routine through a Timelapse only when you turn on its **Follow my routine** ([Timelapse](#timelapse)); a span you spend doing something ("for the next two hours, I sort the stock"), a skip the reply narrates or a time you fix with ✎ leaves you where the story has you.

**Tuning.** A world sets how routines and the story share the day in **Build → Routines → Routines and the story**:

- **What a routine is, by closeness:** the role of each level, **Live**, **Near**, **Far** and **Background**: **Informative**, **Enforced** or **Off**.
- **In a time skip:** for each level, how those whose routine is informative spend a skip: **Stay put**, **Follow their routine** or **Planned by the model**.
- **The narrator:** whether the narrator is told the usual routines of the people in the scene that your character can see and whose routine isn't off (on by default): at the end of **People**, a line or two each ("Maya usually: weekdays, working the counter at Daybreak Café 07:00–15:00"), as background it may use or ignore, never a fact to state. They go in only while the section budget has room, never carry a private block or anything your character can't see, and no helper reads them as fact. For a character whose routine only informs, this and their own mind's **Their usual routine** are the only ways their routine reaches the story.
- **Timelapse from the Actions sheet:** where **Follow my routine** starts (off by default).
- **How strictly people keep to routine** against the story, for those whose routine is enforced: **Story first** (someone the story put somewhere stays there until the story or the GM releases them), **Balanced** (the default: until their night, with Background characters keeping their routine) or **Routine first** (their routine's next block takes them back).
- **Invitations** from routine blocks that ask company along: **Off**, **To the player only** (the default) or **Between everyone**. Plans the story makes, and the GM, invite whatever this says.
- **Sociability:** **Reserved**, **Usual** or **Outgoing**, how fast people's social and fun needs grow.
- **How plans bend routine:** which plans the story makes take time from work and school.

In a chat, the GM can change any of this for one character without touching the world: their routine's role, how they spend a time skip, whether and how strictly they keep their routine, one block, or one schedule, each with its Undo.

Where you meet them:

- **Play → People**: someone's usual day, as far as you may know it, and why each block is there ([People](#people)).
- **GM → Routines**: everyone's routine, schedules and day as this chat has them, with what the story changed and the GM's changes ([Routines in GM mode](#routines-in-gm-mode)).
- **Build → Routines**: the world's routines and their settings ([Routines in Build](#routines-in-build)), and routines proposed from the world's lore ([Proposing routines from lore](#proposing-routines-from-lore)). A routine Build can't read, or a setting it doesn't take, isn't saved: the message names the field to fix. Outside agents edit them the same way, with the same checks ([MCP.md](MCP.md)).

### Needs

From Cast life 4 your character gets hungry, tired, lonely and bored, each from 0 (met) to 100 (urgent). Once a need is pressing (60 by default) the narrator is told of it ("Alex Rivera (you): hungry") and **Me → Needs** marks it; at its alert point (80) it raises the needs alert in a timelapse, which by default is ignored ([Interrupts](#timelapse)).

What meets a need meets it when it's done, so the need grows again from then:

- **Eating** meets hunger at the end of the meal (half an hour, or as long as you say: "I eat for an hour").
- **Resting** meets fun at the end of the rest (half an hour, or as long as you say: "I rest for an hour" takes an hour; a longer rest goes to [Speed through](#speed-through-and-timelapse-offers)).
- **Company**: every turn you spend with someone you know (anyone you've met, there with you as the turn ends) meets your social need, so a day spent with friends keeps you from getting lonely. **Chatting** with someone meets it at the end of the chat (a quarter of an hour, or "I talk to Rosa for two hours"), or at once while the clock is paused.
- **An activity a turn gives you** (a world's own "Have dinner", a short nap or rest) meets its need when it ends within that turn, and **Not an action?** takes that back with the action. One that runs on goes on through the turns that follow and meets its need when one of them sees it out, unless you've done something else or gone somewhere first.
- **Sleep** meets fatigue as you sleep through it: **Go to bed** sleeps until 07:00, then the Speed through or Timelapse offer rests you when the night ends; so does any time skip your routine sleeps through. A nap you give a length ("I sleep for 20 minutes") lasts that long and rests you as it ends: a short one within the turn, a long one through its Timelapse. Going to bed and getting straight up again rests no one: **Get up** ("I get out of bed"), anything else that sets what you're doing, or going somewhere ends the night. Saying goodnight doesn't get you up.
- **Through a time skip**, what you live during the skip meets your needs: your meals and sleep, time out, and time with someone you know. The time you played before it counts only as you played it.
- **What only the narration describes meets nothing.** A meal, a nap or a night that the reply narrates, without your character eating, resting or going to bed through your own message, doesn't meet a need: say it ("I eat", "I go to bed") or use **Eat**, **Rest** or **Go to bed**. LumiRPEngine doesn't yet read meals or sleep from the reply.

**Beds.** Anyone can have a **bed**: a place, or an item such as a cot, wherever it stands. Their nights at home happen there, and Go to bed takes your character to yours. Set it in Build (the person's **Bed** field) or in GM mode (the person's page, **Where it is → Bed**). A bed in a secret place stays secret.

**Needs settings** (**Build → Needs**): each need under its own name, with what meets it, an **On**/**Off** in its header, and each number said in words with its value: **Grows by 4 points an hour, out of 100**, **Told to the narrator from 60** and **An alert from 80**. Under them, how long the need takes from met to each point at that rate ("told after 15 h and alerts after 20 h"); a need switched off shows no numbers. A world without settings plays as before: hunger 4, fatigue 3, social 1.5 and fun 1 an hour, told from 60 and alerting from 80. **Use the defaults** puts them back. A setting the engine can't read is refused when you save it, and **Check world** warns about an alert set below the point its need is told from.

## How checks are decided

A check is the dice an action rolls (see [Checks, pools, progression and sheets](#checks-pools-progression-and-sheets)). How much those dice matter, against how you describe what your character does, is up to the world and to you. A world can set its own rules for checks in **Build → Ruleset**, under **How checks are decided**. A world that sets none rolls every check and lets the roll settle it, exactly as before.

### The presets

| Preset | When checks roll | Your described approach | Who settles it | Retrying a failed check |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Narrative-first** | Only risky ones (under 75% to succeed); likelier actions just succeed | The judge reads it as it picks the outcome | The story: a helper picks the outcome, with the exact odds as guidance; the dice when nothing judged it | Needs a change |
| **Balanced** | Every check, except that sure things (95% or more) pass and hopeless ones (5% or less) fail without a roll | Adds up to +2 | The roll; a roll within 2 of another result is a close call, and the narrator may add a cost or partial progress | Needs a change |
| **Crunchy** | Every check | Doesn't count | The roll | Any time |

Crunchy is how the engine has always played. **Custom** lets you set each value, and changing any value of a preset makes it Custom. A world's rules that name a preset but set some values their own way play as Custom with those values, the preset's filling in the rest, so a world's own values always apply; Build's lint says so ("This names the Balanced preset but sets checks, riskyAt and description.max its own way, so it plays as Custom with them"). The values:

- **When checks roll**: every check; only risky ones, below a chance you set; only where marked (an action marked **Its check always rolls**, a place marked **Checks roll here** and the places inside it, or a quest marked **Checks roll while it's in progress** while it is under way and you can see it in your quests, so a secret quest never shows by making the dice roll); or never. A check that isn't asked for succeeds.
- **Sure things pass without a roll** and **Hopeless tries fail without a roll**, each from a chance you set.
- **How the player's approach counts**: not at all, as a bonus up to **The most it counts**, as advantage from a rating you set, or as a DC lower by up to the most it counts (a check without a DC takes it as a bonus). It shifts a check that rolls; the Actions sheet shows the odds before it.
- **Who settles a check**: the roll; the roll, with close calls within the points you set; or the story.
- **Retrying a failed check**: any time; after a change (the minutes you set passing, a new round of a fight, better or worse odds, another way of doing it such as another stat, tool or target, or going somewhere else; rewording the same thing isn't a change); or once per scene (until you leave and come back).

A chance is a check's exact chance of success, as the Actions sheet shows it: the dice's equally likely results that succeed, counted, out of all of them. A chance exactly at a line counts as at it, so on a d20 19 faces of 20 are a 95% sure thing, and 15 of 20 are 75%, not risky under a 75% line. Success is the outcomes a check lists as success, else its `success` band and every band after it. A check that names no success has no success to give without a roll, so it rolls whenever its action runs, whatever the rules say about when checks happen (when the story decides, it still does, and the dice do when it can't), and a failed try at it isn't remembered.

When nothing is rolled, the check passes as `success` (when the action and the check have none, as the check's **Outcome when not rolled**, else its likeliest passing band); a hopeless try fails as `failure` (else its likeliest failing band); and a retry that needs a change fails the way it failed before.

### Your approach and the judge

When the rules weigh your words, or leave the check to the story, the **Judge your approach** step reads what you wrote before the reply. It rates your approach from 0 up to the most it counts, with one line on why, and when the story decides it also gives each outcome's odds, which the engine draws from with the turn's seed. It takes one call from the before-the-reply budget, ahead of NPC choices, runs only for your own action with a check the rules would roll or leave to the story, only when you described what you do (an action picked in the Actions sheet with nothing typed isn't judged), and is off in the Lean preset. Its answer is kept in memory with the reading of your message's text (the last 64 per chat, until a restart): a Re-narrate keeps the outcome, and a Re-run of the same message reads the same judgement with a fresh draw while it is kept, or asks again. Without a judgement (the step is off, out of budget or fails), a rolled check rolls as the Actions sheet showed, and a check the story would decide is rolled with the dice instead, and the **Roll** chip says nothing judged your approach. Other people's actions always roll.

### What you see

The Actions sheet says what the rules will do before you commit: **No roll needed**, **Too hard to try**, **Retry needs a change** (or **Retry needs a new scene**), or **The story decides (odds 35%)**, with the odds bar as the guidance. After the turn, the **Roll** chip says what was rolled, or "No roll:" and why, with how your approach was rated when it counted and the close call when there was one. The narrator is told the same: to narrate the result, that a close success may come at a cost or a close failure leave partial progress, or what the story decided.

An inferred check (see [Typed text](#typed-text)) shows the same way, with what it was for, and with how each companion did. When someone it went up against set how hard it was, neither the chip nor the narrator gets their numbers, nor the odds, the margin or the difficulty that would give them away, unless the chat's rules show others' numbers. Whatever the rules, no panel shows them, the Journal and the Context inspector included: their rolls are recorded as hidden checks.

### New chats and this chat

**Settings → Checks** holds your own rules for new chats: a new chat in a world that sets none uses them, and **Use my check rules instead of a world's for new chats** makes every new chat use them. A change there saves at once, and a chat keeps the rules it started with, whatever you change later: a chat started before a world's own values over a named preset applied keeps playing that preset, until you set them in **GM → This chat → Checks**. **GM → This chat → Checks** shows the rules in force in the chat you have open; change them and tap **Apply to this chat**, or **Cancel**. The change applies from the next turn, and it is recorded like a realism change, so it follows swipes and branches.

### Inferred checks

A typed attempt that no action covers ("I try to talk the bouncer into letting me in", "I keep my face blank while Marcus lies", "we sneak past the guard") can be read as a check of the world's own sheet, rolled and recorded like any check, so the reply follows the dice. How is set beside the rules above, in the same three places and layered the same way:

- **Build → Ruleset → How checks are decided**, under **Inferred checks**: the world's default. Turn on **This world sets how typed attempts are read as checks** to set it, starting from what the world's realism defaults play; leave it off and new chats take the player's own setting.
- **Settings → Checks**, under **Inferred checks**: yours, for new chats. **By the chat's realism** (the default) leaves each new chat to its realism preset; **My own** sets your own, starting from what your realism defaults play. It fills in for a world that sets none, and **Use my check rules instead of a world's for new chats** makes it replace a world's too. A change saves at once.
- **GM → This chat → Checks**, under the rules in force: the chat you have open. Change any value and tap **Review the change** to see what it does, then **Apply to this chat** (or **Keep editing**); **Cancel** drops the edits. It applies from the next turn, earlier turns keep how theirs were read, and it is recorded like a realism change, so it follows swipes and branches.

A chat nobody set one for, a chat started before inferred checks among them, plays by its realism preset at its Sheets level:

| Preset | Read typed attempts | Which attempts | Who rolls | Difficulty | The narrator is told | Others' numbers | Most rolls a turn |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Story** | Roll automatically | Only when the message says it tries | The player character only | Easy to Hard | The result | Hidden | 1 |
| **Grounded** | Roll automatically | Only risky attempts | With companions | Easy to Very hard | The result | Hidden | 3 |
| **Simulation** | Roll automatically | Every uncertain attempt | Everyone involved | Easy to Heroic | The result and margin | Hidden | 6 |

Story rolls by itself rather than asking, so a Story chat never stops to wait on a **Roll** card unless you pick **Suggest a roll**; it rolls only what your message says your character tries, so other messages play as they always did. At Sheets 0 there is no sheet to roll with, so it is off; at Sheets 1 the narrator is told the result alone, and at Sheets 2 the margin at most. A Custom realism takes the preset its Sheets level matches (1 Story, 2 Grounded, 3 and up Simulation).

The values, the same in each place:

- **Read typed attempts as checks**: **Off** (typed text is read as it was before inferred checks: a message that says it uses a skill reads as the world's own skill action, such as **Use a skill** or **Attempt**, and rolls its check, and an attempt no action covers plays as roleplay, the narrator deciding how it goes), **Suggest a roll** (a chip offers the roll, which happens only when you take it) or **Roll automatically**. Suggest and Roll automatically read such a message as **Try something** in place of those actions, so it is never rolled twice. Under Off the other values are greyed out and keep what they were, for when you turn it back on.
- **Which attempts**: **Every uncertain attempt**, **Only risky attempts** (below the risky line of the rules above) or **Only when the message says it tries**.
- **Who rolls**: **The player character only**; **With companions**, each companion who joins in rolling their own; or **Everyone involved**, the people the attempt opposes or targets rolling against it too.
- **Every skill the sheet has**, or turn it off and pick the skills an attempt may roll. Each place lists the skills its world's sheet has: Build the draft's sheet as you edit it, GM the chat's world's, and Settings the world of the chat you have open (with none open there is nothing to pick from). A picked skill a sheet doesn't have stays listed, marked so, until you take it off; a world whose sheet has none of the picked skills reads no attempt as a check.
- **Easiest difficulty** and **Hardest difficulty**: the bands the difficulty is read from, from the attempt and its scene: **Trivial** (DC 5), **Easy** (8), **Moderate** (10), **Tricky** (12), **Hard** (15), **Very hard** (20), **Heroic** (25) and **Nearly impossible** (30). One outside the range is moved to the nearest band in it, and moving one bound past the other takes the other along.
- **What the narrator is told**: **The result** (passed or failed), **The result and margin** (by how much) or **The whole roll** (the dice, the modifier and the total).
- **Others' numbers**: **Hidden** or **Shown**: whether an opponent's or a companion's dice, modifier, total or score reach the narrator and the **Roll** chip. Hidden keeps a hidden stat hidden.
- **After a success, tell the narrator** and **After a failure, tell the narrator**: the line the narrator is given after each result, up to 240 characters. `{who}` reads as who rolled, `{skill}` as the skill, `{attempt}` as what they tried and `{against}` as who or what it was against. A line left empty is the default's ("Narrate it succeeding.", "Narrate it failing."), and **Reset the lines** puts both back.
- **Most rolls a turn**: 1 to 12. Your character's roll comes first, then companions', then opponents'; past the most, an opponent sets the difficulty without a roll.

## Model use

LumiRPEngine does most of its work without the AI. When it does ask a model, it uses small, focused calls, each with its own job. **Model use** presets set which steps may call a model, when, and how many calls a phase may spend. **Settings → Model use** shows them as four cards, **Lean**, **Standard**, **Full** and **Custom**, each saying in plain words what the preset does and then its limits; tap a card to use that preset, and the one in use is marked **In use**.

| Preset | Before the reply | After the reply | Model steps beyond the narrator: most in one turn | Budgets (calls before / after) | Lore lookups (before / after) | Engine budget (sections) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Lean** | Reads every message you type | Reads the reply | 2 (reading your text, reading the reply) | 1 / 1 | none | 2,000 tokens |
| **Standard** (the default) | Reads every message you type; judges your approach when the rules for checks need it; asks about NPC choices on close calls and invitations | Reads the reply; NPC choices off screen on close calls | 4 (reading your text, judging your approach or an NPC choice with you, an NPC choice elsewhere, reading the reply) | 2 / 2 | 4 / 3 | 8,000 tokens |
| **Full** | Reads every message you type, judges your approach when needed, decides NPC choices for everyone present, writes a brief and a prose outline | Reads the reply, decides off-screen NPC choices | 8 (adds the brief and the prose outline, and has room for judging your approach, an NPC choice with you and a puzzle answer in one turn; an answer has no approach to judge, so a turn runs at most 7) | 8 / 4 | 6 / 4 | 12,000 tokens |
| **Custom** | Any step, any trigger, any connection and model | Same | Depends on the steps you enable; the call meter shows it live | Yours | Yours | Yours |

The number is the most steps a turn can run: each step takes one call from its phase's budget when it starts, so a phase never runs more steps than its budget allows. A step can cost more calls than that: each lore lookup is one more (from its phase's lookup budget), and a JSON repair, a Decision call falling back to the helper, or `interpret` asking again after a dropped connection adds one that no budget is charged for. Most turns cost less, because a step runs only when its trigger fires. The call meter counts every engine model call, not the narrator, so it shows each turn's real count. **Lean** is the floor. It keeps the cost down and leaves NPC choices to the engine's own rules rather than a model. Standard is where a model starts deciding close calls.

### The steps

| Step | When it runs | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| `interpret` (Interpret typed input) | Before the reply | Reads every typed message as the actions it takes, in order (up to five play in the turn), including a request to let time pass, from what your character can do now, the scene and the messages before (see [Typed text](#typed-text)); what the turn doesn't play is a chip for your next message. |
| `npc.choose` (NPC choices) | Before the reply for people who are with you; after the reply for people elsewhere | Picks what an NPC does on a close call or invitation, from a list the engine enumerates (always including "do nothing"). |
| `puzzle.answer` (Puzzle answers) | Before the reply | Judges a free-text puzzle answer, only for puzzles whose author opted in; the answer is a probability against a threshold. It runs unless you turn it off, on every answer while its Signals are empty. |
| `judge` (Judge your approach) | Before the reply | Rates how well you describe your approach to an action's check, with one line on why, and when the story decides gives each outcome's odds. It runs only when the world's rules for checks need it; see [How checks are decided](#how-checks-are-decided). Off in Lean. |
| `brief` (Scene brief), `outline` (Prose outline) | Before the reply, once the engine has planned the turn | Write a short briefing or a prose outline for the narrator. The engine always writes a deterministic outline from its own beats; the brief and the prose outline stack on top of it in the Outline section. |
| `extract` (Extract changes) | After the reply | Reads the reply for changes. See [Extraction modes](#extraction-modes). |

| `choices` (Suggested choices) | After the reply | Offers next-step options as **Suggested choices** chips. |
| `mind` (Character minds) | Before the reply, and at time-skip beats | One call per character, as that character: their monologue, depiction, intent and reactions. Runs on its own cadence; see [Character minds](#character-minds). |
| `memory` (Character memory) | After the reply | Per character who heard your message, at the levels you pick: the notes and commitments they take from what the reply said, and the plans it states. |
| `skip` (Time-skip beats) | Before a time skip, once you turn it on (it is off in every preset) | Proposes further moments in the skip worth stopping at, beside the ones the engine finds. |
| `consolidate` (Memory consolidation) | After the reply | Folds a character's faded and surplus notes into their summary. |
| `knowledge` (Who knows what) | After the reply, once its reading is in, while the chat tracks who knows what (minds on or off) | One call for the scene, or one per character (Settings → Minds → Knowledge): who learned what by seeing or being told it, what someone worked out and how sure, and what someone now doubts. The engine checks it: no one sees what they weren't there for, and whoever tells something must know it. |

A step runs when one of its **signals** fires (in **Settings → Steps and connections**, under each step's **Advanced: signals and lore**). `always` fires every turn; `never` turns the step off and wins over the rest. The others:

| Signal | Fires when |
|---|---|
| `typed_input` | The turn's text was typed rather than chosen. A step saved on the retired `classifier` signal runs on this. |
| `reply` | A reply exists (after the reply). |
| `scene_change`, `slot_crossed`, `day_start` | You changed place; the turn crossed a part of the day; it crossed midnight. |
| `beat` | The plan has outline beats. |
| `agents_present`, `present`, `offscreen`, `tie_zone` | Agents are with you; a close call is open for someone with you; for someone elsewhere; for the people this phase decides for. |
| `invitation`, `timelapse`, `conflict` | An invitation was made; the turn was a Timelapse; your action failed, was declined or rolled a failing band. |
| `every_n:k` | Every *k*th assistant turn. |

Prose steps (`brief`, `outline`, `choices`) are shown only what your character could see: the facts they get are the player-visible ones, and any line that would give away an unrevealed truth or a secret lore entry's text is dropped before the call. A question that carries a secret never shares a call with prose.

### The helper model and connections

Every model call the engine makes for itself (reading your actions and the reply, NPC choices, judging, the brief and outline, minds and their memory, time-skip beats, lore lookups) runs on the **helper model**, set in **Settings → Helper model**, the first page under **Getting started**:

- **Connection**: any of your Lumiverse connections. **None** runs each call on the connection Lumiverse marks as your default, with its own model and settings.
- **Model**: for a connection you picked, its model list (or type a name); empty uses the connection's own model.
- **Reasoning effort**: sent with each call as Lumiverse's own reasoning setting, which Lumiverse turns into the provider's field (DeepSeek reads Low to High as High, and Max and Extra high as Max). **Connection's own**, the default, leaves the connection's reasoning setting as it is, and **Off** turns reasoning off.
- **Max tokens**: 8,192 by default, with a warning below 4,096. Reasoning models think before they answer, and the thinking counts toward the limit, so a smaller one can leave the answer empty.
- **Temperature**, **Top P**, **Top K**, **Frequency penalty** and **Presence penalty**: each is **the connection's own** (its preset's value) until you switch it off and set a number. Lumiverse passes each one you set to the provider, which uses those it supports.

Picking a preset never changes the helper model. The Decisions API takes no effort, max tokens or sampling parameters, so steps on it run with their connection's own settings.

Each step can set its own, in **Settings → Steps and connections**, which opens with a **Helper model** row that leads to the page above. Each step is a card that shows what it runs on: *Helper*, the connection, the model, the effort, the max tokens and any parameter set; or *Decision* and its connection; or **Off**. Open it for a line on what the step does, **Enabled**, a **Provider** (*Decision* or *Helper*), a **Connection** and, for a connection you picked, a **Model**; then for a helper step **Reasoning effort**, **Max tokens** and, under **Sampling parameters**, each parameter. Each starts at **Use helper default**, and the card shows the helper model's value beside it; whatever the step sets wins over the helper model's, one by one. The exceptions are **Character memory**, **Memory consolidation** and **Time-skip beats** (which also plans a time skip), whose **Reasoning effort** starts at **Off**: their answers are short notes on what happened or is due, and a reasoning model could spend the whole of its max tokens thinking about one and answer nothing, leaving the reply's reading to wait half a minute and fail. Set any effort on their cards, **Use helper default** included, and they reason again. Every step but the minds steps has **Recent chat**: **Reads recent chat**, and while it's on how many of the messages before this turn's it reads (**Messages**) and about how many tokens of them (**Tokens**), each a number or **no limit**, the newest first, stopping at whichever limit comes first; the step gets them, oldest first, as a "Recent chat" block after its own input. *Interpret typed input* reads 8 messages and 3,000 tokens unless you change it; every other step reads none until you switch it on. Under **Advanced: signals and lore** are its **Signals** and lore. Nothing is cut or simplified because a model is small; each default is a tap to change. Picking **Lean**, **Standard** or **Full** in **Settings → Model use** replaces every step's settings with the preset's, connections and models included, so pick the preset first; changing a step afterwards makes it **Custom** and keeps the rest.

Each card also has a **Prompt**: your own wording for the step, which wins over the world's step template for it (Build → Step templates), and that over the built-in. A line under its name says what it does to the step, as a world template does: for reading your typed input and reading the reply it is added before the built-in instructions, which it can't change, so its box starts empty (**None added**); for the brief, the outline, suggested choices, NPC choices, puzzle answers and judging your approach it replaces the built-in instructions or question, and its box starts from that text. Below it, folded and read-only, is the built-in: the text a prompt replaces, or, for the two that add to it, what the built-in instructions hold, since they're made fresh each turn from the scene. The answer format and the leak checks never change. **Save** keeps it, **Revert** drops your edits, and **Reset to the default** goes back to the world's template or the built-in. A world's `effects` templates still run beside your prompt. The minds steps' prompts are the same ones as in **Settings → Minds**; *Time-skip beats* has two, its beats' and **The skip plan**'s, the call that plans how the people the model plans spend a time skip. Picking a **Model use** preset keeps every prompt of yours. Beside each prompt, **Placeholders** lists the `{{vars}}` that step is given, when it runs, and what each holds; Build's step templates show the same list, and name each purpose and output in words. A new step template isn't in the draft until **Create**: leaving its page before then asks first.

Each step's card also says which roles of an agent chat run where it does ("On agent chats, Intent (reading your message) runs where this step does."), and which of those roles set a connection of their own. **Settings → Helper model** says the same of the roles whose tasks run on it: those whose step and role set no connection.

By default, the helper model handles `interpret`, `extract`, the prose steps and the minds, and your **Decision connection** (Lumiverse's Decisions API) handles `npc.choose`. The Decisions API takes no reasoning effort or max tokens, so a Decision step runs with its connection's own settings. If a decision call fails, the step falls back to the helper model, and then to the engine alone. A failed call is never blindly retried, and neither a repair of malformed JSON nor a decision falling back to the helper is charged against a budget. The model and connection you pick for a step are the ones its call uses.

**Preview before playing**, at the top of **Settings → Steps and connections**, lets you look at a turn before it plays. Both switches start off.

- **Show the action read from your message before it plays.** When the engine reads an action or a time skip from what you typed, the turn waits, with nothing generated, and your message gets a card saying what **Play** would play, with **Play**, **Edit** and **Cancel**. For actions it names the first one and what it acts on, and how many follow ("Play Take: letter, then 2 more?"); below, one line each, it quotes your own words for it ("“I take the envelope from the clerk”"), then each action after it with your words ("Then Go to: Bluebird Coffee — “I head to the cafe”"), the time your message lets pass after them, anything it left out and why, and any words it didn't read as an action. People and places appear by name only when your character knows of them; something you typed that the world has no name for, such as a thing you say you have ("letter") or a code ("red_door"), appears as you typed it. The card never names someone or something your character doesn't know of that you didn't name yourself: when the reading puts such a name into what you said (an answer to "whoever was in the shop last night" read as a name), that part is left off, while your own words always show as you typed them. For a time skip it says how long and from when to when ("Play Timelapse: 1 h, 10:29 PM to 11:29 PM?"), what fills it in your words ("Filled with: “I sort the stock”"), and what Play does: the clock runs on in one go, you stay where the story has you through a stated length (or follow your routine to a part of the day or a commitment), and the reply tells it. **Edit** opens the Actions sheet at that action with what was read already picked; pick something else and press its **Play** button to play that instead. It offers only what the sheet always offers your character, so nobody and nowhere your character doesn't know of. Cash you hand over plays as **Pay**, and an edit of it still plays as your hand-over would: to someone who isn't there it plays as roleplay, and a smaller amount adds only what your cash lacks of it, while a larger one adds nothing you weren't asked about. For a time skip, **Edit** opens the Timelapse sheet at the skip as read, with Follow my routine as read; choose another length or end and press its **Play Timelapse** button. **Play** plays it as read. **Cancel** drops the read and leaves the turn unplayed, so you can edit your message and send it again; pressing Regenerate instead reads your message afresh, rather than bringing back the card you cancelled. An action you tap in the palette or on a chip plays at once.
- **Show the turn's outline before the reply is written.** Once the engine has planned the turn, it waits with the Outline the narrator would get: the brief, the engine's beats and the outline step's, one per line. **Play** writes the reply from it. **Edit** lets you change, add or remove lines, and the reply is told your outline. **Cancel** leaves the turn unplayed. Playing it keeps the turn exactly as you saw it planned, dice included: no mind, NPC choice, brief or outline is asked again. That holds for that reply only: a swipe after it plans the turn again and shows you its new outline. A line with something your character hasn't learned yet is a spoiler: it stays in the outline as it was, marked as a secret for the narrator, and the card lists it under the others, folded (*A line with a spoiler: tap to show*) until you tap it open; **Edit** never offers it. For a new reply after a reply (**Send** with nothing typed), the card is under the last reply, and **Play** or **Edit** keeps the outline and asks you to press **Send** with nothing typed: only your Send can start that reply, and it writes it from the outline you let through, never rolling the reply before it again.

### Budgets, deadlines and fallbacks

Budgets are a **number of calls for each phase**: **Before the reply** (reading your text, the NPC choices for people with you, puzzle answers, judging your approach, the brief and the prose outline, and the minds beside them) and **After the reply** (reading the reply, off-screen NPC choices, suggested choices). You set them in **Settings → Model use** under **Advanced: budgets and the engine budget**, each phase with **Calls** and **Lore lookups**, beside **Engine budget (tokens)** for the sections; how long each phase may take is in [Settings → Timeouts](#timeouts). Calls, lore lookups and the token budgets have no upper limit. Changing a budget makes the preset **Custom**. Each step takes one call from its phase's budget before it starts; when a budget is spent, or its time is up, each remaining step uses its fallback (**Calls** 0 means no model at all in that phase): number-only NPC choices, the deterministic outline, a check rolled as if unjudged (one the story would decide goes to the dice), or plain roleplay plus reading the reply. Steps run by priority, and independent ones run in parallel. Lumiverse stops a Decision call at 30 seconds, its own limit, and the step then uses the helper. Simulation itself is never cut off by a clock; it is bounded by counts (days, agents, events).

Settings saved before these defaults rose keep every value you changed; a value still at an old default (a step's 2,000 max tokens, which now follows the helper model; the old lore tokens, lookup counts and section budgets) reads as the new one. Likewise, Character memory, Memory consolidation and Time-skip beats saved on **Use helper default**, while the helper model's effort is **Connection's own**, now start at **Off**; an effort you chose for them, or for the helper model, stays. The engine budget rose again when the sections began telling everything whole (every lore entry about the place, every thing lying there, every field of a vehicle): an engine budget still at Standard's old 4,000 or Full's old 8,000 reads as 8,000 or 12,000.

### Timeouts

**Settings → Timeouts** (under *Getting started*) holds every wait the engine keeps, each with what it limits, what happens when it runs out, its default and its value in seconds. None has a ceiling, and **No limit** lifts one wherever that can't break anything. Picking a Model use preset never changes them.

- **Turns**: **Engine run** (5 min: everything before the reply is generated; when it runs out the run stops, no reply is generated, and the chat says which step ran out, with a Retry), **Lore lookups before the reply** (2 min), **Final review** (5 min: the main agent's review of the narrator's prompt on a chat on agents; when it runs out its edits are dropped and the narrator gets the prompt as the engine made it, or the turn stops, as the review's own setting says), **Reading the reply** (5 min: what changed, people elsewhere, choices, memory), **Lore lookups after the reply** (2 min), **Generation guard** (2 min: Lumiverse's wait for the engine's check, and for its writing of the turn's sections, as a generation starts; Lumiverse caps it at 2 min) and **Time skip plan** (the helper call that plans a time skip for those **Planned by the model**; when it runs out nobody is planned, so they keep to their sleep and meals, the GM's routine page says why, and the skip runs).
- **This tab**: **Engine requests** (5 min: how long the tab waits for each answer) and **Preset check** (1 min: how long Caveats waits for its test prompt before saying it's still running).
- **Jobs and background work**: **Minds run on demand** (10 min), **Import and Refine calls** (10 min each; the lore index, map and routine proposal calls in Build take it too), **Token counts** (30 s; 0 always uses the engine's estimate), **Background lane start-up** and **Background lane heartbeat** (1 min each; Lumiverse caps both at 2 min, and No limit on the heartbeat asks it to check none), and **MCP server reconnect** (1 min: how long **Reconnect in Lumiverse** waits for a server Lumiverse runs to come back with the new tools before its line tells you to reconnect it by hand).

The page ends with **Lumiverse's own limits**, which no setting changes. When a timeout runs out, [Diagnostics](#diagnostics) records it with the setting's name and how to raise it, and a request the tab gave up on says so in its error. Settings saved before Timeouts existed keep the wall clocks you set; one still at an old preset's default rises to the new default. The command line and the MCP server take `--timeout <seconds|none>` (or `LRE_TIMEOUT`), 300 seconds by default.

### Lore for the helpers

The engine's own helpers read the world's lore ([World lore](#world-lore)) to do their jobs: `interpret` resolves names and aliases, `extract` recognises who and what the reply is about, `npc.choose` weighs a person's past and motives, `judge` weighs your described approach against what the world says, and the brief, the outline and puzzle answers read what bears on the scene. The narrator reads the lore about where your character is in the **Place** section; the rest of the world's public lore reaches it only through what the brief and the prose outline write, since they may read it to plan the scene.

- **Prefetch.** Before a step asks its model, the engine searches the lore with the step's own inputs: your message or the reply, the people and places in the scene, the quests you can see, or the people and options of an NPC choice. What it finds goes into that step's prompt, cut to fit the step's lore tokens. A prefetch costs no call.
- **Lookups.** A step can also ask for more. On most connections it calls the lore tools (search, read one entry, list a topic); when a call with them fails for good (not a timeout or a busy provider), the step asks once more in a plain form instead (`{"need": [...]}`), and only a connection that said it takes no tools is remembered, to ask in that form from then on. Each time the model asks, the engine answers and the step is asked again: one more call, a **lore lookup**. Steps on the Decision connection can't use tools, so before deciding they get one lookup on the helper that asks what lore the decision needs.
- **Per step**, in **Settings → Steps and connections**, under each step's **Advanced: signals and lore**: **Lore** (*Off*, *Prefetch*, *Look up* or *Both*), **Lore tokens**, and **Lookups by** (*Auto*, *Tools* or *Ask form*). A step without its own setting takes its preset's.
- **Per phase**, in **Settings → Model use** under **Advanced: budgets and the engine budget**: **Lore lookups**; in **Settings → Timeouts**, how long after the phase starts one may begin. Each lookup takes one from its phase's lookup budget, apart from the phase's calls, and the steps that run first get them first. A lookup starts only while a third of the phase's time is left, so a step still has time to answer. When none is left, the step answers with what its prefetch found. **Lean** makes no lookups.
- **Pinned lore.** In **Build**, a character's page and a place's page (a place drawn on a map too) have **Pinned lore**, and **Pinned lore** in Build's **World** list has a section for each step, with what holds for every step's pins said once at its top. **Pin world lore** picks one of the world's entries, and **Pin from World Info** picks one of your World Info books (the ones attached to the open chat first) and then **The whole book** or one of its entries, even one switched off in its book. A whole book is read as it is each time a call reads it: its switched-on entries, in the book's order, so entries added to it later (the chapters another extension writes, say) are read without pinning them again. What is pinned to a character is read first by their mind every time they think; what is pinned to **Character minds** is read by every character's mind, after their own pins, and what is pinned to **Time-skip beats** by every mind at a time skip's beat too, after those; what is pinned to any other step is read first by every call of that step, whatever its **Lore** setting; what is pinned to a place is read first by every helper and mind there, or in a place inside it. **Character memory**, **Memory consolidation** and **Time-skip beats**' own call read only what is pinned to them, and so does the time skip's plan for those whose skip the model plans, which reads what is pinned to **Time-skip beats**. For a helper, pinned text comes first within the step's **Lore tokens**; for a mind, the other minds steps and the skip's plan, within **Lore pinned to them and to where they are** in **Settings → Minds → What a mind reads** (no limit by default). When it doesn't all fit it is cut, and the **Context** panel says so and which limit to raise. Each pin chooses what a cut keeps, under its name: **When it doesn't fit, keep the start** (the default), or **keep the end** for an entry written oldest first, such as a running timeline, a log or a journal, whose newest part matters most; a whole book's entries each keep the part its pin keeps. Pins are part of the world: commit, then **Upgrade** the chat, to use a new pin in it; a pinned book's text is always read as it is now. A pinned entry is as secret as it is everywhere else. A pinned World Info entry is read without its secret lines, and one that is only a secret line reaches only the minds of the people it is known to. A secret one, the world's, a World Info entry an earlier version made secret with its fields, one a secret entry of the world was indexed from or one with the same text as any of these, still reaches only its audience and its holders' minds until its gate holds (both gates, when it is secret in its book and in the world), and the **Context** panel doesn't name it before then; one a Known-by entry of the world was indexed from reads only for those who know what it links; a pinned entry the AI wrote waits for your review like any other. **Check** warns of a pin to an entry that is gone, twice over, or that nobody can read, and opens the page it is on.
- **Secrets.** A secret entry reaches only the helpers its audience names, until its gate holds; the brief, the prose outline, suggested choices and extraction never get it, so Build doesn't offer them as its readers (one an entry named before is ignored). A secret line in your World Info reaches no helper at all, only the minds of the people it is known to. An entry marked **Known by** is read only once your character knows every person and place it links. Nothing a helper writes for you may carry a secret's text: a chip, a Journal line, a section, an action read from your words or the reason given for a judged approach that repeats an unrevealed truth, or a sentence or clause of a secret entry, word for word is dropped, unless the words were yours. The check looks only for clauses of at least four words and 16 characters, so a shorter phrase or a paraphrase isn't caught, which is why a secret never goes to a step that writes for you. The helpers are told only who your character can see there, and a reason for a judged approach that names someone, a quest or a place your character can't see, or a secret entry by its title, another name it goes by or a keyword it is found by, is dropped unless your message named it too, since dropping your own words would tell you a name you typed is hidden; the rating stands. A brief, a prose outline line or a suggested choice that names one of them is left out unless your message, or what the step was told (who is there, what happened, what you have and know; for a choice, the reply too), named it, so what you typed reads the same, on the outline gate and in the **Context** panel's counts as much as to the narrator, whether or not someone hidden goes by it. Everyday words aren't names there: a hidden quest's step titles (*Whispers*), a secret entry's keywords, and anything your character saw that is now used up or gone (the coffee you drank) never leave a line out. Names count word for word: *Vex is watching* names a hidden Vex Morrow, while *dangerous* doesn't name a hidden Dan. A plural counts too (*the Okafors* names a hidden Ada Okafor), and so do the accents people leave out (*Zoe* names a hidden Zoë, *Soren* a hidden Søren). Someone whose name is a single letter, such as *Q*, counts as that capital letter standing alone, except *A* and *I*, which are ordinary words. Anything else, a place, a thing, a quest or an entry's title, counts only by all its words in order, so a secret *Under the Chapel* isn't named by *under* alone. A name in another script counts wherever it appears, even inside a word, which catches a language written without spaces (*李雷在监视你*) and a name with an ending added (*Вексом* for *Векс*).

### The call meter

The call meter counts each turn's engine model calls, seconds and tokens, and its lore lookups when there were any. **This turn** in the World sheet and the pill's details shows the latest turn. **Settings → Model use** has a **Call meter** section with rows for **This turn**, **Average** and **95th pct**, and columns for **Calls**, **Tokens**, **Lookups** and **Time**; **GM → This chat → Context** ends with the same meter. If a preset costs more than you want, this is where you see it. The meter is saved for you, so a restart keeps its numbers.

### Engine tokens versus your context

The engine's sections are a fixed overhead in every prompt, up to the engine budget. Nothing checks that budget against your connection's context size, so keep it well inside it: if the fixed part of the prompt is larger than the context, Lumiverse drops the chat history, including your latest message.

### Diagnostics

While a turn runs, the pill, the status line and the top of the drawer say what the engine is doing and for how long. While the engine runs (or reads a reply), the drawer's line has **Cancel**: it stops it at once. A cancelled turn isn't a failure. Nothing is generated, no red flag appears, and Regenerate or Send plays the turn again. A cancelled reading leaves the reply unread: nothing it found changes the world, and the reply gets **Retry read**. While the line only waits for the reply, or for you to press Send, there is nothing to cancel, so it has no Cancel. If a step fails, they turn red ("Failed: Planning"); the drawer's banner has **Diagnostics** and **Dismiss**. The characters' memories are updated beside the reading of the reply as a step of their own: a memory cut off at its max tokens shows as "Failed: Updating the characters' memories", while the reply's reading, which went through, stays fine.

On a chat that plays on agents (GM → This chat → **Engine**), the drawer's line opens into one row per role at work while the turn runs: its name as **Settings → Agents** gives it (**Main agent**, **Intent**, **Minds**…) and the tokens all its agents have sent and written this turn, with **Cancel** on each row while the turn can be stopped. A role that works for several people at once (the minds, a choice for each person, the memory) still has one row, so the line never tells how many people it is thinking for, someone off-screen or out of your character's sight among them. A row says nothing of what its agents look up or call, since that could name someone your character hasn't met; Diagnostics holds the rest, behind its curtain. Cancel on any row stops the whole turn, every agent of it, as the line's Cancel does.

**Settings → Diagnostics** (also under About) shows what the engine did in this chat. It can name someone your character hasn't met, so it opens behind **Show diagnostics**. Then:

- **Errors** lists what failed, newest first, with the reason and what to change: for example, an answer cut off at its max tokens tells you to raise **Max tokens** for that step in **Settings → Steps and connections**.
- Each **turn** lists its steps in order (planning, the plan, the outline, filling the prompt, reading the reply, the characters' memories, memory) with their time and outcome, and under each the model calls it made: the connection and model, tokens in and out, why the answer ended, and the time. Filling the prompt lists each section, the preset outlet it goes to, and whether it got text.
- An **agent's call** is a row of its own (the GM agent's, outside any turn): the agent's role, the model on its connection, tokens in (with how many came from the provider's cache and, when the provider says, how many it wrote to the cache) and out (with how many were reasoning), the time, and how it ended (ok, failed, cancelled or refused) with the error and the tools it called. A count the provider didn't report is the engine's estimate, marked **~**; what was written to the cache is never guessed, so a call that wrote none (**0 written**) tells apart from a connection that doesn't say (no **written** at all), and a prefix that was written but not read on the next call shows as such. **Show text** opens what the agent was sent and answered, in a scrolling block; once its retention (**Settings → Agents → Retention**, 30 days by default) has passed, the row stays but its text is gone, and it says so.
- **Slow** marks a view or an edit that took over a second.
- **Show** filters to **Problems**, **Model calls** (agents' calls included) or **Slow**; **Copy** puts the shown records on the clipboard as text, **Download** saves them all as JSON for a bug report, and **Clear** forgets them.

The engine keeps the newest records of each chat, so they survive a restart, and writes each as a line to your `diagnostics.log` (an agent's call without its text). **Records per chat**, on the page even before you open the curtain, sets how many: 400 by default, with **Default** to go back. Agents' calls fill them faster than the engine's steps; lowering it drops the oldest.

### Retention

The agents keep what they write once, in your LumiRPEngine storage, shared by all your chats: each turn's part of their session (with the summaries compaction writes and the spans they replace) and its transcript, the workspace's files, each chat's learned files, your global files (~/), and what each of their calls was sent and answered. A reply's record, a chat's learned files, your global workspace and Diagnostics point at these texts, so a swipe, a Re-run or a Branch copies nothing. **Settings → Agents → Retention** says which turns keep their transcripts, and how long a text stays once nothing points at it; each setting has **Default** to go back.

**Turn transcripts** are what GM → Workspace lists under transcripts/: everything each turn's agents did, as GM → Live showed it. By default every turn keeps its own, however many and however old; set either of these to let older ones go:

- **Turn transcripts** (No limit): how many of a chat's latest turns keep their transcript. Turn **No limit** off and it keeps the latest 50, which you can change; after each turn, the transcript of the turn that falls past it leaves the list, and no agent reads it any more. Each chat counts its own turns, a Branch too, so a Branch keeps the transcripts of its own latest turns even when they are older in the chat it came from.
- **Turn transcript age** (No limit): how many days a turn's transcript is kept after it was written (30 when you turn **No limit** off). One older leaves the list the same way. A transcript written before this setting came has no date to count from, so only **Turn transcripts** above lets it go.

A transcript that leaves the list is still stored until a sweep finds nothing points at it any more and **Unused texts** has passed (below), so raising a limit again soon brings it back; one a sweep has removed by then lists but reads as gone. The stored texts:

- **Agent call texts** (30 days): how long Diagnostics keeps what each agent call was sent and answered, after the call. The call's row stays as long as **Records per chat** keeps it; only its text goes. **No limit** keeps each text as long as its row, and 0 keeps none.
- **Unused texts** (7 days): how long a text nothing points at any more is kept before a sweep removes it: one only a deleted reply, swipe or chat used, a turn's transcript past the limits above, or a call's text past its limit. It counts from the first sweep that finds it unused, so the sweep that first finds a text unused never removes it, however long ago it stopped being used; with 0, the sweep after that one does.
- **Sweep** (every 24 hours): how often a sweep looks. It runs while you use LumiRPEngine and never while a turn runs, and one under way stops removing when a turn starts. **Never** removes nothing.

A sweep never removes a text anything still points at: a record on any swipe of any reply in any of your world chats (a Branch included, whether or not LumiRPEngine has opened it; a turn's transcript only while the limits above keep it in that chat), the session parts a compaction's summary or archived span reads, any chat's learned files, your global files, the call texts Diagnostics keeps, and what a turn under way, a change waiting for a turn to end or a chat's journal holds that isn't on its messages yet. While your global files, a chat's messages, or the engine data on one of them, can't be read, a sweep removes nothing and says why in the log. It reads your chats without keeping them in memory, so the chat you're playing isn't read again whole after it. So deleting a reply, a swipe or a chat frees only what nothing else points at, once **Unused texts** has passed.

### Settings → Agents

**Settings → Agents**, the first page of its own group (**Agents**, **Roles**, **Context**, **Main agent**, **Assistant**, **Prompts**, **Retention**), holds what LumiRPEngine's agents run under: the GM assistant, and the turns of a chat whose engine is **Agents** (GM → This chat → **Engine**). A chat on the steps engine reads none of it.
