The page opens with **A turn at a glance**: each stage of a turn in the order it plays (1 Intent; 3 Scene, minds and decisions; 5 Beats; 6 Final review; 7 Read; 8 Memory, or *Memory, left to the next turn* while memory runs before the next turn), the roles that work in it, where each runs (the connection and model its tasks actually go to, and where that comes from: *its own*, *from Steps*, *from Helper model*, or Lumiverse's default connection) and how, when that isn't as an agent with tools (*one call, no tools*; *in a time skip*); roles alike share a line, a stage with nothing to run says so, and each role's name opens its page. A badge says which pipeline runs the turn. Then **Every agent call**: **Streaming** and **Thinking in tool loops**. Then a row for each of its sections, each a page of its own under its name, saying what it holds now and how many of its settings are changed: **Pipeline**, **Review card**, **System instructions**, **Budgets**, **Compaction**, **Recording**, **Throttles**, **Caching** and **Acceptance runs**. Throughout these pages a setting that differs from its default says **Changed**, and only then shows its default and **Default** to go back to it; a limit is folded to its name and value until you open it; Back, or Escape, goes up one level, and focus returns to the row you opened.

- **Thinking in agent tool loops** (off): while it's off, every agent call that offers tools goes with reasoning off, whatever the agent's effort, because Lumiverse doesn't yet pass back what a reasoning model thought before a tool call, which it must be sent again. Calls without tools keep their effort. Switch it on once Lumiverse passes it back. While it's on, a call with an effort of its own (not *Connection's own*) also asks for the model's thinking summarized, so its text comes back: some models leave it out unless asked.
- **Streaming** (on): **Stream every agent call's answer** has each agent call take its answer as Lumiverse streams it, as the model writes it, rather than all at once at the end, which a gateway or proxy between the model and Lumiverse may cut off on a long call. The answer reads the same either way (its text, tool calls, reasoning and usage), **Cancel** stops a call mid-stream, and every time limit (a call's, an agent's, the turn's, the final review's) still counts the whole call, however long it streams. Off, each call waits for its answer whole.
- **Pipeline**: **How a turn is run**, *A fixed pipeline* (the default: each role runs in the steps' own order, working its step's part, with the steps engine's gates and cards) or *A main agent decides* (one main agent per chat decides which roles to run and what to tell them, in the order the engine keeps, and keeps a session from turn to turn); **When memory runs**, *After the reply* (the default, as the steps engine does) or *Before the next turn* (the reading of the reply ends sooner, and what the minds remember belongs to the next turn, which remembers it once, even after waiting at a gate, on either engine: switching the chat to the steps engine loses none; a Re-read edit or Retry read of that reply remembers in its own reading instead); and **The Curator keeps the world's things and places** (on): after the reply, beside the read, the [Curator](#settings--agents) makes the world agree with it (the things in it and who has them, the places it makes, the people who walk on and a purchase's goods), and the read leaves those to it; off, the read reads them itself, as the steps engine's read does.
- **Review card**: where a turn's changes that ask first wait: *On the review card after the reply* (the default: the turn goes on, and the narrator isn't told of a change until you apply it) or *On the review card before the reply* (the turn waits after its plan, as at the outline gate, until you answer). See the review card under [Under each reply](#under-each-reply).
- **Compaction**: **Compact the session past** (32,000 tokens) and **Recent turns kept whole** (3): once the main agent's session passes the size, everything but its latest turns becomes one summary, which GM → Workspace can roll back; and **The main agent's summarize tool** (on), which lets it compact its own session sooner.
- **Recording**: **Narrator-facing output, each** (2,000 characters): how long each line an agent turn records for the narrator may be (an outcome, montage or outline line, the brief, a scene note); a longer one is cut to it, ending in "…", and the cut is noted in your `diagnostics.log`, not on the Diagnostics page.
- **System instructions** (8,000 tokens): how much of each agent's prompt its system files may take together: **AGENTS.md** and its own **roles/<role>.md**, your global workspace's (~/) and this chat's, written in GM → Workspace (see [Workspace](#workspace)). They follow the agent's system prompt, the global ones first, so this chat's have the last word; past it, the file that no longer fits is cut at a line, the rest are left out, and the agent is told which. None ships, so until you write one they cost nothing.
- **Budgets** is a page of its own: *Each agent*, *Each turn, every agent together*, *Loops and large results*, *The GM's edits*, *Kept in memory*, each a group with its Changed count; a role, the main agent or the assistant may set any of the first group as its own, on its page. Each limit says what it limits and what happens when it's reached: for **each agent**, the prompt and the answer of one call (the answer at 8,192 tokens), the time of one call, the tool calls one answer may make, its prompt and answer tokens in all, its rounds of tool calls (10), its time, its **revisions** (2: the messages it may be sent after its first answer) and how many of its tool calls run at once (4); for **each turn**, every agent together: prompt tokens, answer tokens, rounds and time, each none by default (a long chat's turn sends far more than a short one's, so each agent's own limits bound it; set one only where you want a limit for the whole turn). A call never asks for more answer tokens than the agent or the turn has left, and one that its prompt would take past a budget isn't sent: the agent stops with what it has, and its record says which limit stopped it.
- **Fresh runs per request** (1), among each agent's budgets: how many times the main agent may run a role afresh on a request it already answered; the fresh run drops what the last one staged, and its revisions start again. It applies to turn roles only (not the assistant, the main agent or the final review).
- **Loop detector** (3): the same tool call with the same arguments three times in a row stops the agent before it's run. The final review's rewrites and take-outs count only while the prompt hasn't changed since the last one: they name a line by its number as it reads now, so the same number after an edit is the next line. **Tool results clipped past** (4,000 characters): a longer result reaches the model clipped, and the rest waits in the agent's scratch, which it reads with its built-in **scratch_read** tool.
- **GM edits per turn** (10), among the budgets: how many of the GM's own edits (a spawn, a removal, any field, a stage, the clock) a turn's agents may stage together, over the whole turn: those made before the plan, after it and after the reply all count; the ones past it are refused, and the agent that asked is told so. Only a role whose tier grants the GM's edits (**Roles**) makes any.
- **Kept in memory**, among the budgets, each with its **Default**: **Agent turns under way** (64): past it, starting another lets the oldest go, a turn that went stale; **Turns waiting at a gate** (256), across your chats: a turn that waits on you (the action or the outline shown before playing, a question asked first, the review card) keeps everything its agents had done, so answering it asks none of them again; past it, your oldest waiting turn forgets that, so when you answer it the turn asks its agents again where it still needs them, and a review gate's turn still plays its plan and lands what you let through, but its record no longer says which role made the changes from before the gate, and the narrator isn't told the lines its roles had; **Chats whose review cards are watched** (256): past it, the chat watched longest ago stops hearing about its cards until its page asks for them again; **Chats in the live view** (16): how many chats' latest agent turn GM → Live keeps, the one started longest ago let go past it (Live then shows nothing for it until its next turn, and its transcript stays in GM → Workspace); **Agent texts kept once read** (4,000,000 characters): the agents' stored texts (the main agent's session, the GM assistant's conversation, the workspace's files) kept in memory once read, so reading one again takes no trip to storage; past it the text read longest ago goes, and 0 reads every one from storage; and **Messages kept worked out** (65,536): how many of your chats' messages the engine keeps worked out, each with what it did to the world, so working a chat out again starts where it changed rather than from its first message; past it, the message used longest ago is worked out again when next needed. Keep it above your longest chat's messages. Each is at least 1 but **Agent texts kept once read**, which may be 0.
- **Throttles**, per connection: requests and tokens per minute, each off by default. A call waits until its connection's minute has room; only one bigger than a whole minute's tokens is refused.
- **Caching**: the page has every role's switch and **Bust**, then the agents with caching of their own, each opening its page; each agent's own caching is on its page. The stable part of an agent's prompt goes first, with cache breakpoints where the provider takes them (Anthropic's 4 per request, its connection's own prompt caching included: one each for its system messages, its last message and its automatic mode when they're on, and one for its tools however many there are, since Lumiverse marks only the last tool; that is staging Lumiverse's behaviour, which LumiRPEngine targets). **Bust** puts a new key at the top of the prompt, so the next call reads nothing from the provider's cache; off sends a new key every call, which also defeats a provider's automatic caching.
- **Acceptance runs**: none of your connections is guarded until you add one here; a connection you add starts marked **Only in acceptance runs**, with ceilings of 300,000 prompt and 30,000 answer tokens per run. A connection so marked is refused unless you've opened a run, here, with a label; an outside agent (the `lumirpengine` CLI or its MCP server) can neither open one nor change these settings: a settings change of its that would leave them otherwise, a reset of the whole Agents section included, is refused whole. A **Model Roulette** connection sends each call on one of its connections, picked at random, so an agent's call on one is refused while any of them is guarded here. An agent's call on a connection LumiRPEngine can't read (your default connection when it can't be read, or one whose settings can't be read, which might be such a roulette) goes ahead, counted against no guarded connection, and while any connection is guarded the log and your diagnostics say so, once for each such connection rather than on every call. While a run is open, each guarded connection's calls count against its ceilings for the run (prompt and answer tokens), and every call first holds its estimated prompt plus its max tokens, so one that would pass a ceiling isn't sent. A model that hasn't reported real usage yet holds its estimate times the **estimate factor** (2). The page and a line at the top of the drawer show what the run has spent, cached and held; **Close the run** keeps its report as the last run's. The guarded connections are kept with the runs, apart from your other settings, so a build from before the agents, which keeps no agent settings, never drops them: back on this one, they are guarded as you left them. If that file can't be read, or was damaged (it is kept aside, as `agent-acceptance.json.corrupt-…`) while your settings kept no copy of the list, agent calls go ahead under the list your settings file keeps (none, if it keeps none), and the log and your diagnostics say so once, naming that list. While the file can't be read no run counts as open, and each call tries the file again; a damaged one lasts until you set the list again here (add a connection, or remove one). Play itself goes on as before.
- **Against the steps engine** (2×), under acceptance runs: the target an acceptance comparison holds agent turns to: per role, an agent turn's tokens against the same scenario's turn on the steps engine, at most this many times. The engine doesn't compare turns itself or stop one past it; each turn's tokens, per role, are in **Settings → Diagnostics**, to compare against this. Any amount above 0; under 1, an agent turn should spend less than the steps engine.

**Settings → Agents → Roles** lists the roles of an agent turn in the order they commit (**Intent**, **Scene and lore**, **Minds**, **Knowledge**, **Director**, **Read**, **Curator**, **Memory**, **Skip beats**, **World steps**), one row each: where its tasks run, anything about how it runs that isn't its default (*one call, no tools*, what it does on a failure, *Off* for a Curator switched off in Pipeline), and how many of its settings are changed; **Main agent** and **Assistant** follow. A row opens the role's page, the same template for every role, the main agent and the assistant:
- **Runs on**: each of its tasks (Intent: *Reading your message*, *Judging your approach*, *A puzzle's answer*; Director: *People's choices*, *The brief and the outline*, *Suggested choices*; and so on) with the connection and model it actually runs on. With no connection of its own (the default), a role runs each task where that task's step runs (Settings → Steps and connections), else on the helper model, else on Lumiverse's default connection; the director's brief and outline run where the brief does, or where the outline does on a turn without one. Then **Connection and model** (*None: each task on its step's*, or a connection and, once picked, its model) and **Reasoning**.
- **What it may do**: its **Tools**, one chip per family, and **What it may change**: *Nothing*, *The story's changes* (the default) or *The GM's edits too*.
- **What it reads**: what **Settings → Agents → Context** sets for it (below): its **Perspective**, its **Sources** (each under **Each source**: *Starts with*, *When needed* or *Never*) and its **Pinned files**.
- **When it runs and fails**: **One call, no tools** (off: on, it makes its step's own call, with that step's prompt and answer format) and **When it fails**: *Run its step's one call* (the default), *Go on without it*, or *Stop the turn* (the Intent's default). The Curator's page says whether Pipeline runs it, and opens Pipeline.
- **Budget**: only the limits it sets of its own, each folded to its value with **Every agent's** to give it back; **Set a limit of its own** adds one more at the limit it follows now (a common number where that is no limit). The Curator's page has **Curator ops per turn** here, and its own **Tool rounds per agent** (6), which it ships with, so they aren't a change.
- **Caching**: its own switch and **Bust**; with caching of its own, **Default** follows every role's again.
- **Prompts**: opens its page on Prompts.

While anything on the page differs, **Reset this page** puts the whole role back: its settings, what it reads, its own caching (and the Curator's ops per turn). Whichever **When it fails** you pick, nothing a failed run wanted to change lands, and the main agent decides it for itself when it runs the turn; after the reply, *Stop the turn* stops reading the reply instead, which then waits unread with **Retry read**. Every role's tools read everything, each secret marked with whom it is known to and kept from: what a role works from is a hint on the **Context** page, never a screen.

**The Curator** keeps the world's things and places true to the reply on a chat on agents: who has what and whose it is, the rooms and spots the reply puts people in, the people who walk on, and what a purchase brings. It works after the reply, beside the read, which leaves all of that to it, from the GM's view with every secret marked, the turn's own changes in words and the Place, Gear and People sections (never minds), which list the scene's things and people by the names its tools read, so it names what they show and looks up only what they don't. Each change is one call of one of its tools: making a thing (it looks first, and is refused when the thing is already there), moving it, handing it over (to hold, as a loan, back, or for keeps), making it someone's, letting someone use it, where it's kept, using it up, wearing it, wearing it down, losing it, renaming it, merging two that are one; making or renaming a place; adding a walk-on; a purchase. Each is checked and staged with the turn, lands after the read's, and asks first or applies by the reply's types' modes ([Extraction modes](#extraction-modes)). Its rules, editable in **Prompts → Curator**, say: the reply is canon, change as little as makes the world agree; work from the sections, naming what they list as they name it; find before you make (a make looks first itself); make the changes as soon as you know them, several calls in one answer, lookups together before them; a handover keeps the owner unless the reply says given, sold, traded or kept; a thing too big to carry is placed, never held; say why; a hidden thing the reply reveals is found; make a place only when the reply puts someone there and nothing fits; when unsure, change nothing, and stop once the things are right. Its card on this page adds **Curator ops per turn** (24): past it, its changes are refused, and it is told so; and its budget ships with **Tool rounds per agent** of its own, 6 (every agent's is 10): when its rounds run out before it answers, what it staged and noted still lands, and Diagnostics warns that it stopped short. Taking a thing out of the world needs *The GM's edits too*. The read still gives the things, places, walk-ons and purchases it sees, but holds them aside for the Curator: they land only where the Curator's own work doesn't cover them, when its rounds ran out, its agent failed (beside its prompt-only read), or it never ran (a main agent that didn't run it), each once, never what the Curator staged already; while its work is whole, none of them lands. Switch the Curator off under **Pipeline** (**The Curator keeps the world's things and places**, on by default), and the read reads things, places, walk-ons and purchases itself, as classic does; prompt-only, it is one call of classic's reading of those alone.

**Settings → Agents → Context** is the overview of what each role starts with, and the guide the main agent briefs the roles by: one row per role with its perspective, what its prompt starts with (or *its task alone*), what it is never given, and its pinned files, with how many of those are changed. A row opens the role's page at **What it reads**, where they are set: its **perspective**, *Your character's*, *Everyone's, secrets marked*, *The GM's* or *Its character's own* (the character its task is about, as a mind works), a hint in its system prompt that never changes what its tools read; how it gets each source, the **Lore index** and each of the narrator's sections (the Scene, the Minds, the Outcome and the rest, as the narrator would get them then, secrets marked): **Starts with** (in its prompt from the start, in either pipeline), **When needed** (the default: the main agent may give it when the task needs it, and its tools can read it) or **Never** (the main agent is told never to give it); and its **pinned files**, workspace paths one a line, read whole within its pins budget before those `.pins.json` lists for it and those you pinned in GM → Workspace (a path the chat has no file at is passed over). By default the **Director**, which briefs and outlines the reply, starts with the **Scene** (with the scene role's lore and notes so far) and the **Minds**, and the **Curator** with **Place**, **Gear** and **People**, never the **Minds**; every other role starts with its task alone; the intent works from your character's perspective, the minds and the memory from their own character's, the Curator from the GM's, and the rest from everyone's. A role never gets less than this page gives it, whichever pipeline runs it; with a main agent deciding, the main agent is told every role's settings here and decides what else each is told.

**What a role is told.** Each role's agent gets its system prompt (its perspective in it), then your system instructions (AGENTS.md and its own roles/<role>.md), then its instructions with your preset's guidance for its steps (Presets), then its **pinned files**: those its **Context** pins, then those the workspace's `.pins.json` lists for it, then those you pinned in GM → Workspace, each whole within its pins budget (the `tokens` `.pins.json` gives it, else **Pinned files, per role** on the Main agent page), a longer one as its headings; then the lore index when its context starts with it, and its task with the sections its context starts it with. Every role gets its pinned files and its tools, the minds, the memory and a person's choice too. The director's brief and outline, and the suggested choices, are kept whole: a line that carries a secret, or names someone or something your character can't see, goes on marked as a secret kept from your character, never left out; the outline card shows it as a spoiler, and a suggestion that carries one is a spoiler chip. The scene's notes for the narrator all go to the Scene section: one that names someone your character can't see, or carries a secret, goes in marked as a secret, as a Minds line does (left out only while the narrator's **Secrets** setting leaves secrets out). What someone learns from your message before they choose (the knowledge step before the plan) is kept as after the reply: not at all while **What you learn (who knows what)** in **Settings → Changes from the reply** is *Never*. A role at *The story's changes* may write, move, pin or remove any workspace file, `.pins.json` included, which sets what every agent is shown first. On the review card a change to the agents' workspace reads as one line that names no file, as your Journal shows none. A change a role makes to a file under `learned/` or `~/` is made when its turn ends, and never by a turn you cancel.

**Keeping it lean.** What a role reads is the agents' to keep short, never a filter: the start of each prompt stays the same across a turn's calls, so the provider's cache takes it, and an agent can **condense** a long file, a turn's transcript or a lore entry into a short workspace note (one call of its own model, the note opening with what it came from), which lands with the turn and is read meanwhile by the turn's later runs; the main agent condenses for a role it names, so it can point a later task at the note.

**What the roles hold to.** The **intent** reads your message for what it is for, never its first verb: "I get up from the couch, stretch, and sit down next to Danny to ask how his shift went" is the talk with Danny, and getting up and stretching only lead up to it. The **director** briefs and outlines only what the turn's facts, your words and its lookups support, held to the turn as the engine planned it: who is here, who holds what, where things lie, and the engine's clock once your turn's actions are done, with how long they took. The engine then checks each of its lines against the turn as it started and as it ended: a line that has someone here whom the engine has elsewhere, someone gone who is here, someone holding or handing over a thing they don't have, or a thing lying where it isn't, both before your turn's actions and after them, is flagged (a beat whole, the brief a sentence at a time) and told with the run, and the narrator still gets it. A line that fits either stands unflagged, so your turn's own action is told as it happens: "Jordan hands Danny the pocketknife" on the turn you give it to him, or Danny nodding as you head for the door. The check only reads plain statements about people and things your character can see, so a phone call, a memory, a plan, an arrival or a goodbye never trips it. The **read** is told the engine's clock as the Scene tells it, when your message came and now, and measures the time the reply narrates from when your message came: "the church bell rings seven" lands the clock at seven, past what your turn's own actions took, and a time only mentioned (an appointment, an opening hour) moves nothing. It is also told that a thing the world doesn't have yet is made only when someone else hands it over, in the reply's words, or when it is bought.

**What a run gave.** After each run, the main agent is told what it answered and what it gave the engine: the changes it staged (landing when the turn commits, or held for the review card), any it was refused and why, and anything of its answer the engine didn't take, with why and how many: a knowledge run says how many facts and learnings it recorded and, for each kind of refusal, how many ("1 refused (no matters: a new fact says why it matters and to whom)"), a read says which proposals it didn't read and which things its changes name that the world doesn't have ("1 name a thing the world doesn't have (brass key): …"), each only a *Not tracked* note, a director run says which of its lines were flagged and why, each quoted ("1 flagged, still in the reply's prompt (it has someone here whom the engine has elsewhere: "Marcus Delgado leans on the counter beside Jordan.")"), and an answer with nothing in it says so. **Settings → Diagnostics** shows the same words as a record of the role, a warning when something wasn't taken, on the fixed pipeline too, and so does your `diagnostics.log`. The words are on the **Main agent** page of **Prompts**, under *What a run gave*.

**Settings → Agents → Main agent** is on the role page's template: **Runs on** (*Deciding each turn*, while the pipeline has a main agent decide, and *The final review*), its connection, model and reasoning; **What it may do** (its tools: it only reads); **What it reads** (Turn digest, Pinned files per role); **When it runs** (whether it decides turns, opening Pipeline); **Final review** (on or off, what a review that fails does, its time, and the limits the review sets of its own over the main agent's); **Budget** (its own limits only, with the picker); **Caching**; **Prompts**. Settings home's row says what it does now: on a fixed pipeline with the review on it *reviews the narrator's prompt*; it reads *not used* only on a fixed pipeline with the review off. Its pickers never offer **Fresh runs per request**, which only a turn role takes. It is read while **Pipeline** has a main agent decide, and in either pipeline for the final review (below): its **connection** and **model** (your default connection), its **effort** (*Connection's own*), the **tools it reads with** (*Workspace*; they read everything, secrets marked, as every role's do; it changes nothing itself, only through the roles it runs, the notes it condenses for them and its final review's edits of the narrator's prompt), its **turn task**: **Turn digest** (2,000 tokens of what changed since its last turn) and **Pinned files, per role** (2,000 tokens, every role's too where the workspace's pins set none), and its **budget** for each stage it works, every agent's until you set its own. Its system prompt is followed by your AGENTS.md and roles/main.md, and the start of its prompt carries every role's **Context** settings (its perspective, what it starts with, what it is never to be given, its pinned files and its tools), the guide it briefs them by; so does its final review's.

**The final review.** Before every reply of a chat on agents, whichever pipeline plays it, the main agent reviews the prompt the narrator is about to get, after the engine has made the turn's sections and before they go into the prompt: every section (the Outcome, the Scene, the people, money and the rest), the Outline with its brief, and the narrator rules. It checks each line against the world and the turn, and may fix it a line or a block of lines at a time: rewrite a line or a block (a lore entry the Scene tells whole, say), add lines at the end of a section, or take a line or a block out. Each fix answers with the lines it changed and two either side, numbered as the section now reads, and the review reads a section whole only when it asks to, so a review of many fixes stays short. The narrator gets the prompt as it leaves it, which is what Lumiverse's Prompt Breakdown shows for that reply, and GM → Live shows each edit (see [Live](#live)). On the fixed pipeline the main agent runs for this alone, on its connection and model here, with a digest of the chat so far and its pinned files; with a main agent deciding, the review is the start of its stage 6, and its session keeps it. There every stage, the review too, is offered the same tools (a tool that isn't the stage's own answers that it works elsewhere), so the review reads the session your connection's prompt cache holds instead of sending it all again. The turn's record keeps its edits, so a **Re-narrate** tells the narrator the same revised prompt with no model call, and a reply that failed tries again with the same prompt; a **Re-run** is reviewed afresh. Its settings are the **Final review** section of this page: **Review the narrator's prompt** (on), **When the review fails** (*Send the prompt unrevised*, the default, which leaves a Diagnostics record of why; or *Stop the turn*, which stops it before its reply with the chat's Retry), its time (**Final review** in Settings → Timeouts, 5 minutes, shown here too: a large model reviewing from a long session can think for two minutes before its first edit; settings saved with the old 2 minutes move to 5 once), and its own budget over the main agent's for a stage (each limit the main agent's until you change it). A review fails when it runs out of time or budget, or its model call fails; one that edits nothing isn't a failure. Turned off, nothing runs and the narrator gets the prompt as the engine made it. Its texts are on Settings → Agents → Prompts → Main agent, under Final review, each with its Reset.

**One run at a time.** The main agent may ask for several runs in one answer, and they work together, but each thing the engine asks of a role runs once at a time: asking for it again, or for a revision of it, while its run is still working is answered that it is running, so only the run that stands lands what it changed. A run whose request the turn goes on without lands nothing it changed, even when it had answered: when the time for reading the reply runs out before the main agent hands it over, when the main agent fails, or when the turn ends. **GM → Live** shows such a change as dropped, *the turn went on without its run*.

**What the main agent tells a role, and what its session keeps.** Its task and revisions for a run reach each of the run's tasks whole, whatever the task's perspective: what to tell a role is the main agent's to decide, guided by the **Context** page. A role run inside another's (a skip's minds at its beats, the knowledge step before a decision) is told none of it. Its session is read whole: every message of its own and the compaction summary. Only what it reads counts toward **Compact the session past** and is summarized, so a fixed pipeline's digest of its turns, which the main agent never reads (its turn task's digest stands in), is neither. A decision it runs again or revises adds what the people choosing learned first once, from the run whose answer stands.

**Settings → Agents → Assistant** is the GM assistant's own page (GM → Assistant), on the role page's template: **Runs on**, **What it may do** (how its changes land), **What it reads** (everything, outside the story; History tokens), **Budget** (its own limits only, with the picker; never Fresh runs per request), **Caching** and **Prompts** (the GM agent's page). Its **connection** and **model** are your default connection and its model unless you pick one; its **effort** (*Connection's own* by default: the connection's reasoning setting, else yours in Lumiverse; *Set by the model* for a model that picks its own; while **Thinking in agent tool loops** is off, a call that offers tools goes with reasoning off, whatever this says); **How its changes land**, *Each waits for your Apply* (the default) or *At once, with an Undo chip*; **History tokens** (20,000): how much of the conversation goes with each message, the newest first, the older messages left out and the assistant told how many; and its own **budget**: each limit above is every agent's until you change it here, for the assistant alone, and **Every agent's** follows that one again. Nothing here limits what it reads: it works outside the story and reads everything (see [Assistant](#assistant)). The assistant runs outside every turn, so a limit a turn would set when it is none (a call's time, the tokens in and out, the run's time) is no limit at all here, and the page says so. Its texts are on **Prompts**, page GM agent.

## Character minds

With **character minds** on, the people near the story think for themselves. Each one's mind is its own model call, made as that character and scoped to what they know. By default a mind reads the story as it stands: who they are (their own fields, agendas and secrets), where they are and whom they see there, the lore pinned to them and the lore the turn names, how the latest reply ended and what you say; their usual routine, as context they may weigh; and their own plans, so they keep to what they agreed. Their own memory and life (their summary, notes and what they saw, last thoughts, the turns they recall, their money, today as the planner lays it, and their commitments) they read only if you switch them on in **What a mind reads** ([Settings → Minds](#settings--minds)). Minds are blind to each other, so no mind decides what another character says or does, and none reads what another character's memory says it told them. A mind answers with an inner monologue, a **depiction** (how they come across), what they intend and their reactions, and the narrator is told how to depict it. After the reply, the concrete changes (money, moves) are still read from what the reply actually says, and the characters' memory is written from it.

A character's **plans** come only from what the story states: after the reply, their memory takes the plans the turn sets out (something said aloud, agreed with someone, or scheduled in the story as written), never a guess at what they might do. A mind makes none, since it sees what its character knows, not where the story is going. A plan moves them at its time, may ask you along, and can stop a time skip. In a chat from before, plans a mind made stay in GM mode as thoughts, and do none of this.

A plan with other people in it is **one plan they all share**: "Danny and Priya agree on dinner at the Anchor at seven" reaches both of them (and anyone else in it), whoever's memory took it from the turn, and a second memory of the same turn doesn't add it again. Each of them lives it as their own day allows: someone who lives their own day goes there at its time, and anyone else (someone who doesn't, or a Background character) lets it pass. You're never given a copy: your plans are yours to make, and when their plan asks you along, one of them invites you once. **Accept invitation** books it in your character's day: an appointment at the meeting's time and place (when you know the place), as long as their plan or routine block for it lasts, under the host's name. A Timelapse that follows your routine takes you there, and accepting again books nothing more. A payment or a loan stays the payer's own.

A plan to pay or lend someone money is carried out by the money engine: at the plan's time, the amount goes from the character to the person the plan is with, from their own money as they'd pay anything, and the plan is done. If they can't cover it, nothing moves and the plan is dropped. Their journal says only **Payment** or **Loan**, never what they had in mind.

Each mind is recorded on the turn, so swipes and branches keep it: **Re-narrate** reuses the turn's minds, **Re-run** asks again, and Playtest and Check world never call a model for them.

### The fidelity ladder

Every turn, and at each beat of a time skip, each character gets a focus score and a level from it:

- **Live**: the closest to the story. Only people at hand are Live: in your scene, or named this turn. Anyone else is Near at most, however much of the story they carry.
- **Near**: they think again when something calls for it: they come into the scene, they are named, or their mind is old enough (the Near refresh).
- **Far**: they think now and then, on the Far refresh period, and their plans shape their day if they live their own day.
- **Background**: no mind; they follow their routine.

Who thinks follows your scene and what's in motion:

- **Everyone in your scene** thinks every turn, best on the ladder first, up to your Live limit. Your scene is where you are, or a place up to two levels inside or around it, as long as nothing between is spread out (a town or a region: a place whose parts a drive, a boat or a walk of more than ten minutes joins, or whose parts short walks don't all join, such as a town whose homes and sites have no routes between them). Someone the story put on the fourth floor is with you in the bath of a suite on it, even in a building drawn room by room with short walks between; someone in the room next door, somewhere else in a big building, or somewhere in town, isn't.
- **Someone elsewhere** thinks only while they're caught up in something that could come up soon: a plan or appointment with you within the next day, an invitation to you, an agenda of their own under way, your intent for them in GM mode, a place or activity the story holds them to, or their interrupting a time skip. They think on their level's refresh, or every turn when they're Live, and what they thought is on record, though it never shows in the Minds section. Being named, idling or going about their routine makes no one think.
- A mind that falls due while nothing calls it still keeps the level it fell due at, so a character's routine role never changes with who thinks.
- **Minds think while the engine reads your message**, not after it, so they no longer add their time to the wait before the reply. If your message turns out to be a time skip, the minds started for it stop (a time skip's minds think at its moments instead), and Diagnostics lists their calls as not needed. With the action preview on, they think while the preview waits: **Play** goes straight on with them, and **Cancel** stops them.
- **No mind thinks while nothing would read it**: when you switch the Minds section off for a chat (**GM → This chat → Sections**) and **Their last thoughts** is off in **What a mind reads**, no mind is asked (they're still marked as due, so nothing about who thinks changes when you switch it back on). A refresh you ask for in GM mode still runs.

The score adds up proximity, being named, their feelings toward you, how they figure in the story (quests, secrets, agendas, story roles, rules that name them), what is pending between you (invitations, appointments, plans, commitments), their tier and how recently you saw them. The world sets the weights and thresholds, and who is always Live or never gets a mind ([Character minds in a world](#character-minds-in-a-world)); **GM → Minds** can pin anyone at a level.

### Memory

Each character remembers in four layers: a **witness log** of what they saw happen or were told; **recall** of the turns they were present for (and only those: what was narrated of other people's private thoughts is ignored); **notes** written after a reply, for the levels you pick, which fade with time unless they matter; and **commitments** (promises, debts, grudges, favours, plans with someone) that stay until they are kept, broken or released. Faded and surplus notes can be folded into the character's **summary**, at most once a period for each character and for at most two characters a turn. The folding runs beside the turn's memory notes, on what the character held before the turn, so the reply's reading takes as long as the longer of the two; when a character's memory rewrites their summary that turn, their folding is asked again afterwards, into the new summary. It runs only while something reads what it folds: the memory step after the reply (on for a level in **Memory notes after the reply**), or minds that read **Their summary** or **Their notes and what they saw**. The world sets the defaults; your Settings can override any of them.

### Settings → Minds

**Settings → Minds** opens with a line on what minds are and the **Character minds** switch. Off, no character gets a mind, and the engine's own rules decide invitations as before. On, these sections follow:

- **How many think**: **Live minds a turn**, a number or **no limit**; and **Near and Far refreshes a turn**, how many Near and Far minds may think in one turn besides the Live ones, the most pressing first, or **no limit**. A refresh you ask for in GM mode always runs.
- **Near refresh**: when they come into the scene or it changes; when the latest message or reply names them; every so many turns; when their mind is so many game minutes old. Each number can be turned off.
- **Far refresh**: every so many game minutes, or never.
- **Memory notes after the reply**: a switch for each level (**Live characters who heard you**, and so on) that gets the memory step. On a turn that takes you somewhere else, the people you leave remember it, and the people you meet there don't, since they weren't there to hear it.
- **What a mind reads**: each source a mind may read, with its switch and, while it's on, its limit in tokens, which can be **no limit**. Within its limit a source comes whole, and nothing else cuts it. Who they are, where they are and whom they see, and what they mean to do are always in it.
  - **The story as it stands**, on by default: **Lore pinned to them and to where they are** (no limit); **Lore the turn activates**, the world's entries your message or the latest reply names by a key, an alias, a title or a lookup-table term, as World Info activates them (1,600 tokens); **The latest reply**, how it ended, for those who were there for it (the whole reply; with a number they read its end); and **Your message** (all of it).
  - **What they know**, on by default: **What they know: their own holdings**, while the chat tracks who knows what: the facts they hold and how they came by them, in place of the secrets in who they are. Its limit is **What they know, for each mind (tokens)** from **Knowledge** below, which this row shows and sets too. With **Track who knows what** off it says it reads nothing unless a chat turns tracking on.
  - **The chat as you see it**, off by default: **Recent chat**, the latest messages before the turn as you see them in the chat (hidden JSON blocks left out), oldest first, up to **Recent chat (messages)** and **Recent chat (tokens)**, whichever comes first (8 messages and 3,000 tokens when you turn it on; either can be **no limit**). It includes turns the character wasn't there for: it's the chat as you see it, not what they know. A mind that reads **The latest reply** doesn't get that reply twice: Recent chat gives the messages before it. **Character memory**, **Memory consolidation** and **Time-skip beats** read it too while it's on, with the same limits, and so does the time skip's plan for those whose skip the model plans; their step cards have no Recent chat of their own. The **Context** panel counts the messages each mind was given.
  - **What they usually do and mean to do**, on by default: **Their usual routine** (400 tokens, in whole lines): their routine blocks and their own place and activity schedules, as the world and the story set them, while their routine isn't off ([Routines and schedules](#routines-and-schedules)). It's what they usually do, theirs to weigh, never what they must do: a private block only when it's their own, and never the day the planner lays or the friends it fills free time with. **Their plans** (400 tokens, in whole lines): their open plans, soonest first, with when, where and who else is in it, from what the story's turns stated and what you set in GM mode. A plan they share with others is each one's: in its own words for the one whose memory took it from the turn (and for everyone, once you write its words in GM mode), and for the others as its kind, time, place and people, since one character's memory never speaks for another's. The **Context** panel says how many lines of routine and how many plans each mind got, and when some were left out over the limit.
  - **Their own memory and life**, off by default: **Their summary**, **Their notes and what they saw**, **Their last thoughts**, **Turns they were there for** (its limit is recall's tokens, a memory setting) and **Their life: money, the day and commitments** (today as the day planner lays it). Each can carry what the story never set up: the planner fills a free evening with dinners and friends, and a commitment their memory took from narration that showed another character's mind isn't theirs. Switched on, each is read within its limit; what another character's memory says it told them never reaches a mind.
  - **Their things**, on by default (300 tokens, in whole lines), listed with their own after their life: what they have on them, what they hold for someone else and since when, what of theirs someone else has ("Jordan Ellis has your truck keys (since Tue 2:00 PM)", or lent until when), and what of theirs is missing. A thing taken from them reads as taken, with whoever has it unknown to them: a mind never learns who stole it from this.
  - Settings saved before **What a mind reads** keep their limits: the latest reply's allowance, and, if you set the Character minds step's lore tokens yourself (a **Custom** preset), those as both lore limits; a preset's own lore tokens give way to the defaults above. The memory step after the reply reads the turn within the latest reply's and your message's limits.
- **Advanced: the Minds section budget and time skips**, folded away: **Minds section budget (tokens)**, the narrator's Minds section's own budget, apart from the section budget, so minds never crowd out Scene or People (when the minds don't all fit, the characters lowest on the ladder are left out of it first, and the Stance section tells of them instead); and under **Time skips**, **Beats a skip may stop at** and **Minds at each beat**, either of which can be **no limit**.

Three sections are there whether minds are on or off:

- **Knowledge**: who knows what. The knowledge step runs after each reply, once the reply's reading is in (it reads what the reading found), so it runs whether character minds are on or off. It rides on the story: what happens in a scene is known to everyone who was there through the chat itself, so it records only the few facts, usually none and at most one or two, that would change what someone does, says, hides, or believes about another person (a secret, a revelation or a lie; something a person would act on, hide, trade, or be hurt or helped by; a promise or debt; an open question someone is pursuing). Something said or done in plain view is recorded only when it is one of these, so a revelation or a promise made aloud still is; the story holds the rest, and small talk, advice and description never are. Each new fact says why it matters and to whom, which **GM → Minds** shows beside it; one that doesn't say is left out, and the log says so. A fact is made once: each call of the step is shown the facts already in play and, with **One call per character**, those the calls before it made this turn, and is told to have whoever learns one of them, however it is worded, learn that fact rather than make it again. Should the step say one again anyway (and so may a later turn), worded the same but for case, spacing or punctuation, it is the fact already there, not a new one, so it counts once against the limits below, keeps its first wording and reason, and whoever learns it later simply joins those who hold it. What happens in front of everyone reaches everyone there, your character included, unless the story shows someone missed it (a whisper out of earshot, someone asleep). Someone there for only part of a turn (who arrives during it, is met at its end, or leaves partway) witnesses none of it, and learns what happened in it only when someone tells them.
  - **Track who knows what**: on, each character holds the facts they know; after each reply the knowledge step works out who learned what, minds read what they know, the narrator gets **Who knows what**, and Play shows **What you know**. A chat's own switch in **GM → This chat** wins. While it's on, the memory step no longer records who told whom what: the knowledge step records telling.
  - **Suspicion threshold (%)**, in steps of 5: a fact someone holds less surely than this is a suspicion. A chat takes a new threshold from its next turn.
  - **The knowledge step**: **One call per scene** (the default) works out everyone who could perceive the turn at once; **One call per character** asks about each of them alone, a call each, one after another, so each is shown the facts the ones before it made.
  - **Limits**: **What they know, for each mind (tokens)**, **Who knows what, for the narrator (tokens)**, **Who knows what: facts per person** and **The knowledge step's context (tokens)**; under **More limits**, **A fact's claim (characters)**, **Facts made in play, per chat**, **People who may hold one fact**, **New facts a turn** (3 by default: most turns need none), **Learnings a turn** and **A fact stays in play for (turns)**. Each is a whole number with a line on what it limits. A new fact counts against the turn's and the chat's limits only once someone learns it. A refusal names the limit it hit. **Who knows what** tells the narrator a fact at a time (who holds it, and who with you doesn't know it), the most relevant first, a secret before anything else; a fact everyone here holds alike, you included, with no one keeping it from anyone, is one line, "Everyone here: …". Over its limit it leaves out whole facts from the end, never part of one.
  - **Texts**: the words knowledge sends a model besides its own prompt, folded by where they appear: **What a mind reads**, **How a fact someone holds reads**, **The narrator's Who knows what** (including **What everyone here holds alike**) and **The knowledge step's context** (including **Facts this turn already made**, **Reusing a listed fact**, which tells the step that a fact it lists is one fact however it is worded, and **What the turn can still record**, the new facts, learnings and room in the chat it has left). Each starts from the shipped text with a **Shipped** or **Your own** badge, and **Save**, **Revert** and **Reset**. Placeholders such as `{{claim}}` must stay: a wording that leaves one out can't be saved. Saving the shipped text unchanged, or an empty one, keeps no copy.
- **Memory**: **Your overrides**, folded away, with a badge counting the ones you set. Each of the world's memory settings (see [Character minds in a world](#character-minds-in-a-world)) says "The world's applies (built in: …)" until you set your own; then it shows a **Yours** badge and **Use the world's**, which goes back to the world's.
- **Prompts**: a row for each minds step (Character minds, Character memory, Time-skip beats, Memory consolidation, Who knows what), with a **Built-in** or **Your own** badge. Open it for the text, with **Save**, **Revert** and **Reset to the default**; the built-in text is under **The built-in default**, inside it. **Save** keeps your own; **Reset to the default** drops it. Saving the built-in text unchanged keeps no copy, so you get later improvements to it. Your own prompt wins over a world's own step template for that step, and the world's template over the built-in. **Who knows what** is the knowledge step's own prompt.

The **Model use** presets set minds too: **Lean** turns them off; **Standard** runs up to 3 Live minds and 2 refreshes a turn, with memory notes for Live characters; **Full** has no limits, with notes for Live and Near. Picking a preset resets everything above but what a mind reads, knowledge, your prompts and your memory; changing any of the rest makes the preset **Custom**. The Minds section's own budget is 1,200 tokens on Lean, 2,400 on Standard and 4,000 on Full, and a mind recalls up to 4,000 tokens of past turns unless the world or your memory overrides say otherwise (either can say **no limit**). Where each minds step runs (its connection, model, effort and max tokens) is in **Settings → Steps and connections**, as for the other steps; what a mind reads, its lore included, is set here, and the memory, time-skip and consolidation steps read only the lore pinned to them in Build. A minds step you haven't set there shows **On**, on the helper model with 8,192 max tokens, because that is how it runs; only its **Enabled** switch turns it off. **Time-skip beats** is the exception: it shows **Off**, and runs only once you turn it on. Minds' calls aren't counted in the presets' step counts above: a turn makes one call per mind that runs, and after the reply one memory call per character who heard your message, at a level you picked, and now and then a consolidation.

### Minds in GM mode

**GM → Minds** starts with the chat's time (**Now**) and **This world's secrecy**, then **The player character**, whose row opens what your own character knows (they have no mind: you decide for them), then **Search by name** and a level filter (**Every level**, or one level). Below, every character but you, closest on the ladder first: their name, their level, when they last thought (or **No mind yet**), a **Pinned**, **Refresh** or **Intent** badge, and their score. Tap someone for their page, with a back button to **Minds**:

- **Fidelity**: their level and score, and each thing that added to it. Under **Pin and refresh**, **Pin their fidelity** opens a page that keeps them at a level in this chat whatever their score (**By score** unpins); its button stays off until you pick something other than what's in force. The author's pin, if any, has its own row. **Refresh their mind next turn** opens a page that asks for a fresh mind on the next turn, whatever the refresh settings say.
- **Your intent for them**: what they mean to do now, as you set it. Its page has **What they mean to do**, in your own words (up to 600 characters), and **How they go about it**: **Their mind decides** (the default), **Reactive** (they answer the scene and what they're asked, and start nothing of their own) or **Proactive** (they pursue their own aims without waiting to be asked). Either can be set alone. Their own mind reads it first, right after who they are, and works from it, so the story follows what they then intend; it is never cut to fit, and no other character's mind, the narrator or anything in Play ever sees it. It stays until you change it: emptying both, or **Clear intent**, clears it, and its edit's **Undo** brings it back. Swipes keep it, as they keep a pin.
- **Latest mind**: when and why it ran, where they were, their mood, monologue, depiction, intent and reactions, and whether it interrupted a time skip. A **To the narrator** badge marks what the narrator got under the world's secrecy, and a warning says when a depiction was kept from the narrator because it gave something away.
- **What they know**: each fact they hold, with how they came by it (**From the start**, **Witnessed**, **Told by** someone, **Inferred** or **Guessed**) and since when, why it matters and to whom (**Matters:**, as the knowledge step said it; a fact from the world, or one recorded before the step said so, has none), what they make of it and how sure they are, whom they keep it from, and what it rests on: another fact they hold, or the line of their witness log itself. A **Suspicion** badge marks one held less surely than the threshold in **Settings → Minds → Knowledge** (70% by default), and a fact that is false or an open question says so. Each row opens its page: **How they came by it**, **Who told them** (a told one needs someone), **What they make of it**, **How sure (percent)**, **Whom they keep it from** (**No one**, **Everyone** or **Some people**) and **What it rests on**, then **Save**, and **Delete holding** at the bottom. **Add a holding** gives them a fact the chat already holds that they don't. Your own character's page has the same list. Each change has its edit's **Undo**, and swipes and Re-runs keep it as they keep any edit.
- **Plans**, **Commitments** and **Memory notes**: each row opens the item's own page to edit it, with **Delete plan** (or **Delete commitment**, **Delete note**) at the bottom, and **Add a plan**, **Add a commitment** and **Add a note** open a page for a new one. A plan has what it is, its kind, when it starts (hours from now) and how long it lasts, where, with whom, a priority from 1 (whenever it fits) to 5 (it must happen) and its status. A plan with others in it is theirs too: adding, editing or deleting it on anyone's page does it for everyone in it (each keeps their own status), someone you take out of it loses it, and someone you add gets it. A plan to **Pay someone** or **Lend money** also has an amount, shown beside its kind, and needs someone it is with: the money goes to the first of them at the plan's time. A plan their mind made in a chat from before shows a **Thought** badge and "a thought from their mind, which drives nothing": it moves no one, pays nothing and stops no skip, and isn't counted among the open plans. A commitment has its kind, its text, who it is with, an optional due time (hours from now) and its status. A note has its text and a salience from 1 to 5, and shows how much it has faded.
- **Summary** and **Witnessed**: what they have folded into their summary, and what they saw or were told.
- **Run their mind now** asks for their mind at once, and **Pinned lore** shows what is pinned to them and to where they are, with **Edit in Build** to change it.

Above the list, **Run minds now** has **Run everyone Live and Near (…)**, which says how many minds it runs and asks first (**Run … minds** or **Cancel**), since each is a model call made at once, and **Choose who to run**: tap people in the list to pick them, or use the bar under the list, which stays above GM mode's tabs, whose **Select all** picks everyone the list shows (its search and level filter as they stand) and a button for each level under **Select a level** exactly the characters at that level, then **Run their minds**. A run asks each mind from the chat so far and their lore, as a turn would (your World Info as it is now, each entry kept as secret as a turn keeps it), four at a time. Its card shows each mind as it goes, **Waiting**, **Thinking**, **Recorded** or **Failed** with why (the time ran out, the call failed, the answer was empty or cut off), with its time, tokens and the pinned lore it read; **Cancel the run** stops it and records nothing. When it ends, what came back is recorded as one edit, and **Undo this run** takes it back. A run between turns reads how the latest reply ended; a run while a reply is being written lands when it finishes. It stops at your **Minds run on demand** limit in **Settings → Timeouts**: 10 minutes unless you change it, or no limit.

Each change is a GM edit like any other: one correction with its own **Undo**. Minds stay in GM mode: nothing a mind thinks reaches Play, the Journal, People or chips. **GM → This chat → Context** lists each mind call with the pinned lore it read and whether any was cut.

### Routines in GM mode

**GM → Routines** shows the routines and schedules everyone follows in this chat. A routine or a schedule is never a script. By default it only informs: the story and planning decide what people do, and only Background characters keep an enforced routine. Even then the story leads whoever shares a scene with you, and no routine interrupts play. The world's routines and schedules are edited in Build (**The world's routines are in Build** goes there); what you change here is this chat's alone.

The list has everyone but you, closest on the minds ladder first, with **Search by name** and a filter (everyone, **Changed in this chat**, **Routine leads**, **Story leads; routine informs**, **No routine**). Each row shows their fidelity and how much their routine binds them, from their routine's role, and a gold badge when this chat changed their routine or schedules: **Changed by you**, **Changed by the story** (a reply), **Changed from your message** (a routine your message stated, added with **Yes**), or more than one ("Changed by you and the story"). Tap someone for their page:

- **How much their routine binds them**, from the role it has for them (the role of their level, as the world sets it, unless this chat sets theirs): **Story leads; routine informs** when it's informative (the story and planning decide what they do, and their routine is only context for their mind and the narrator) or enforced while the planner doesn't lay their day (it needs an agent who acts, at Cast life 3 or higher); **Routine leads** when it's enforced and the planner lays their day; and **No routine** when its role is off or their routine is off in this chat. The lines under it say what the role does, where it comes from and, for an enforced routine, what the world's awareness setting does for them. **In this chat**, **Their routine's role**, **Story and lore awareness** and **In a time skip** open one page with all four: **Follows their routine**, **Follows it, no invitations** or **No routine**; **Their level's default** (naming it), **Informative**, **Enforced** or **Off**; **Their level's default**, **Stay put**, **Follow their routine** or **Planned by the model** ([Routines and schedules](#routines-and-schedules)); and **As the world has it**, **Story first**, **Balanced** or **Routine first**. **Save for this chat** makes it one edit; **Use the world's settings** clears what you set.
- **What the story holds now**: a place or activity the story established, which their routine yields to, and for how long.
- **Planned for the skip**, once a time skip has been planned: what the model planned for them in the current or last skip (**This skip** or **The last skip**), each block with where, how far into the skip and for how long, and the plan's note when the call failed or was skipped. Whenever no block covered them, they kept to their sleep and meals.
- **Their routine**: each block with when and where it happens, whose it is (their own, a group's, a kind's, a selector's, the world's default, the basic needs, or **Changed in this chat**), what it rests on (authored, a lore entry, a sheet field, the story or you, with how long ago) and what it replaces. Tap a block to change it for this chat with the same form as Build's, **Switch it off for them**, or, for a change this chat made, **Use the world's block** (or **Remove the block** when only this chat has it). The block's page shows the lore entry's quote, with **Open the entry** in Build. **Add a block for this chat** adds one only this chat has.
- **Their schedules**: each schedule that applies to them, the most specific owner first for each thing schedules set, with what it rests on, and **In force**, **Shadowed** (a more specific schedule wins where both run) or this chat's change. Tap one to give them **Their own version** in this chat, with Build's schedule form, or put it **Off for them** until you clear it or for some hours; **Use the world's schedule** drops the change.
- **Today**: their day as the planner laid it, each block with where, why it is there (their routine, a schedule, a plan, an appointment, a trip, an invitation, free time) and the reasons it gave.
- **Invitations**: the ones they made and received, each with where it came from: a plan the story made, a block of their routine (with its source), you, or one recorded before invitations needed a ground.
- **Changes made in play**: each block or schedule a reply or your own message changed in this chat, with where it came from (**The story** for a reply, **From your message** for one you said **Yes** to), the turn it came from (its id shows under **Show ids**) and how long ago. A block added this way is in force from the next day, and its row says **In force from the next day** until then. Tap one in force to keep it, change it or go back to the world's.

Each change is a GM edit like any other: one correction with its own **Undo**, and the list's badge follows it.

## Extraction modes

After the narrator writes, the engine reads the reply for changes the story implies. Each kind of change is a **type**, and each type has a **mode**.

| Mode | What happens |
|---|---|
| **auto** | The change is checked and applied, and an **Undo** chip appears. |
| **confirm** | A chip asks you first, with **Apply** and **No**. If you ignore it, it expires when you send your next message and nothing is applied. |
| **never** | The change is ignored. |

| Type in Settings (as a Mode change chip names it) | What it covers | Starting mode |
|---|---|---|
| **Time passing** (Time skips) · `time_advance` | The story jumps ahead ("three hours later"). Up to [Skips without asking](#skips-without-asking) (a day by default) applies with the world caught up (under confirm, when you tap **Apply**), whether or not the clock is paused or a fight is under way; a longer jump becomes a **Run timelapse** chip in either mode, and one of more than 366 days runs at most 366 days at a time, each step ending with **Continue timelapse**. No time the story tells is dropped | auto |
| **Moving somewhere** (Moves) · `location` | Someone moves. Moves are story first: a move the reply tells lands even where no route leads, whatever the world's travel simulation says (it holds only **Go to** to the routes), and of anyone, a body included. The reading is offered the places you can see by name, the ones the reply and your message name first, then the nearest, so a room deep inside a big building is named as readily as the town; a move it gives by a place's name goes to the place by that name you know, the nearest one when two share it. A reply that sends someone to their usual spot ("Danny heads out to his usual lunch place") moves them to the place their routine has for now, as you could know it: never a private part of their routine or a place you haven't found, and never someone the reply doesn't send anywhere. A reply that has you somewhere you know of, other than where the engine has you, without telling the move (it takes it as given) gets an **Apply** chip for your move there, whatever the mode (none on **never**): a place the story only implies may be a flashback, a phone call or a misread, so you stay where you are until you tap **Apply**, and your answer doesn't count toward learning. None when your own action this turn took you elsewhere: what you do comes before what the reply takes as given. A move the engine can't keep gets a **Not tracked** receipt in either mode: a move of someone you don't know, or to a place you don't know or the world doesn't have. It shows the reply's words as written and an id only as "someone you don't know" or "a place you don't know" ("Not tracked: Someone you don't know went to a place you don't know"), the same whether the reply made them up or the world keeps them from you | auto |
| **New places the story brings in (a room, a booth, a new bar)** (New places) · `place_make` | The reply puts someone in a room, a spot or a new place the world doesn't have, and says what it's in ("slides into the back booth at the Rusty Anchor"): the place is made there, found first if the world has one by that name there (so "the booth" next turn is that booth), and they move in. A new venue in town asks first unless you pin the type. A reply makes at most the world's places per reply (2) and a chat the world's places per chat (64), both in **Build → World**; one past either gets a **Not tracked** receipt | auto |
| **Conditions (hurt, tired…)** (Condition changes) · `condition` | A status changes (tired, injured, soaked) | auto |
| **Learning a fact** (New facts) · `knows` | You learn of a public person, thing or place the reply shows you, or hear of a place you didn't know (never a secret one). A fact told only in words, or one naming someone or somewhere you don't know, has no place in the engine: it gets a **Not tracked** receipt, the words as the reply wrote them ("You learn: The ferry stops running at nine.") and an id you don't know only as "something you don't know", and the story keeps it | confirm |
| **Where things are (picked up, put down, put away, fetched)** (Things moved, picked up or put down) · `item_move` | Something is picked up, put down, put into something or somewhere, or shown in someone's hands: the thing the reply's words mean, found where the story has it. A thing of yours left elsewhere that the reply has you use is **brought along**, with a receipt saying where it was. A built-in thing moves only when you tap **Apply** | auto |
| **Handing things over (holding, lending, giving back)** (Things handed over, lent or given back) · `item_hand` | Someone hands a thing over to hold, lends it, or gives it back, and it stays whose it was. Holding it lets the receiver use it, unless they could already (a mechanic handed the shop's tool at the shop) | auto |
| **Whose things are (gifts, sales, trades)** (Things changing owner) · `item_own` | A thing becomes someone else's: a gift ("Keep the jacket"), a sale, a trade, or the reply saying whose it is now | confirm |
| **New things the story brings in** (New things) · `item_make` | The story acts on a thing the world doesn't have yet (picks it up, puts it away, hands it over, uses it), so it is made where the story has it, whose the story makes it. One that costs more than the world's claim cap or is bigger than an armful asks first, unless you pin the type | auto |
| **Using things up** (Things used up) · `item_use` | Some or all of a thing is used up | auto |
| **Putting on or taking off** (Things put on or off) · `item_wear` | Something is put on or taken off | auto |
| **Wear and damage to things** (Wear and damage) · `item_condition` | A thing wears, breaks or is mended, as far as the reply says | auto |
| **Losing things, or having them stolen** (Things lost or stolen) · `item_lose` | A thing is lost, or stolen into someone's hands while it stays its owner's | confirm |
| **Purchases** · `purchase` | A narrated purchase, priced from the catalog. When the seller has the thing (you sell Marcus your old guitar), that same thing changes hands and the price goes to the seller, with no sales tax. Something bought that's too big to carry goes to the buyer's home, never into their hands | confirm |
| **Money transfers** (Payments) · `transfer` | Money moves between people, or between someone's own accounts | confirm |
| **Feelings toward others** (Feelings) · `regard` | Someone's feelings change, as far as the reply says, each feeling staying between −100 and 100 | confirm |
| **Quantities** · `quantity` | A count changes (ammunition, supplies) | confirm (no starting mode of its own; Settings shows *From the field's bound*) |
| **New people** (New characters) · `spawn` | A new character appears (up to the world's limit, 24 by default). A name someone you know already has is that person, so no one is added and a **Not tracked** receipt says so; a thing introduced as a character gets one too. A name only someone you don't know has is left out without a word, so it never tells you such a person exists | confirm |
| **Life and death (dying, dead, collapsed…)** (Life and death) · `life` | Someone dies, is dying, collapses or comes round (what you already knew is left out) | confirm |
| **Routines (a new job, a habit, a move…)** (Routine changes) · `routine` | The reply says someone's week has changed for good: a new job or shift, a new habit, one they gave up, or a move to a new home. It changes their routine in this chat from the next day (what they gave up, and a move, at once), and GM → Routine lists it among the changes the story made, with its turn. Only someone the reply names (or you), at places and with people you know of; a habit only mentioned, or what is already their routine or schedule, changes nothing, and so does a new shift that clashes with their schedule unless the reply says it takes its place. A character's thoughts never count | auto |
| **Votes on organizations' motions (for, against, abstaining)** (Votes on motions) · `vote` | Someone votes on an open motion you are in, in so many words; applied, the vote is cast, and a vote that carries the motion carries out what it decides. Never one they lean toward or promise | confirm |
| **Answers to requests for approval (approved, denied, withdrawn)** · `approval` | Someone answers a request for approval you asked or may answer; applied, an approval does the act then. Only someone up its chain of command may approve or deny it, and only its requester withdraws it | confirm |
| **What you learn (who knows what)** · `learn` | While the chat tracks who knows what, the knowledge step after the reply finds your character learning a fact that matters: seeing or hearing it in front of them (unless the story shows they missed it), or being told it. What your character makes of things is yours, so it never has them work something out or doubt it. Applied, it shows in **Story → What you know**, and the narrator's **Who knows what** tells it as known to your character. The reply's own reading never proposes it | auto |
| **Secrets the story reveals to you** · `reveal` | While the chat tracks who knows what, the knowledge step finds the story revealing a secret to your character. Applied, the secret opens on your screens (its quest in **Quests**, and **What you know**). The reply's own reading never proposes it | confirm |

Beyond the mode, every field has a limit on how much a single change may be, and **the strictest rule always wins**. Fields a world marks as locked are never offered to the model. A reply that tells this turn's own action again is read as that action, never as a second change. A purchase takes in the seller's handover and the payment, so one sale is not applied three times, and a payer has to be an account.

The strict reading rules are: time jumps only for an explicit jump or stated duration; knowledge only for a public person, thing or place you newly learn of, never a sentence; feelings only when the prose states a change; handovers only between two characters; routines only for a lasting change the prose states, never a one-off, a plan, a wish or a habit only mentioned.

**Things.** The reading names things in the reply's own words, and the engine finds the thing they mean: one your character can see before one they can't. When the words fit more than one, a **Which one?** chip offers each, and **No**. A thing hidden in the scene that the words name is found, never made twice. A change naming someone you don't know gets a **Not tracked** receipt, unless its type is *Never*. Pins you set before these types existed carry over to them once: picking up and putting down to **Where things are**, handovers to **Handing things over**, and using up, wearing and losing to theirs; picking up and putting down pinned differently ask (*Confirm*).

**Settings → Changes from the reply** also sets what the reading is told and keeps of things, under **Things in the reply**:

- **Things the read is shown** (40, from 1 to 200): how many things the reading is told of, each with whose it is, its size and where it's kept: those the reply or your message names first, then those around your character, then your character's own.
- **A change's why, at most** (120 characters, from 20 to 500): the longest reason a change to a thing keeps, as the reply gave it.
- **Things made from a reply, at most** (6, from 0 to 500): how many things one reply may bring into the world. One more gets a **Not tracked** receipt instead; 0 makes none.

### Learning on your own model

The starting modes come from measurements on a small model. Your model may do better or worse, so the engine **learns from how you answer**, on each connection separately, with no extra model calls:

- Each time you confirm or reject a change, or undo an automatic one, that counts. Something applied automatically and not undone by the next turn also counts as accepted. An ignored confirm chip counts nothing, and so does your answer to the **Apply** chip for a move a reply only implies (see **Moving somewhere** above).
- When a `confirm` type reaches **90% accepted over its latest 30 outcomes** on a connection, it flips to `auto` there, and a **Mode change** chip tells you ("Purchases now apply automatically") with an **Undo** button.
- When an `auto` type's undo rate in the latest 30 passes **10%**, it flips back to `confirm`, with the same chip ("Purchases need your confirm again").
- **Undo** on either chip restores the previous mode and holds it for another 30 outcomes.

You can pin any type to **Auto**, **Confirm** or **Never** in **Settings → Changes from the reply** (under *Replies*): setting a mode pins it, and **Unpin** hands it back to learning. Each type shows where its mode comes from: *Pinned by you*, *Learned on <connection> (n kept, n rejected)*, *Default* (with *learning (n kept, n rejected)* once anything is counted) or *From the field's bound*. A pinned type is still counted but never flipped. The order of precedence is: your pinned mode, then what was learned on the turn's connection, then the starting mode, then the field's own bound.

### The GM assistant's changes

**Settings → Changes from the reply** has a second section, **The GM assistant's changes**: what an agent (the GM assistant) may change on its own, by type. No reply proposes these, so the engine's reading of a reply never uses them. Each type has **Auto**, **Confirm** or **Never**, where its mode comes from and **Unpin**, as the reply's types do:

| Type | What it covers | Starting mode |
|---|---|---|
| **Adding people or things** | Someone or something new. The assistant can't add a person once the chat has brought in as many new people as the world allows (its spawn cap, in **Build → World**), counting everyone the chat brought in, gone or not | confirm |
| **Removing people** | Taking a person out of the world | confirm |
| **Destroying things** | Taking anything but a person out of the world | confirm |
| **Money adjustments** | Adjusting someone's money, setting or adding to a money field, or correcting one | confirm |
| **Quest and secret stages** | Moving a quest or a secret to a stage | confirm |
| **Other GM edits (fields, tiers, minds, routines, sections, the clock)** | Every other edit the GM can make | auto |
