| **Workspace notes** | The assistant's files in the chat's workspace: writing, editing, moving, deleting or pinning one | auto |
| **Where things are kept** | Where a thing goes back to when it's put away | auto |
| **Who may use things** | Letting someone use a thing, taking that back, and standing permissions to use someone's things | auto |
| **Things' names, descriptions and sizes** | A thing's name, the other names the story calls it by, its description and its size | auto |
| **Two things merged into one** | Two things that are one, made one (things made in play only, unless it's your own edit) | confirm |
| **Things removed from the world** | Taking a thing out of the story | confirm |

With the assistant set to apply at once, an **Auto** change applies with its **Undo**, a **Confirm** change waits for your **Apply**, and a **Never** change is refused. Your own GM edits are never held back by these. They learn from how you answer as the reply's types do, counted for each agent on each connection apart.

The **workspace** is the chat's own files, kept for the GM: notes that follow the chat's turns as everything else does, so a swipe, a Re-run or a Branch carries them, that can be pinned, and that every agent reads (GM → Workspace); the learned notes, under `learned/`, stay as they are whatever a swipe or an Undo does, and your global notes, under `~/`, are shared by every chat of yours. The assistant can copy a lore entry into a file there (under `lore/`) or draft one under `lore-drafts/`, but it never writes your World Info books or the world's lore: you apply a draft yourself, in **Build**, with Build's own **Save**.

## The Context inspector

**GM → This chat → Context**, under *Inspector*, shows what the engine put into the prompt for each generation. It doesn't need the GM gate, because it shows only what you may know. At the top are any warnings: the world's book wasn't attached to the chat or some of its section entries were missing (both put right for the next turn), your preset has a setting that causes trouble, or a damaged journal was replaced (with where the old file is kept). The **Older generation** and **Newer generation** buttons step through earlier generations. **Activation** reads **Activation confirmed** once Lumiverse reports every section the engine switched on as activated, and **Not confirmed** until then or when one wasn't, with a **Section budget** bar of the tokens used. **Engine sections** lists the sections it filled in (Outcome, Scene, People and so on) with their sizes and whether each was **Delivered**, **Not delivered** or **Unchecked**. A section too big for what was left of the section budget loses whole lines from its end until it fits, and its row says how many ("3 lines left out: over its caps or the section budget"; its own caps count there too). One whose first line alone doesn't fit shows **Clipped** and "Left out: over the section budget", and is never shown as delivered. The Minds section has a budget of its own: when some of its lines don't fit, its row says how many were left out ("2 lines left out: over its own budget"), and when none fit it is **Clipped** with "Left out: over its own budget". Sizes are the token counts of Lumiverse's tokenizer for each section's exact text, counted for your main model. Until Lumiverse has counted a section, or if that takes more than a second, the engine uses an estimate that stays above the real count. The [call meter](#the-call-meter) comes last.

**Lore the helpers read** lists, for each model step of the generation, what it read of the world's lore ([Lore for the helpers](#lore-for-the-helpers)): its searches (the prefetch and each lookup) with how many entries each found, the entries it was given with their tokens (and *cut to fit* when shortened), its lookups and whether they went as tools or the ask form, and why a lookup didn't happen. It shows only what you may know. A secret entry isn't there at all: not listed, and not counted in what a search found. An NPC's choice shows only whose choice it is (*Dev's choice*), never what they might do: it reads *private*, its lookups show as —, and nothing it found is counted or listed. A choice made only by people you haven't met or can't see isn't listed. A step that may read a secret shows its lookups as — with how many entries they found, never what it asked. Any other search that would name a secret, a secret entry, someone you haven't met or a place you don't know is withheld, unless you or the reply said those words that turn; a name counts as it does in a judged approach's reason (see [Lore for the helpers](#lore-for-the-helpers)), so someone counts by any word of their name, however short.

**Minds** lists the generation's minds calls ([Character minds](#character-minds)): whose mind it was, which step (a mind, a memory, a time skip's beats or consolidation), their level, when it ran (before the reply, after it, or in a time skip), whose prompt it used (the world's, yours or the built-in), and how it went, **recorded** or **failed**, with its seconds and tokens. Never a word of what a mind thought or said, nor whether the narrator got it: that is in **GM → Minds**. A call for someone you can't see or haven't met isn't listed, even when they are in the room with you.

With **Time-skip beats** turned on, its call before a time skip is listed there too, as the step alone: it thinks as no one, so it shows no one's name and no level.

### The People section

**People** tells the narrator who is in your scene ([The fidelity ladder](#the-fidelity-ladder) says what your scene takes in): your character first, by name, with any needs pressing on them from Cast life 4 ("Alex Rivera (you): hungry, tired, lonely"), then each person there you can see, marked by their tier when they're in your party ("Danny Okafor (party companion) is here."), what they're doing when you may see it, and how they regard you. The Minds and Stance sections read your scene the same way.

### The Stance section

**Stance** tells the narrator how the people with you stand toward you and what they are about, in a line or two each. It lists only the people you can see in your scene: someone hidden from you gets no line, and no line names them.

- **What they know of you:** whether they know you (a **Knows** relation to you, or regard for you) and how they regard you, in your world's words for regard ("warm", "cold"), never numbers. From Relationships 3 it also names any side of their regard that differs from the whole: "regard: warm (trust: cold)".
- **What they are about:** what they are doing, as the People section says it; for each public agenda they have, its cue, or its current step as **Play → People** shows it; and, for someone who lives their own day, the part of today they chose themselves (an NPC choice), the one under way or the next: "Decided: See friends at 18:00".
- **Cues:** how they behave while a secret of theirs stays hidden, as the world's author wrote it: never the secret, only how it shows ("evasive about the cellar"). A cue comes from the current stage of a track they own, or from their own **Cues** in Build, and it goes once its secret is out.

The section opens with a short note for the narrator: these lines say how people behave, and it never says outright what a cue only hints at. Whose knowledge the narrator has is the narrator's knowledge (see [What the narrator is fed](#what-the-narrator-is-fed)). A cue that would carry a secret you haven't found, or name someone or something you don't know, is left out on its own; the rest of the section stays.

Stance comes after the facts on the section budget (Outcome, Scene, People, Money and the rest), so on a small budget it is left out before them; only the newer Place and Property sections come after it and are left out first. Its cues come last of all: they go in only when the budget still has room once every section has its place, and otherwise Stance goes without them. The inspector's size for Stance, and the budget it shows as used, count Stance's people lines only, because the cues change with secrets you haven't found. To see it more often, raise **Engine budget (tokens)** in **Settings → Model use** (under **Advanced: budgets and the engine budget**), or give it a lower rank in the world's own sections. The engine's helpers (the brief, the outline and suggested choices) don't read it: a cue is a hint for the narrator to play, not a fact to state.

### Your lore in world chats

Your own lore books work in a world chat as they do anywhere else in Lumiverse: their entries activate by Lumiverse's own rules (keywords, constant entries, groups, recursion and the World Info budget), and LumiRPEngine never switches one on or off by writing to your book. It changes an entry only for the prompt at hand, in two cases. An entry with a secret line in its text (see **Secrets in your World Info** under [World lore](#world-lore)) reaches the narrator with each secret line marked as a secret, saying who knows it or whom it is kept from ("*its text* [secret; known to Marcus Delgado]"), or without that line, as the narrator's knowledge says (see [What the narrator is fed](#what-the-narrator-is-fed)); an entry that is only one secret line is left out of that prompt when its line is. An entry with the same text as a secret line (the copy a character's card brought, say, or one Lumiverse builds from a card for the prompt) reads as that secret. And an entry an earlier version of LumiRPEngine kept secret with its own fields, or one a secret entry of the world was indexed from, stays off until its gate holds, then comes on, as before. If the engine can't tell where the chat stands, every secret stays out of that prompt. If it doesn't answer in time, Lumiverse leaves every secret line out by itself, so no secret line reaches the prompt; but an entry kept secret only by something outside its own text (one a secret entry of the world was indexed from, or a copy with a secret line's text and no line of its own) then goes in as stored, so write the secret line into that entry too, or switch the copy off. Nothing waits for your other books, and a book LumiRPEngine can't read switches nothing off. The engine's helpers and minds also read your books as lore ([World Info as lore](#world-lore)). The Context inspector doesn't list your entries.

- **Your books are written only when you press for it:** in **Build → Lore → World Info entries** (a secret line into an entry's text, or the entry's own fields) and in [Secrecy in your World Info](#secrecy-in-your-world-info). No turn, save, commit or Upgrade switches one of your entries off or writes anything into it. An entry an earlier version of LumiRPEngine stored switched off for a secret stays off in its book, in chats that play no world too, until you move its book to secret lines, which lists those entries for you to switch back on.
- The engine switches on only the sections it fills in, in the world's own book, and keeps every other entry there switched off, even one you switch on by hand.
- The sections share the section budget in rank order. The Outcome section is never left out; a later section too big for what is left loses whole lines from its end until it fits.
- World Info recursion follows your own settings, so a section's text can trigger one of your entries, as any other entry's text can.
- If the engine stalls, the world's sections are missing from that turn's prompt (they stay switched off) and the Context inspector has no entry for it; a turn the engine couldn't plan in time is also marked **Not fully simulated**.
- The world's own lore (Build → Lore) is kept in the world. The engine's helpers read it ([Lore for the helpers](#lore-for-the-helpers)), and the narrator gets the lore about where your character is in the **Place** section.
- Your World Info is read as lore live, each entry as its book has it now, and nothing is copied into the world: **World Info as lore** under [World lore](#world-lore) says which books, and [Settings → Lore](#settings--lore) narrows them.

### Settings → Lore

**Settings → Lore** (in the **World Info** group) says which of your World Info books LumiRPEngine reads as lore, and how its agents find an entry in them. Each part has its default beside it, and **Reset** puts it back.

- **Books LumiRPEngine reads:** **Every book a chat reads** (the default: the world's **Lore books**, the chat's own books and your global ones) or **Only the books I tick**, which lists your books to tick. A ticked book is still read only where a chat reads it anyway. A ticked book you have since deleted stays listed as no longer one of your books, for you to untick. World Info folders can't be picked: Lumiverse doesn't tell extensions which folder a book is in.
- **Lore index:** the agents find a World Info entry by a **short id** in the lore index, one line per entry with its id, its title and what it holds. An entry with a keyword that matches the **Tag pattern** (a regular expression, by default `^lr\d{4}$`, so a keyword such as `lr0101`) goes by that keyword, its tag; any other entry gets an id of LumiRPEngine's own, the **Id prefix** (`lre`) and a number padded to **Id digits** (4), such as `lre0007`. An entry keeps its id for good, even as other entries come and go, so an id an agent wrote down still names the same entry. **Holds line (characters)** is how much of an entry's first words stands beside its title (100; 0 for the title alone).
- **Index tokens** (1,500): how much of a role's prompt the index may take, for a role whose context starts with the **Lore index** (**Settings → Agents → Context**), cut there and saying how many entries it left out. Any other agent asks for the index with its `lore_index` tool when it needs it.
- **Your own index entry:** pick the book and the entry of yours that already indexes your lore, and the agents get its text, as written, instead of the index LumiRPEngine makes; an id it names that is a tag reads that entry. **Clear** goes back to LumiRPEngine's index.
- **Index entry kept in sync:** the entry **Build → Lore → Build an index entry** wrote (see **The lore index** under [World lore](#world-lore)), which LumiRPEngine writes again whenever the index changes. **Stop** leaves the entry in its book as it stands, at once, even while a turn is under way; delete it there once you no longer want it. An entry you build while a turn runs is the one kept from then on.

### JSON blocks in replies

Some presets ask the AI to end every reply with a block of JSON between `<json>` and `</json>`, such as a tracker or a ledger. In a world chat LumiRPEngine keeps those blocks out of Lumiverse's World Info scanning and hides them in the chat, and the engine's own helpers read the reply without them. Your preset's own regex scripts still read every reply as the AI wrote it, so a preset that draws its block as a tracker keeps its tracker (LumiRPEngine hides only a block still showing as raw JSON, once every other display script has run), and one that strips old blocks from the prompt still finds them. The block itself stays exactly as the AI wrote it, so anything that reads it, such as the `{{jsonBlock}}` macro, still finds it. Regex scripts in the regex panel's LumiRPEngine folder do this, on new replies, older ones and edited ones: two in each world chat, and two global ones that change nothing but the wrapper LumiRPEngine saves around a block. To see the blocks, turn off **Hide JSON blocks in world chats** in **Settings → Preset** (under *Your preset*), which removes the hiding; World Info still skips the blocks. Turning it on again brings the hiding back, starting with the chat you have open. Deleting a world chat removes its scripts, and deleting a world removes its chats' scripts for good. Uninstalling LumiRPEngine leaves the scripts where they are, and they keep working together, so replies read as before; delete them all from the regex panel if you want them gone, and the saved replies then show their blocks, with World Info still skipping them.

### Your preset

The preset Lumiverse generates a world chat with has a say in it: the selected preset, else the one the chat's connection links, with any blocks a preset profile switches on or off for that chat. If Lumiverse can't say which preset that is, the engine stops before the reply and the chat says why. A block of it holding `{{outlet::lre_scene}}` (or `lre_outcome`, `lre_people` and so on) puts that section there instead of where the world places it; switched-off blocks named **RPEngine · extract**, **RPEngine · brief** and so on give that helper of the engine style and rules for your preset; and in world chats a switched-off **RPEngine · narrator** block adds your rules to the narrator's, after the engine's own, which every reply in a world chat gets: the order of authority first (your instructions, then what the story has established, then the sections, the world as it stands), then, under it, what the marks on facts your character doesn't know mean. Saving another preset changes nothing for the chat; saving this one between the engine's run and the reply makes the engine run again only when the save moves an outlet or changes a guidance block or the narrator rules. **Settings → Preset** (under *Your preset*) shows the **Active preset**, its **Outlets** and its **Guidance blocks** (each marked **Missing**, **Empty**, **Read** or **On**), and **Add RPEngine blocks to this preset**, which adds the missing guidance blocks, switched off and holding notes on what each is for, under an **RPEngine** category. [Presets and RPEngine](PRESETS.md) has every outlet, the guidance each helper takes, and examples.

### Delivery verification

Activation isn't delivery: an entry can be switched on and still not reach the prompt. Opening the Context inspector runs a **test prompt build**, a dry run that sends nothing and saves nothing, and searches it for each section of the latest generation by its heading (such as "[Scene]"): a section it finds is **Delivered**, and one it doesn't is **Not delivered**. Outcome, Montage and Outline come only from a real turn's plan, which a dry run doesn't make, so the test build leaves them **Unchecked**, as it does a section left out over the budget. The check tells you whether a section reached the prompt, not where in the prompt it landed.

Anything the engine inserts into the prompt, a name, a price, a number, a model's output, an Import or Refine proposal, has every brace replaced with a look-alike (｛ and ｝), its control characters removed (Lumiverse's macro engine turns some of them back into braces on a later pass) and its `<user>`, `<char>` and `<bot>` shorthands broken with look-alike angle brackets (＜ and ＞), so it can never form a Lumiverse macro, however many times it is processed. The same goes for the labels and the text of the sections and for the messages the engine writes into your chat. Your own authored lore is not changed: it keeps its macros, as it would in a native World Book (see [Import and export](#import-and-export) for reviewing a world someone else made).

## Authoring

You build worlds in **Build**, which works at phone width as well as on a desktop. Each page opens scrolled to the top, and each has a back button at its top left.

- **Worlds.** Build opens on your worlds. Each row shows the world's name and how many revisions it has, with a **This chat** badge on the world the open chat plays; tap it to open the world. Its **More for** *name* button (⋯) holds **Export** and **Delete**, and Delete asks first ("Delete “*name*” and all its revisions? Chats attached to it stop simulating."). The **Review before playing** card, after an import that needs one, sits at the top (see [Import and export](#import-and-export)).
- **New world** opens a page of its own. Under **Start from**, pick **Blank** or one of the [templates](#templates), each with a line on what it is. The **Name** field takes the template's name until you type your own; then tap **Create world**. **Or bring one in** has **Import a file** (an lre-world-v1 file, as Export writes it).
- **The world bar.** An open world has a bar that stays at the top as you scroll: the back button, the world's name, the state of its draft (**Uncommitted** or **All committed**, with its error and warning counts) and **Commit**. Its **Draft actions** button (⋯) holds **Discard changes**. See [Revisions and Upgrade](#revisions-and-upgrade).
- **The world home** lists the world's parts, **World**, **Calendar**, **Realism defaults**, **Tiers**, **Time defaults**, **Ruleset**, **Lore lookup table**, **Minds** and **Routines and the story**, each with a line on what it holds and a badge for its issues. Then come its collections in seven groups: **People & things** (**People**, the characters; **Organizations**, the companies, households, groups and trusts; **Things**, see [Things in Build](#things-in-build); **Money**, the money records; then Kinds, Classifications, Relations), **Places** (Places, Maps, Routes, Grids), **Daily life** (Routines, Schedules), **Rules** (Actions, Checks, Rules), **Story** (Tracks, Lore), **Engine** (Data tables, Sections, Step templates, Packs) and **Advanced** (**All entities**, every entity in one list), each with a count and an issue badge. Last are its **Tools**: **Problems**, **Import from lore**, **Index this text**, **Build / Refine with AI**, **Describe a map**, **Propose routines from lore**, **Playtest**, **Check world** and, when the chat you have open isn't a world chat yet, **Attach to this chat**. A tool that has run shows how its last run went (**Running**, **Done**, **Failed**, **Cancelled** or **Interrupted**).
- **Collections** list their items by name, with a search box once there are more than eight. **New** asks for the name of the new item and makes its id from the name ("Old Miller Tom" becomes `old_miller_tom`, with `_2` added if that id is taken). You can change the id under **Advanced**, or in the open with **Show ids** on. **Continue** opens the new item's page, and **Create** adds it to the draft. Data tables have no New: new tables come from packs, and you open one to edit this world's copy of it.
- **Changing many at once:** **Entities** and **Places** have **Select**. Tap rows to pick them; the bar under the list has **Select all** (what the search shows), a button for each tier under **Select a tier** (Entities: exactly that tier's entities) and **Clear**. **Change…** opens one change for everyone picked, saved to the draft at once: for entities, **Tier**, **Detail in one area** (a level override, or **From the tier** to remove it) and **Pin lore**; for places, **Default scene type** (**Set it for …** with a type typed or picked, or **Remove their scene type**; a place a map draws keeps the map's) and **Pin lore**. Every control starts on **Choose…**, and its button stays off until you pick a value; each card says how those it reaches stand now. An entity's tier, level and pins go to the people picked, unless you turn on **Also the … picked that aren't people**. A pin is added where it isn't pinned already, from the world's lore or a World Info book. Only what changes is written.
- **An item's page** is headed with its name. **Save to draft** and **Revert** sit in a bar that stays at the bottom of the page, and a part's page has the same bar. Your first keystroke in a field counts as a change, so **Save to draft** lights up at once. Its **Item actions** button (⋯) holds **Delete**, which asks first and names the item ("Delete the entity “Rosie Alvarez”? References to it will show in Problems.").

Ids stay out of sight unless you turn on **Settings → Display → Show ids**: then each item's id shows under its name in the lists, and Problems and the tools show their paths, codes and keys.

For the formulas you write in requirements, checks, derived fields and rules, see [EXPRESSIONS.md](EXPRESSIONS.md); for every kind, field, effect, function and lint code the packs offer, the [reference](REFERENCE.md); and for small worlds to copy from, [RECIPES.md](RECIPES.md).

### Editors and lint

Every part of the world has an editor built from its definition: a page of sections, each field with its label over it, then its help, then any problem with it. A reference to something in the world is picked by name; the line under a choice names its pack, or its id when two share a name. Money is typed as the currency writes it ("$12.50"), a length of time like "1 h 30 min" (a rule's cooldown, a route's time, a scene's tick), and a moment of the world's clock with a date picker (a day number and a time on a calendar of your own). A list of things (effects, a relation's fields, appointments, trips, chores, conditions, bills, a kit's pieces) shows a row for each, which opens as a sheet, with buttons to add, reorder and remove; a list the editor knows no shape for stays JSON. A new entry in a keyed list asks for a name and takes its id from it. An optional field says "· optional". Some parts get visual editors: maps are drawn and grids painted (see [Maps](#maps)), a schedule's day is a bar of slots and a track's stages a graph (see [Schedules and tracks](#schedules-and-tracks)), a routine block's time is a bar across the day (see [Routines in Build](#routines-in-build)), and formulas have an **expression** editor.

What you can define:

- **Kinds**: types of things, with single inheritance and typed fields (number, text, yes/no, choice, reference, list, dice, money, date, duration), each with a default, limits, **bands** (numbers shown as words), **derived** formulas, a lock, a limit on model-proposed changes, and a **visibility**: public, known by particular characters, or secret. A field's **Min** and **Max**, **Enum values**, **Ref kind**, **List of** and **Bands** show only for the types they fit, or when they already hold a value. A kind of yours with the id of one of the packs' kinds adds to it: your fields join the pack's (yours win where both define one), your label is shown, and it builds on what the pack's does unless you set **Extends**. So a Person of your own with only a Grit field still has the pack's Home, Bed and Place. Words you wrote in such a field before, an Employer given as a business's name or a Bed as "the couch", stay as you wrote them: Problems warns that they name nothing in the world, and the world still commits and imports; a reference field your own kind defines must name something. Problems warns when your **Extends** leaves some of the pack's fields behind (a Vehicle of yours that extends Item loses a container's Capacity), at the kind whose **Extends** does it, naming the fields and saying how to keep them. A kind of yours that holds things (something in the world is inside one of them, or an action gives, hands over or lends things to one) but isn't built on a container, a vehicle or a person is told too, since a transfer, a gift or a GM's Put inside into it is refused; and so is a kind of yours whose things the world moves (one of them is inside something, or an action moves, gives or lends one) but that isn't built on Item, since nothing can take it, give it or put it in anything: give it **Extends** Item. A lend counts only in an action that requires the lender to carry the thing (`carries(self, item)`), since a lent vehicle stays where it is parked.
- **Relations**: containment, ownership, use grants ("assigned to", loans, with an end time), worn or equipped, knowledge, membership, regard, routes, kinship and your own, with the kinds allowed at each end, cardinality, symmetry and an **inverse** (the relation the other side holds back). The relation picker lists the packs' relation types beside the world's own.
- **Entities**, **places** (with hierarchy and time zones), **routes** with travel modes, and optional **grids** with terrain. Entities, places, tracks and fields each have a **visibility**. **known_by** shows the thing to whoever knows it: a `knows` relation to it, or matching its **Known by**, a [selector](#selectors) (the members of a group, people of a kind or tier, chosen ids) that the editor shows beside a known_by visibility. A place can also be **discoverable**: hidden from the player until they have been there, are told of it or have it in `known_places`. A visibility the engine doesn't recognize, or a Known by it can't read in full, hides the thing rather than showing it, and lint says so. A point-of-view character with no place of its own starts at the world's start place, and from Space 3 arriving at a place drawn on a grid puts them on its cell. An entity's fields come grouped by realism domain, as its kind defines them; a derived field is shown, not edited, with a line saying what it reads; and a value its kind doesn't define sits under **Advanced: other values**. A relation's fields are typed by its relation type, and its **Until** is a moment of the world's clock.
- **Cues**, on a person: what the narrator sees them do while a secret of theirs stays hidden ("evasive about the cellar"), each standing for one secret: a secret lore entry (until its gate holds), a track (while it keeps something hidden: a truth not yet revealed, or the track itself) or a story flag (until it is set). Write how they act, never the secret; the stage cues of the tracks a person owns work the same way. They reach the narrator in the [Stance section](#the-stance-section).
- **Characters**: a person (any entity whose kind is a person, or extends one) opens on its own **Character page** rather than the generic form. A new one is made in the generic form first, then opens there. Its sections, in order:
  - **The header**: the name, the kind and tier, and **Player character** on yours.
  - **Who they are**: the name, kind, tier, classes and tags, how they start (life), and the fields of who they are (birthday, occupation, region, wealth and the kind's own). Each has its own input, with a plain label and its help under it; a number shows its range; a list of words such as traits is a list of chips.
  - **Where they live**: **Home**, **Sleeps** and **Where the story starts them**, each picked by the place's path, and **Starts inside** for a vehicle they start in (see [Picking a place, a bed or a list of words](#picking-a-place-a-bed-or-a-list-of-words)).
  - **Their day**: **Lives their own day (agendas and plans)**, their routines (each opens with **Edit their routine**) and schedules, each shown by its label or in words ("Weekdays 6:30 AM–12:00 PM: Alder Automotive, …"), and the fields of their day (activity, appointments, trips, chores).
  - **Work and money**: **Works for**: **A place** (a shop or workplace in the world, picked by its path) or **A business** picked by name; then the money fields (how they pay keeps its own editor).
  - **People**: their relations as **Family**, **Feelings**, **Groups** and **Other**, each with remove; **Add someone** picks how they're tied and who, people and groups searched by name. A relation's own details are under All fields.
  - **Stats**: the sheet's numbers and conditions.
  - **Things they have**: what they own and have, in groups: **Outfit**, **Carried**, **Wardrobe** (by holder), **At home**, **Elsewhere**, **Lent out** and **Holding for others** (At home leaves out what Wardrobe lists), each row with **Move…**, **Give…**, **Lend…** and **Remove**; **Give them a thing** opens **Add a thing** already set to them. Their vehicles and property open their pages.
  - **Secrets**: visibility, Known by and cues.
  - **Lore pinned to them**.
  - **All fields**, folded: the whole generic form, for anything the sections don't lay out.
  
  **Save to draft** saves only what you changed, so an edit made in another window to anything else stands; **Revert** drops your edits.
- **Maps**: the inside of a place, drawn on a grid or as linked places, which make that place's places, grid and routes (see [Maps](#maps)).
- **Actions**: a label and a **Description** (a line on what the action does: the helper that reads typed messages is told it, so it knows when your words mean this action), parameters, requirements (each with a **Hard lock** switch, below), an optional dice **check** with outcome bands (the odds shown to players are computed exactly from it), effects per outcome, a duration, and the activity it starts. A ref parameter can say **Only candidates matching** a [selector](#selectors): the Actions sheet offers only those, and typed text or a request naming anyone else is refused.
- **Hard locks.** A requirement is plain unless you switch on **Hard lock** under it. When a player types an action whose plain requirement isn't met, their words still happen in the story: the engine plays none of the action, the reply plays the words as plain roleplay, and a receipt says why. A hard lock refuses the attempt instead (**…: couldn't**), and the reply is told it failed. So mark hard every lock, every puzzle's solution and every guard on a secret: the safe that opens only once its code is found, the door that needs its own key, the papers someone keeps out of reach. **Existing worlds' locks are soft until you mark them:** a world made before hard locks has none, so every lock in it plays as roleplay for anyone who types past it, until you open the action here and switch on **Hard lock** for its requirement. The starter worlds come with theirs marked. Check lists, as a suggestion, each requirement that reads like a lock but isn't hard (`soft_lock`): on an action about a door or a container, one that the actor carries a named thing (a key), or a stage or flag the requirement reads together with one of the action's choices. A hard lock keeps its action in the Actions sheet while it's locked, even a step the story hasn't come to, so a typed attempt finds it; that's why a story step on its own (a stage or a flag the action needs before it's offered at all) is never suggested: it stays hidden until the story gets there, and marking it hard would tell the player it exists.
- **Rules**: "when this happens and that is true, do this", with run-once and cooldowns. A cascade stops at eight steps.
- **Tracks**: quests, secrets (revealed by conditions only), puzzles with attempts and a hint ladder, and NPC agendas.
- **Routines**: how people's days usually go, owner by owner (everyone's, a group's, a kind's, a selector's or someone's own), as blocks at a set time, within a window or optional, each resting on what you wrote, a lore entry or a sheet field (see [Routines in Build](#routines-in-build)).
- **Schedules**: calendar recurrence plus tables of fixed values or weighted choices, for shifts, timetables, opening hours, weather, bills, paydays and price paths. A schedule's owner is an entity, a group (its members), a kind, or a [selector](#selectors). When several set the same field of the same person, the most specific owner wins while its slot runs: their own schedule, then a group's, then their kind's, then a selector's; when the more specific slot ends, the field returns to the less specific one still running.
- **Lore**: what the world knows, as entries with a title, text, keywords, aliases, topics and links, where each came from, and secrets with a gate and the helpers that may read them, plus a lookup table of terms and topics (see [World lore](#world-lore)). It is kept in the world and the engine's helpers read it; the narrator gets the entries about where your character is, in the **Place** section.
- The **calendar** (Gregorian or your own) and **realism** defaults, with the band caps under the band labels.
- **Tiers**: the world's own ranks of importance, on the **Tiers** part. Each has a **Label**, a **Band** (Leads, Supporting or Background), a place in the order (its move up and move down buttons), an optional description, and whether its people live their own day (agendas and plans) by default. Type a name under **New tier's name** and tap **Add tier** to make a new one (its id comes from the name, under **Advanced**); a tier still in use can't be removed. **New people join** picks the tier for people the story introduces (by default the first Background tier). An entity's editor picks its tier from these, and its **Lives their own day** choice can follow the tier ("As its tier"); lint flags an entity whose tier the world doesn't have.
- **Section templates**: each section's **Guidance**, the text the narrator reads ahead of the section's lines, and where the section goes. **Build → Sections** lists every section the world has, and each one's page opens with its guidance. `{{lines}}` marks where the lines go and must stay in it: the field says so, and **Save to draft** waits until it does. A template may use only that marker; any other macro, control character or `<user>`-style shorthand in one is a lint error (`section_macro`), because it would run beside the engine's own values. Each built-in section's guidance tells the narrator what the section holds and that its lines are the authoritative, specific record to write from: the exact items, vehicles, rooms and their contents, people, amounts and conditions, used as given whenever the prose touches them, never a summary or a generic stand-in (and the Outcome's lines are narrated as having happened this turn, the Outline's beats are written in order, and so on). None of them outranks your own instructions or the story, which the narrator's rules put first. A world with no sections of its own gets the built-in ones, each marked **Built-in** in the list: its page shows its guidance, prefilled with the built-in text, with **Reset to default**, and saving keeps only the wording you changed (the world's `sectionTemplates`), so the sections you leave alone keep the built-in text and nothing else about the world changes. Once a world lists sections of its own, it gets only those, plus the built-in Minds unless it lists its own, so list `stance` to keep it; a listed section with a built-in section's id also has **Reset to default**. **Position** is `{"type":"chat_depth","role":"system","depth":1,"direction":"from_end"}` (that many messages from the end of the chat, or from its start), `{"type":"outlet","name":"…"}` (only where a `{{outlet::…}}` macro of that name stands, in your preset or a card, and nowhere else) or `{"type":"native"}` (where the section's entry sits in the world's book, which you can move in Lumiverse's World Info editor; a new one starts before the character). When you change a section's Position and commit, its entry in the world's book moves to match.
- **What a section is fed**: each section in **Build → Sections** also has **In the prompt** (off leaves it out), **Whose knowledge**, **At most lines** (its first lines), **At most tokens** (lines past it are cut from the end) and **Sources** (the renderers that fill it; none, its own). A section with none of these set goes in as it always has. See [What the narrator is fed](#what-the-narrator-is-fed).
- **Step templates**: your own wording for a model step, per purpose (interpret, NPC choices, puzzle answers, judging your approach, brief, outline, extract, suggested choices), with an output (text, decision, or `effects` for the steps that read changes, which run after the reply only) and the signals it runs on (a template's own signals replace the step's). A player's own prompt for the step (**Settings → Steps and connections**) wins over it. The brief, outline, extract and suggested-choices templates, and every `effects` template, can use engine placeholders: `{{world}}`, `{{pov}}`, `{{place}}`, `{{clock}}`, `{{scene}}`, `{{present}}`, `{{facts}}`, `{{outcome}}`, `{{beats}}`, `{{brief}}`, `{{user}}`, `{{reply}}`, `{{recent}}`, `{{gear}}` (what your character carries and wears), `{{money}}` (your character's money), `{{property}}` (what your character owns or may use), `{{positions}}` (who is here with your character, and what they're doing) and `{{knowledge}}` (what your character knows of the quests); each of the last five is what the section that tells it has now, empty in a world without that section. Two more go only to some steps: `{{usual}}`, the usual routines of the people with you, in extract, suggested-choices and `effects` templates, and `{{sections}}`, the sections the narrator has this turn, in an outline template. An interpret template fills in only `{{world}}` and `{{pov}}`, and none at all on a Decision connection; NPC-choice, puzzle-answer and judge templates are sent as you wrote them, with nothing filled in. The template's page lists the placeholders its purpose is given under **Placeholders**. `{{brief}}` is this turn's brief (an outline template that uses it waits for the brief; it's empty in the brief step itself), and `{{recent}}` is the Journal's last five lines before this turn, where each ✎ fix or GM edit you can see takes a line of its own. They are all player-visible: a line that would reveal an unrevealed truth is dropped before the call. A template whose output can't be used by its purpose, an unknown signal and a placeholder its step isn't given are lint warnings, and so is a blank template (it replaces the built-in instructions with none). Changes a step template proposes with `effects` are checked and bounded exactly like extraction, and money in them is stated as a plain amount in the world's currency (8 for $8), which the engine converts. Which steps run at all is still your choice, in **Settings → Steps and connections**. A world made with an earlier version keeps its step templates, except those for lore selection and for the contradiction check, which no longer exist; a secret entry's readers and the lore pinned to steps drop the contradiction check too.
- **Step templates for minds**: the four minds steps (Character minds, Character memory, Time-skip beats, Memory consolidation) take templates too, with output **text**. They can use `{{character}}`, `{{world}}`, `{{pov}}`, `{{place}}` and `{{clock}}`, and the engine always adds the answer format after them. Time-skip beats think for no one, so there `{{character}}` is empty, `{{place}}` is where your character is and `{{clock}}` is when the skip starts. A minds template runs on the minds' own cadence, so its signals are ignored, and only the first template of a step (by id) with any text is used, a blank one being skipped; lint says so for each. **Build → World → Minds** lists them and starts new ones from the built-in prompts (see [Character minds in a world](#character-minds-in-a-world)).

Formulas use a small, safe expression language: numbers, comparisons and a fixed set of functions. They can't run code and they always produce a finite number. `table(id, key, column)` reads a cell of one of the world's data tables, and `table_step(id, value, column)` reads the row whose number key is the largest at or below `value` (a level table keyed by XP, say). `roll(notation)` works only in an effect's arguments (a value that starts with `=`): the effect's event records the result, so a replay or a swipe sees the same number. Anywhere else a roll would change the odds you were shown and what the engine reads, so it is a lint error and reads as nothing. For classifications and groups: `kind_is(e, 'person')` (a kind or one inheriting from it), `in_class(e, 'role', 'antagonist')` (a value or one nested under it), `has_tag(e, 'smuggler')`, `member_of(e, harlows)` (directly or through a member group), `tier_of(e)` and `band_of(e)` (the band its tier belongs to).

In the **expression editor**, the **Operators and functions** button (ƒx) opens the operators in groups (**Arithmetic**, **Compare**, **Logic**) and every function a formula can call there: the math built-ins, the engine's own and the formulas of the packs the engine runs, taken from the engine itself, so the list never names one that doesn't exist. It also lists the **Names** the formula can read, by what the world calls them (their ids show with **Show ids** on), with find boxes for long lists. On a desktop these open under the formula; on a phone, where they wouldn't fit, ƒx opens the formula on a page of its own, and the **Open the formula editor** button opens that page at any width (**Done** closes it). As you type, chips suggest the ids, fields and functions that start with what you typed, and the names every formula can read (`self`, `pov`, `here`, `clock`, `hour`, `daypart` and the rest of the clock); tap one to put it in. Under every formula a line says in plain words what it reads and uses ("Reads Sam's cash and the hour; uses min."). An action's **Effects per outcome** are a list of effects for each outcome, with the check's outcomes suggested. An effect's **arguments** are edited one by one: each is a **Value** (`self`, `might`, `2`, or JSON for a list or an object) or a **Formula**, worked out when the effect runs; **Add argument** adds one by name, and its remove button takes it away. Only an argument's formula offers `roll(`.

#### Picking a place, a bed or a list of words

A character's places (their home, where the story starts them, where they are) show as paths, from the widest place in to the place itself: "Riverton › Elm Street › Elm Street Apartments › Floor 1 › **Apartment 2B**", with **Change…** beside it. **Change…** opens the place picker, headed with what you're picking and where it is now:

- **Search by name or where it is.** Each word you type matches the start of a word in the place's name or in the places around it, so "elm 2b" finds the Apartment 2B on Elm Street, and "kitchen elm apartments 2b" picks that flat's Kitchen out of every Kitchen in the world. Each result shows its path; the places whose own name has every word come first, then the shallower ones. **Enter** picks the first. A search shows at most 60, and says so: type another word to narrow it.
- **Suggestions**, before you type: the places the page thinks likely, such as where they are, their home or where your character is.
- **Browse** opens among the current place's neighbours, or at the top when there's none. **All places** and the places above it form a trail you tap to go back up. Each row says how many places it holds; **Choose** picks it, and **›** opens it.
- **Secret** marks a place the player doesn't see, and **Hidden until found** one they find by going there or being told of it.
- Where the place may be left empty, a button at the bottom clears it (**No home**, say).

**Sleeps** shows where they sleep, with its own **Change…**. Its picker offers what's likely first: **Sleeps at home** (no bed of their own; they sleep in their home, the default), then **Rooms in their home** and **Things in their home** (the beds, bunks, sofas and hammocks first), then **Somewhere else**: **Another place…** opens the place picker, and **A thing somewhere else…** finds a bed, a sofa or a hammock anywhere by its name or by where it is, each with its place's path. A part built into a vehicle (a pickup's truck bed, a glovebox) is never offered: it holds cargo, not a sleeper.

**A list of words** (traits, likes, things they'd say) shows as chips. Type one and press **Enter** or tap **Add**; **✕** on a chip removes it. Spaces are tidied, and a word already there, in any case, isn't added twice.

### Schedules and tracks

**Schedules.** A schedule's page starts with **Who and what**: **It applies to** either **Someone, a group or a kind**, picked by name under **Owner** (or **No one: a world process (weather, prices)**), or **A selector**; and **It sets**, the field or relation each slot's value sets (place, activity, weather, price…). **Repeats** is **Daily**, **Weekly** (tap the weekdays), **Monthly** (tap the days of the month) or **Yearly**, with **Not on** chips for the calendar's holidays and **From** and **Until** dates. **The day** draws the slots on a bar across the 24 hours. Tap a slot, on the bar or in the list under it, to open its sheet; drag a handle on the bar to move where one slot ends or the next starts (arrow keys move a focused handle by 15 minutes). The **Slot** button adds one. A slot's sheet has **Starts** and **Ends** (an end at or before the start runs past midnight), **Its value**, either **Fixed** or a **Weighted choice** (each **Choice** with its **Weight** and its share), **Given the slot before (Advanced)**, and **Remove slot**. A slot with bad times, no value or weights that add up to nothing, and slots that overlap, are marked **Fix**, and **Save to draft** says how many there are ("Save to draft · 2 to fix").

**Tracks.** A track's page starts with **The track**: **Label**, **Kind** (**Quest**, **Secret**, **Puzzle** or **Agenda**), **Who knows of it** (**Everyone**, **Those who know**, with a **Known by** selector, or **Secret**), **Whose it is**, picked by name (or **GM**: the world's own story, no one's in it, so no one starts out knowing it, knowing someone never reveals it, and its cue is something anyone may notice; GM mode shows it as the GM's), and **Checks roll while it's in progress**. **Stages** shows the stages as a **List** or a **Graph** (**Show the stages as**); a phone starts on the list and a desktop on the graph, which runs from the start at the top down. The start is marked **Start**, a stage nothing leads to says **Can't be reached** (a banner counts them), and on the graph a dashed red way is how a stage fails. Tap a stage to open its sheet:

- its fields: the label, the cue the player sees, the secret truth, the **Objectives**, a gate, hints, the effects on entering, rewards and a puzzle's answer. Each objective has **What the player reads**, the line they see in their quests, and **Met when**, its condition; one with no words shows the player its condition, so give it some. **Add objective** adds one, and **Add an item check** sits with them (see Items in Build);
  - **Rewards** are granted as the track leaves the stage onward (not when it fails); an end stage, which is never left, grants its rewards as the track reaches it. **Effects on entering** apply on reaching the stage either way.
  - **Hints**: on a puzzle stage with an answer, the player earns one for each wrong answer given at that stage; on any other stage, every hint is there as soon as the track reaches it. Either way the player opens them one at a time on the quest sheet, and the narrator is told only the opened ones.
  - A **cue** is what the player can notice at the stage, told in their quests. On a track a person other than the player character owns (**Whose it is**), while it still keeps something from the player character (a truth not yet revealed, or the track itself), the cue is how that person behaves instead: Stance tells it under their name while they're in the scene, and the quests never show it.
- **Make it the start** (the start says **Where the track starts** instead);
- **Next stages**: each **Goes to** a stage picked by name, **When** its condition holds, and the first that holds wins. The move buttons reorder them, **Open** *stage* opens the one it goes to, and **+ Next stage…** adds one;
- **Failing**: **It can fail**, then **It fails when** and **Then goes to**;
- **Delete stage**, which waits until another stage is the start.

The **Stage** button asks for the new stage's **Name** and what it **Comes after** (it is added there as a next stage, with "true" as its condition until you change it), then **Add the stage**.

On both pages the **Item actions** button (⋯) holds **Delete**, **JSON (Advanced)** shows the whole schedule or track as the world file keeps it, and **Save to draft** and **Revert** sit in the bar at the bottom. A stage's page has the track's own **Save track to draft** and **Revert track**: a stage is saved with its track.

**Items in Build.** A place's page lists its **Things here**, the furniture and what lies loose there, with **Add a thing here**; each row opens its thing, marked **Hidden until searched** when it is. A thing's page has **Hidden until searched**: on, it stays hidden until your character searches where it lies or opens what holds it (it must lie somewhere first), and **Visibility** reads the same; **Save to draft** keeps it. A thing's page leaves out what only a person has (life, cues, the Lumiverse character, their own day). A stage's **Add an item check**: pick **Has it with them**, **Has one like it** or **It lies at**, the thing (and the place), and **Add objective** writes the objective with the world's own ids and a plain line for the player ("Have the spare relay with you"), which **What the player reads** may change.

### Things in Build

**Build → Things** (under **People & things**) is the world's things, read as a chat would start, with **Search things** (by the words the story would use: names, the other names a thing goes by and its type). **By place** is a tree of places, what lies there and what holds what (Port Alder › 114 Harbor Street › Kitchen › Refrigerator), each row with its size, whose it is and its badges (**Hidden until searched**, a problem); **By owner** lists each owner with their things **On them**, **At home** and **Elsewhere**, and **Nobody's**. **+ Add a thing** asks four things:

1. **Name.**
2. **What is it?** Search the catalogs: a pick fills in its size and details. Or say how big it is, each size with an example: **Pocket**, **Hand**, **Armful**, **Heavy**, **Furniture** or **Built-in**.
3. **Where is it?** One picker: places by their path, people, and the things that hold things, each with whose it is and where ("Toolbox (Jordan's, at Alder Automotive)"); one that can't take it says why.
4. **Whose?** A person, a household, a business or a group, or **Nobody**, with a line saying what that means ("Presumed the household's at 114 Harbor Street").

**Kept at** follows where it is unless you change it, and **Create** opens the thing. A thing's page has **Where it is** and **Kept at** (the place, holder or **On its owner** it goes back to), each with **Change…**; **Whose**; **Who may use it**, a chip for each ground someone may use it on ("The Kowalczyks · members", "Alder Automotive staff · at Alder Automotive", "Alder Automotive customers · while it trades"), each grant with when it ends and ✕, and **Let someone use it**; **What it is**: its type, size, the other names the story may call it (**Also called**), its description, quantity (from Gear 2), condition and slot (from Gear 3), **Sleep in or on** and **Consumable**; **Holds**, its contents with **+ Add inside** and the largest size it **Takes things up to**; **Secrecy**; and **Advanced**, every field, its relations and its id. It never shows a tier, a life or its own day.

**Problems** flags a thing too big to carry that the world puts on a person ("Refrigerator is on Jordan Ellis: too big to carry. Put it at 114 Harbor Street?") and one both lying somewhere and inside something, each with **Fix**; an authored thing no size answers for is a note only.

**The world's things settings** are the gear pack's (**Things settings** on Build → Things opens **Build → Packs → gear**): **Everyday carry** (who carries what from the start: by default people carry a wallet, keys and a phone; each rule a selector and the types it gives, and an empty list carries nothing), **Sizes by name** (each name's size, its other names, what it holds, and *Clothing at home rests in it*; the shipped names are marked, each change has its own reset, and there are **Use the shipped names** and a bring-back for names taken out: a fridge, a washer or a sofa is Furniture and a TV Heavy, while a mini fridge, a microwave or a hair dryer keeps its own size), the Gear section's **Not with you** lines (3), **Put away** (**Put things away**, on; **After (hours out of sight)**, 24; **From Gear level**, 3) and **Tidy up** (**Tidy impossible holds every turn**, on). The rest is the pack's config, below its fields: `sizes` (`ceiling`, the largest thing a person carries, Armful; `byCategory`, a catalog category's size; `byVolume`, the upper bounds in litres of Pocket, Hand, Armful and Heavy, 0.5, 5, 40 and 400; `byName`, the sizes by name), `access` (the implied rules: `household`, `housemates`, `partners`, `staff` and `customers` on, `family` off) and `fetchMinutes` (how far on foot a fetch reaches, 3). Whatever the config leaves out reads as its default. **Build → World** sets how many places the story may make, beside the people replies may introduce: **Places one reply may make** (2) and **Places a chat may make in play, in total** (64), each from 0 (none) to 500.

### Organizations in Build

**Build → Organizations** (under **People & things**) lists the world's organizations grouped **Companies**, **Households**, **Groups** and **Trusts** (and a party, where the world authors one), each with how many belong to it and a badge for its issues, with a search box past eight. **+ New** asks what it is (a company, a household, a group of one of the world's group kinds, or a trust) and its name, and makes its id from the name.

An organization's page has, from the top:

- **Basics**: its name, kind, structure, its places and hours, and who knows of it.
- **People**: its members by **Member of**, each with a title, a position, a rank and since when, and **Add a member**; its **Staff**, the jobs it employs, with **Add a job** (worker, title, position, how they're paid and the rate); its owners, each with a role, a share, units and a class; and **Acting for it**, the delegations to it, with **Let someone act for it** (who, the powers, the limits and who granted it).
- **Positions**: each one, with **Add position**: its title, its powers as chips by family, seats (0 is any number), whom it reports to, how far its staff and member powers reach, its limits, who fills it when it falls vacant, and whether new members join in it.
- **Money**: its accounts as Money lists them, its pay schedule and payroll account, its dues, its payouts and its invoice terms.
- **Ownership**, for a company: its share classes and the votes a decision needs.
- **Decisions**: how its motions count their votes (shares or members), their quorum, pass mark and days, its standing votes, and how many days a request for approval waits.
- **Property and entry**: its properties, and its places with who may enter them.
- **All fields**.

The organization's own fields save with **Save to draft**, as a patch of only what changed; a member's link, a job and a delegation are someone else's, each saved to the draft from its own sheet at once.

**Problems** tells an organization's mistakes in plain words, most with **Fix**, a draft patch of only what it changes: a position or a delegation naming a power there isn't (Fix takes it out); a position reporting to one the organization doesn't have, or up the line to itself (Fix has it report to no one); more holders than seats (Fix makes a seat for each); a member or a job holding a position the organization doesn't define (Fix takes it off); a delegation giving more than its granter may (Fix cuts it to what they hold, or ends it); owners counting units whose shares don't match them (Fix sets each share from its units); a company that passes its profit to its owners held in part by a household, a group, a trust or the party, so that no one pays income tax on that share (no Fix: who should be taxed is yours to say); a group of the `company` kind with owners or staff, which a business would run (Fix makes it one); a place's entry that isn't one of the five, or keys that aren't a list of words; and, as a note, a household member titled "head" with no Head position (Fix writes one out, holding every power, held by them).

### Routines in Build

A **routine** is how someone's days usually go: when they open the shop, eat with the family or go to the gym. It is something the world can rely on, never a rule, and it never outranks the story. What it is to the engine is the role **Routines and the story** gives each level (below): by default it only informs Live, Near and Far characters, as context their mind and the narrator may weigh while the story and planning decide what they do, and Background characters keep to theirs. Whatever the role, a routine never moves anyone out of a scene with your character, contradicts what's been told or interrupts play, and a chat changes anyone's routine as it goes, through the story or the GM.

**Where a character's day comes from**, most binding first: what the story has established (where they are, what they're doing, a trip, a plan under way); their commitments (schedules such as shifts and school, appointments, trips); the plans the story or the GM gave them; their routine; and their basic needs, sleep and meals. A character with no routine has the rest of that list, and nothing more: no outings, visits or invitations are made up for them.

**Build → Daily life → Routines** lists the routines by whom they're for, the most specific first: **Someone's own**, **A group's** (a family, a household, a crew), **A kind's**, **By selector** and **Everyone's** (the world's default). Where routines set the same block for someone, the most specific wins: their own, then a group's, a kind's, a selector's, then everyone's. **Routines and the story**, at the top, opens the settings. **New** names a new routine, as in any collection; a routine without a name goes by whom it's for.

A routine's page has:

- **Whose routine**: **It's for** picks the level (**Someone**, **Group**, **Kind**, **Selector** or **Everyone**), then who, and an optional **Name**.
- **Their days**, for anyone but everyone: **Have a routine** or **No routine**. With no routine, their days come only from the story, the GM, their commitments and their basic needs, and no broader routine applies to them. With a routine, **Keeps the blocks of broader routines** says whether a group's, a kind's and everyone's blocks still apply where this one doesn't set the same block.
- **Blocks**, in the order of the day, each with its kind, its time, its place and where it comes from. The **Block** button adds one, and **Change a basic need or a broader block…** copies sleep, a meal or another routine's block in, to change it for whom this routine covers. Tap a block to open its sheet.
- **Turned off here**: blocks that don't apply to whom this routine covers, such as breakfast for a night owl. **Turn off a block…** adds one, and its undo button puts it back.
- **Where this routine comes from**: what its blocks rest on unless a block names its own.

A block's sheet has:

- **What it reads as** ("Opens the shop") and **What they're doing**: sleep, a meal, work, socialize, leisure or the world's own activity.
- **When it happens**: **At a set time** (the same time on each day it's on), **Within a window** (somewhere in its window, wherever the day has room; **How often** can make it **A few times a week**, with **Times a week**) or **Optional** (only likely, with **How likely**: **Rarely**, **Sometimes**, **Often** or **Usually**, and the first thing a busier day leaves out). The bar across the day shows when it runs, or for a window when it may start: drag either end, or type the times. **How long** takes a length such as "1 h 30 min".
- **How much it matters**, **Least** to **Most**: when blocks want the same free time, the one that matters more goes first.
- **Where**: **At home**, **At work**, **Wherever they are**, **At the home of whoever they're with**, **A chosen place**, or **The nearest place of a type**.
- **Who with**: a [selector](#selectors) that names people or groups, and **Asks them along**, which makes the block an invitation, made only as **Routines and the story** allows.
- **Which days**: weekdays, days off, holidays, birthdays (their own or a friend's), seasons and weather; with none set, every day.
- **Needs and privacy**: the needs it meets, and **Private**, which shows it to the player only as "Busy".
- **Where it comes from**: **Written here**, **From lore** (an entry picked by its title, with **The words it rests on**; the entry's row opens it) or **From a sheet** (whose sheet, and which field). The list shows each block's source too.
- **Its id (Advanced)**: a block with the id of a basic need (`sleep`, `breakfast`, `lunch`, `dinner`) or of a broader routine's block replaces it. A block added on the page takes its id from what it reads as.
- **Remove block**.

**Save to draft** (**Create routine** for a new one) and **Revert** sit in the bar at the bottom, and the **Item actions** button (⋯) holds **Delete**. A routine's problems show on its page, and **Problems** opens a block's problem on that block's sheet.

**Routines and the story** (in **World**, and at the top of Routines) tunes what routines are to the engine, and how aware they are of the story and its lore:

- **What a routine is, by closeness**: for **Live** (in the scene with you), **Near** (close to the story), **Far** (off screen, still followed) and **Background** (everyone else), **Informative** (the story and planning decide what they do; their routine is only context their mind and the narrator may weigh; the default for all but Background), **Enforced** (their routine and schedules run their day when the story doesn't, and never pull them out of a scene with you; Background's default) or **Off** (not used at all, not even as context). The GM can set it apart for one character in a chat. The world's schedules (weather, prices, bills, paydays, opening hours) and pay run whatever you pick.
- **In a time skip**: for each level, how those whose routine is informative spend a time skip: **Stay put**, **Follow their routine** (Background's default) or **Planned by the model** (the default for Live, Near and Far; the model plans everyone it covers at the skip's start). A level whose routine is enforced or off says it applies only to characters the GM makes informative. The GM can set it apart for one character in a chat ([Routines and schedules](#routines-and-schedules)). **Plan a skip** picks how the model is asked: **All in one call** (the default, and cheaper) or **One call per person, at once**, which keeps each answer small for a large cast or a long skip; if one person's call fails, the others are still planned and the GM's note says how many weren't.
- **The narrator**: **Tell the narrator people's usual routines** (on by default), for the characters in the scene whose routine isn't off, as context it may use, never what they must do ([Routines and schedules](#routines-and-schedules)).
- **Timelapse from the Actions sheet**: **Follow my routine by default** (off by default), where the Timelapse sheet's **Follow my routine** starts ([Timelapse](#timelapse)).
- **Story and lore awareness**, for those whose routine is enforced: **Story first** (enforced routines bend for the story's plans and the GM's, and what the story sets holds until it's released), **Balanced**, the default (Background characters keep their enforced routine, others bend it for the story's plans, and what the story sets holds for the rest of that day), or **Routine first** (enforced routines hold, bending only as **How plans bend routine** allows, and what the story sets holds until their next block). The GM can set it apart for one character in a chat.
- **Invitations**: whom routine blocks may invite: **Off**, **To the player only** (the default) or **Between everyone**. A block invites only when it names its company and asks them along, so nobody is invited out of nowhere; plans from the story and the GM's invitations need no setting.
- **Sociability**: **Reserved**, **Usual** or **Outgoing**, how fast the social and fun needs grow.
- **How plans bend routine**: which plans made in the story may move work or school: **Only plans that must happen**, **Strong plans**, **Strong plans, and set-time plans for the cast** (the default) or **Strong plans, and set-time plans for anyone**.

**Save to draft** saves them all, and **Use the defaults** takes the world's own settings out again: a world never tuned keeps none.

### Maps

A **map** draws the inside of one place, its `scope`: a manor's floor as rooms on a grid, or a town as places linked by routes. It is a small JSON document in the **lre-map-v1** format (its JSON Schema is `src/shared/lre-map-v1.schema.json` in the source), which you draw in the map editor, write as JSON there, or describe in your own words for the AI to write (see [Describe a map](#describe-a-map)). A map makes its places (inside its scope), its grid and its routes for you, and keeps them in step with it: they show in the Places, Grids and Routes lists as the map's, read-only, with **Edit in map** to change them where they come from. Delete the map and they go with it. A place, grid or route you made yourself always wins: a map that would make one with the same id leaves yours alone, and the lint says so.

A **grid map** is rows of characters, one per square, all the same length. The `legend` says what a character means: a terrain (`{"terrain": "grass"}`, with `"blocked": true` for one you can't cross), a place's area (`{"place": "kitchen"}`), a door on a room's edge (`{"door": true, "place": "kitchen"}`: a door that belongs to the kitchen, so whoever stands in it is in the kitchen, and that joins the kitchen to the room on its other side; it should touch the kitchen, or the map gets a warning), a zone (`{"zone": "cellar"}`) or a thing lying on that square (`{"item": "lantern"}`, which must be a thing of the world on one square only, and not inside something; add `"place"` to say which room's floor it is, otherwise the room around it). Without a legend entry, `#` is a wall, `.` floor, `+` a door and a space nothing. A place stands at its `at` square, or in the middle of its area (its floor, not its doors). `cellMeters` says how many metres a square is across (80 when you leave it out), for walking times. In the map editor, the **Room door** brush paints a door of the room you pick.

```json
{
  "id": "manor_ground",
  "scope": "manor",
  "title": "Ground floor",
  "layout": [
    "#############",
    "#ppppp#kkkkk#",
    "#ppppp+kkkkk#",
    "#ppppp#kkkkk#",
    "###+#####+###",
    "#hhhhhhhhhhh#",
    "#####+#######"
  ],
  "legend": {
    "p": { "place": "parlour" },
    "k": { "place": "kitchen" },
    "h": { "place": "hallway" }
  },
  "places": [
    { "id": "parlour", "name": "Parlour" },
    { "id": "kitchen", "name": "Kitchen" },
    { "id": "hallway", "name": "Hallway" }
  ]
}
```

A **node map** sets its places in a frame of `size` by their `at` (leave it out and the place goes on a ring), and its `links` are the routes between them, each with its `minutes` and, if you like, its `mode` (`walk` unless given, or `road`, `offroad` or `water`). Link every place on the map or none, because once a place's inside has links, travel there follows only them.

```json
{
  "id": "old_town",
  "scope": "town",
  "title": "Old town",
  "size": [100, 100],
  "places": [
    { "id": "square", "name": "Market square", "at": [50, 50] },
    { "id": "inn", "name": "The Gull Inn", "at": [25, 30] },
    { "id": "docks", "name": "Docks", "at": [80, 75] }
  ],
  "links": [
    { "from": "square", "to": "inn", "minutes": 3 },
    { "from": "square", "to": "docks", "minutes": 6 }
  ]
}
```

Without the grid system, walls and doors are drawn, not walked: travel inside a place follows its routes, or, with none, a short walk between any two of its places.

The world's own map settings are in its **World** part in Build. **Display** is how a new chat's map starts out, **Known** or **Fog** (players can switch it on their map). **Grid movement** turns on the **grid system**: Space and travel is at least level 3 in every chat of the world, and on a grid, people stand on squares. **Travel simulation: Go needs a route** is off by default, so **Go to** goes anywhere, walking where it can, else at the time a road or a boat would take, else in 15 minutes. On, **Go to** needs a walking route. Either way a move the reply tells always lands, since the story stands as told; grid steps keep their walls, and nobody's routine changes. **✎** on your place moves you there as a real move: you leave what you were doing, and on a map you stand where you arrive.

- Arriving at a place puts you on its square, or else on a door at the edge of the map, and you walk from there with **Walk here** on the map or the **Move on the map** action, around walls and through doors.
- A floor plan without `links` gets walking routes through its doors, between rooms a walk joins without crossing a third room, so **Go to** the kitchen walks there in the time the walk takes. A room no door leads to gets a warning.
- **Sight radius** (3 squares unless you change it) is how far you see; walls block it, and Fog shows only the squares you've seen.
- A thing drawn in the legend starts on its square. To take it, wear it or use it there, you have to be on its square or next to it: rules and actions can ask the same with `reach(self, thing)`, and `on_grid(thing)` says whether it lies on a square.
- A rule can fire when someone steps into a zone or onto a square: `{"zone": "cellar", "in": "manor"}` or `{"cell": [4, 2], "in": "manor"}` (every room's area is also a zone named after the room). It fires for you unless `who` picks others (a selector, like `{"kind": ["guard"]}`), once each time they step in, never while they stay.

**Travel times**, also under the map settings, tune how long getting about takes in the world's chats. Left empty, every route takes the minutes written on it. A **speed** by way of travel (on foot, by road, off road, on water, in km/h) times every route of that way by its distance instead, and a **speed by vehicle class** (the vehicles table's class, such as `atv_utility`) does the same for that class's vehicles, whichever way they go. **Getting a vehicle going** and **Parking it** (a minute each by default) add to every trip, and **Longest walk to fetch a vehicle** (30 minutes by default) is how far you'll walk to one parked elsewhere. New moves and drives take the new times; what's already happened in a chat stays as it was.

The **map editor** (open a map from **Maps**, or **New**) has four tabs.

- **Draw.** A new, empty map asks **What kind of map?**: **Grid map** or **Node map**, each starting from a small example you can change. On a grid map the tools are **Paint** (drag to paint with the brush), **Fill** (tap a cell to fill the area around it) and **Move** (drag a place to its cell). Pick the brush from the chips: wall, floor, door and nothing, then the legend's own. **Room** and **Brush** add a room or a legend entry, and **Edit brush** opens the one you're painting with. On a node map the tools are **Move** (drag a place; tap one to select it), **Link** (drag from one place to another to join them with a way) and **Add** (tap where the new place goes). Two fingers pan and zoom. **Undo** takes back the last edit.
- **Rooms** (**Places** on a node map) lists the map's places, then its **Legend** on a grid map or its **Ways** on a node map, and the **Map** with its **Edit** button. Each opens a sheet: **Map settings** (**Title**, **Draws the inside of**, the size in cells and **Cell size (metres)**, or **A fixed frame** for a node map), a place (its name, its cell or position, its room and the brush that paints it, **Its inside is drawn by**, and its id under **Advanced**), a brush (what **It paints**: wall, floor, door, terrain, a room, a zone or a thing lying there) or a way (**From**, **To**, **Minutes**, **By** and **Both ways**).
- **Preview** draws the map as Play will show it, every place shown, with each problem marked on it: a solid mark for an error, a dashed one for a warning.
- **JSON** is the map as lre-map-v1 JSON, with **Grid template**, **Node template** and **Tidy**, which lays the JSON out a field per line, and line numbers marked where the problems are. Every edit in the other tabs rewrites the JSON, laid out as Tidy lays it out.

The editor checks the map as you edit, against the format and against the rest of the world. Above the tabs it says "A valid lre-map-v1 map." or counts the errors and warnings, which open into the list: rows of different lengths, a character the legend doesn't know, a place outside the grid, a link to a place that doesn't exist, an id another place already has. Tap a problem to open the place, way or brush it is about, or its line in the JSON tab. The **Item actions** button (⋯) holds **Start from a grid template**, **Start from a node template** and **Delete**; a template asks before it replaces a map you have drawn. **Save to draft** (**Create map** for a new one) and **Revert** sit in the bar at the bottom.

A **grid** that no map draws has a page of its own: **Size** (**Width (cells)**, **Height (cells)** and **Cell size (metres)**) and **Paint**, with the tools **Pan**, **Terrain**, **Blocked** and **Zone**, the terrain and zone chips to paint with, **Add zone** for a new zone, and **Undo**. Its **Item actions** button holds **Delete**, and **JSON (Advanced)** shows the grid. A place that sits on its parent's grid has an **On** *parent*'s **grid** card on its page, above the bar at the bottom: tap a cell to put it there.

### Rulesets and profiles

**Build → Ruleset** is a list of the ruleset's parts: **Start from a profile…**, **How checks are decided**, **Level floors**, **Progression**, **Sheet Designer**, **Combat** and **JSON (Advanced)**. Each row says how that part stands and counts its issues, and each opens its own sheet, with **Save to draft** and **Revert** at the bottom.

A profile is a ready-made ruleset to start from; once applied, everything it added is yours to edit. Tap **Start from a profile…** and pick one:

- **Modern**: present-day adventure on a d20. Six abilities (10 is average) with modifiers of (score − 10) ÷ 2 rounded down; **Defense** of 10 + Dexterity + a level bonus + an equipment bonus; twelve skills with ranks (Athletics, Drive, Stealth, Computers, Investigate, Repair, Notice, Read People, First Aid, Persuade, Deceive, Intimidate); Fortitude, Reflex and Will; **Action Points**, which **Push a skill** spends to add 1d6 to the roll, and which **Rest for the night** and each new level refill (the sheet's row says so); HP from Constitution; Reputation; and levels at 0, 1,000, 3,000, 6,000 XP and so on. A world that applied Modern before Rest for the night refilled them takes it by applying Modern again and choosing **Take the profile's** for Rest for the night and the sheet.
- **Classic**: classic fantasy on a d20. Base attack on a good, average or poor progression and saves on good or poor ones (each character's progressions are fields), thirteen skills with ranks, Armor Class, HP from a hit die, and the same XP scale.
- **Fifth**: heroic fantasy on a d20. A proficiency bonus by level (+2 to +6), skills at proficiency 0, 1 or 2 (2 is expertise, twice the bonus), two proficient saves, Armor Class, **Dodge** (attacks on you roll with disadvantage until your next turn), and hit dice: a **Short rest** spends one to heal, a **Long rest** heals fully and regains half. A well-told approach earns advantage rather than a bonus.
- **Custom**: the least to grow from: one stat (Skill), one check (**Try something**), an HP pool whose max is a formula, a plain attack, and a progression with level 1 only.

Every d20 profile has **Use a skill** and **Make a save**, with one option per skill or save in the Actions sheet and its exact odds; the difficulty is moderate (10) unless the action is given easy, hard, very hard or a number. In an attack a natural 1 always misses and a natural 20 is a critical (the damage dice roll twice). **Total defense** (Dodge in Fifth) lasts until your next turn, and **Rest for the night** heals (in Modern it also refills Action Points).
